lvl1[1]Algebra (Core Branch){note:"The branch of mathematics that deals with symbols and the rules for manipulating those symbols. Algebra is a unifying thread of almost all of mathematics. It provides the language and structure for expressing patterns, relationships, and generalizations. From solving simple equations to exploring abstract structures like groups and rings, algebra is fundamental to advanced mathematics, science, engineering, and computer science.

Key principles:
- Variables represent unknown quantities
- Expressions combine variables and operations
- Equations state equality between expressions
- Functions describe relationships between quantities
- Polynomials are expressions with non-negative integer exponents
- Abstract algebra generalizes algebraic structures"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]Elementary Algebra{note:"The foundational study of algebraic expressions, equations, and functions. Elementary algebra provides the tools for solving linear and quadratic equations, working with polynomials, and understanding functions. It is the gateway to all higher mathematics."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]Linear Equations{note:"Linear equations are equations of the form ax + b = c, where a, b, c are constants and a ≠ 0. They have one solution and graph as straight lines. Linear equations model many real-world situations: cost, distance, and uniform motion. Solving linear equations involves isolating the variable using inverse operations.

Key properties:
- Highest exponent of variable is 1
- Graph is a straight line
- One solution (unless a = 0)
- Can be written in standard form: ax + b = 0
- Addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division properties of equality apply"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]One-Step Equations{note:"One-step equations require a single operation to isolate the variable. They are the simplest type of linear equations. To solve, use the inverse operation on both sides of the equation. This maintains equality because whatever you do to one side must be done to the other.

Key types and their inverse operations:
- x + a = b → subtract a from both sides (inverse of addition is subtraction)
- x - a = b → add a to both sides (inverse of subtraction is addition)
- ax = b → divide both sides by a (inverse of multiplication is division)
- x/a = b → multiply both sides by a (inverse of division is multiplication)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of one-step equations with step-by-step solutions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Addition Form){note:"Solve: x + 7 = 12

Step 1: The equation is x + 7 = 12.
Step 2: To isolate x, subtract 7 from both sides (inverse of addition).
Step 3: x + 7 - 7 = 12 - 7
Step 4: Simplify: x = 5
Step 5: Check: 5 + 7 = 12 ✓

Answer: x = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Budgeting){note:"Practical Application: Tracking remaining money after spending.

Scenario: You receive $12 in allowance. After buying a snack for $7, how much money do you have left?

Variables:
- x = the amount of money you have left (this is what we want to find)
- 7 = amount spent on the snack
- 12 = total allowance received

The equation: x + 7 = 12
- x represents your remaining money
- 7 is the money you spent
- 12 is the total you started with

Step 1: Subtract 7 from both sides of the equation:
   x + 7 - 7 = 12 - 7

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 5

Result: You have $5 left after buying the snack.

Real-world application: This equation appears anytime you need to find an original amount after spending some money. For example:
- If you have $50 and spend $32 on groceries, how much remains? x + 32 = 50 → x = 18
- If you have $100 and buy a $45 item, how much is left? x + 45 = 100 → x = 55"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Distance Traveled){note:"Practical Application: Finding distance remaining in a journey.

Scenario: A delivery driver has a route of 12 stops. After completing x stops, 7 stops remain. How many stops have been completed?

Variables:
- x = the number of stops already completed (what we want to find)
- 7 = the number of stops remaining
- 12 = the total number of stops

The equation: x + 7 = 12
- x represents completed stops
- 7 represents stops still to go
- 12 is the total route length

Step 1: Subtract 7 from both sides:
   x + 7 - 7 = 12 - 7

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 5

Result: The driver has completed 5 stops.

Real-world application: This equation works for any journey where you know the total distance and remaining distance:
- Marathon is 26.2 miles. After running x miles, 10 miles remain. How far have you run? x + 10 = 26.2 → x = 16.2
- A flight is 1,200 miles. After x miles, 400 miles remain. How many miles have been traveled? x + 400 = 1200 → x = 800"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Inventory Management){note:"Practical Application: Calculating how many items to order for restocking.

Scenario: A store needs 12 boxes of cereal on the shelf. There are currently 7 boxes. How many more boxes need to be brought from the stockroom?

Variables:
- x = the number of boxes to bring from the stockroom (what we want to find)
- 7 = the number of boxes already on the shelf
- 12 = the target number of boxes needed

The equation: x + 7 = 12
- x represents boxes needed from the stockroom
- 7 represents boxes already on the shelf
- 12 is the total desired quantity

Step 1: Subtract 7 from both sides:
   x + 7 - 7 = 12 - 7

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 5

Result: The staff needs to bring 5 boxes from the stockroom.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any restocking situation:
- A warehouse has 100 units in stock. After shipping x units, 65 units remain. How many were shipped? x + 65 = 100 → x = 35
- A school needs 30 computers for a lab. They currently have 18. How many more do they need? x + 18 = 30 → x = 12"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Subtraction Form){note:"Solve: y - 4 = 9

Step 1: The equation is y - 4 = 9.
Step 2: To isolate y, add 4 to both sides (inverse of subtraction).
Step 3: y - 4 + 4 = 9 + 4
Step 4: Simplify: y = 13
Step 5: Check: 13 - 4 = 9 ✓

Answer: y = 13"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Sports Scoring){note:"Practical Application: Calculating original score before penalties or deductions.

Scenario: A basketball team's final score is 9 points after 4 points were deducted for a violation. How many points did they actually score?

Variables:
- y = the actual points scored (what we want to find)
- 4 = the points deducted for the violation
- 9 = the final score after deduction

The equation: y - 4 = 9
- y represents the actual points scored
- 4 represents the penalty deduction
- 9 represents the final score

Step 1: Add 4 to both sides (inverse of subtraction):
   y - 4 + 4 = 9 + 4

Step 2: Simplify:
   y = 13

Result: The team actually scored 13 points.

Real-world application: This equation applies whenever a deduction is made from a total:
- A hockey team has 2 goals after 3 goals were disallowed. How many goals did they actually score? y - 3 = 2 → y = 5
- A golfer's score is 3 under par after 2 strokes were added for a penalty. What was the original score? y + 2 = -3 → y = -5 (5 under par)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Temperature Change){note:"Practical Application: Finding the original temperature before a change.

Scenario: A city's temperature is now 9°C after dropping 4°C overnight. What was the temperature before the drop?

Variables:
- y = the original temperature (what we want to find)
- 4 = the number of degrees the temperature dropped
- 9 = the temperature after the drop

The equation: y - 4 = 9
- y represents the original temperature
- 4 represents the temperature drop
- 9 represents the final temperature

Step 1: Add 4 to both sides (inverse of subtraction):
   y - 4 + 4 = 9 + 4

Step 2: Simplify:
   y = 13

Result: The original temperature was 13°C.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any situation where a value decreases:
- A stock price is now $72 after falling $5. What was the price before the drop? y - 5 = 72 → y = 77
- The water level is now 3 feet below normal after rising 2 feet. What was the original level? y + 2 = -3 → y = -5 (5 feet below normal)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Finance and Debt){note:"Practical Application: Calculating original debt before payments.

Scenario: A person owes y dollars on a credit card. After making a $4 payment, the remaining balance is $9. What was the original balance?

Variables:
- y = the original balance owed (what we want to find)
- 4 = the payment made
- 9 = the remaining balance after payment

The equation: y - 4 = 9
- y represents the original balance
- 4 represents the payment amount
- 9 represents the remaining balance

Step 1: Add 4 to both sides (inverse of subtraction):
   y - 4 + 4 = 9 + 4

Step 2: Simplify:
   y = 13

Result: The original balance was $13.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any situation where a payment reduces a debt:
- A business had a loss of $5,000 after selling $3,000 in assets. What was the total loss? y - 3000 = 5000 → y = 8000
- After depositing $200, a bank account has $450. What was the balance before the deposit? y + 200 = 450 → y = 250"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Multiplication Form){note:"Solve: 3x = 21

Step 1: The equation is 3x = 21.
Step 2: To isolate x, divide both sides by 3 (inverse of multiplication).
Step 3: 3x/3 = 21/3
Step 4: Simplify: x = 7
Step 5: Check: 3×7 = 21 ✓

Answer: x = 7"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Shopping Cost){note:"Practical Application: Finding the price per item when you know the total cost.

Scenario: 3 bags of apples cost $21 total. How much does each bag cost?

Variables:
- x = the price of one bag of apples (what we want to find)
- 3 = the number of bags
- 21 = the total cost

The equation: 3x = 21
- 3 represents the number of bags
- x represents the price per bag
- 21 represents the total cost

Step 1: Divide both sides by 3 (inverse of multiplication):
   3x/3 = 21/3

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 7

Result: Each bag of apples costs $7.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "total cost = price × quantity" situation:
- 5 gallons of gas cost $25. How much per gallon? 5x = 25 → x = 5
- 4 movie tickets cost $48. What is the price per ticket? 4x = 48 → x = 12"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Hourly Wage){note:"Practical Application: Finding hourly pay rate from total earnings.

Scenario: A worker earns $60 for 8 hours of work. What is the hourly rate?

Variables:
- x = the hourly wage (what we want to find)
- 8 = the number of hours worked
- 60 = the total amount earned

The equation: 8x = 60
- 8 represents the hours worked
- x represents the hourly rate
- 60 represents the total earnings

Step 1: Divide both sides by 8:
   8x/8 = 60/8

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 7.5

Result: The worker earns $7.50 per hour.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "total pay = rate × hours" situation:
- A contractor charges $1,200 for 10 hours of work. What is the hourly rate? 10x = 1200 → x = 120
- A freelancer earns $450 for 15 hours. What is the hourly rate? 15x = 450 → x = 30"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Recipe Scaling){note:"Practical Application: Finding the amount per serving when scaling a recipe.

Scenario: A recipe makes 8 servings and uses 40 ounces of pasta. How many ounces of pasta per serving?

Variables:
- x = the number of ounces per serving (what we want to find)
- 8 = the number of servings
- 40 = the total ounces of pasta

The equation: 8x = 40
- 8 represents the number of servings
- x represents the ounces per serving
- 40 represents the total ounces

Step 1: Divide both sides by 8:
   8x/8 = 40/8

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 5

Result: Each serving uses 5 ounces of pasta.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "total = amount per item × number of items" situation:
- A cake recipe requires 6 cups of flour for 3 cakes. How much flour per cake? 3x = 6 → x = 2
- A party needs 120 cupcakes from 10 boxes. How many cupcakes per box? 10x = 120 → x = 12"}

Step 1: The equation is x/4 = 6.
Step 2: To isolate x, multiply both sides by 4 (inverse of division).
Step 3: (x/4)×4 = 6×4
Step 4: Simplify: x = 24
Step 5: Check: 24/4 = 6 ✓

Answer: x = 24"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Food Portions){note:"Practical Application: Finding total quantity when you know the portion size and number of servings.

Scenario: A cake is cut into 8 pieces, and each piece weighs 3 ounces. What is the total weight of the cake?

Variables:
- x = the total weight of the cake (what we want to find)
- 8 = the number of pieces
- 3 = the weight of each piece in ounces

The equation: x/8 = 3
- x represents the total weight of the cake
- 8 represents the number of pieces
- 3 represents the weight per piece

Step 1: Multiply both sides by 8 (inverse of division):
   (x/8)×8 = 3×8

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 24

Result: The total cake weighs 24 ounces.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "total = portion size × number of portions" situation:
- A watermelon is divided into 6 equal servings, each serving is 2 cups. What is the total volume? x/6 = 2 → x = 12
- A loaf of bread is sliced into 12 pieces, each piece is 0.5 ounces. What is the total weight? x/12 = 0.5 → x = 6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Distance per Unit){note:"Practical Application: Finding total distance when you know the rate and time.

Scenario: A car travels at 60 miles per hour for 5 hours. What is the total distance traveled?

Variables:
- x = the total distance traveled (what we want to find)
- 5 = the number of hours
- 60 = the speed in miles per hour

The equation: x/5 = 60
- x represents the total distance
- 5 represents the number of hours
- 60 represents the speed per hour

Step 1: Multiply both sides by 5:
   (x/5)×5 = 60×5

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 300

Result: The total distance traveled is 300 miles.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "distance = speed × time" situation:
- A construction crew can lay 100 feet of pipe in 4 hours. What is the hourly rate? x/4 = 100 → x = 400 (total pipe laid in 4 hours)
- A swimmer covers 400 meters in 8 minutes. How many meters per minute? x/8 = 400 → x = 3200 (total distance in 8 minutes)"}

- A company's debt is -$10,000 after borrowing $4,000. What was the original debt? x + 4000 = -10000 → x = -14000"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Recipe Scaling){note:"Practical Application: Finding the number of batches needed when you know total ingredients required.

Scenario: A recipe uses 1/3 cup of sugar per batch. You need 5 cups of sugar total. How many batches can you make?

Variables:
- x = the number of batches (what we want to find)
- 3 = the denominator representing 1/3 cup per batch
- 5 = the total cups of sugar needed

The equation: x/3 = 5
- x represents the number of batches
- 3 represents that each batch uses 1/3 cup
- 5 represents the total cups needed

Step 1: Multiply both sides by 3:
   (x/3)×3 = 5×3

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 15

Result: You can make 15 batches.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any recipe scaling situation:
- A medication requires 1/2 tablet per dose. You need 3 tablets total. How many doses? x/2 = 3 → x = 6
- A cake recipe uses 2/3 cup of milk per cake. You need 4 cups total. How many cakes? (2x)/3 = 4 → 2x = 12 → x = 6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Measurement Conversions){note:"Practical Application: Converting between units of measurement.

Scenario: A recipe calls for 2.5 cups of flour. If 1 cup = 8 ounces, how many ounces of flour are needed?

Variables:
- x = the total ounces of flour (what we want to find)
- 8 = the number of ounces in one cup
- 2.5 = the number of cups needed

The equation: x/8 = 2.5
- x represents the total ounces
- 8 represents the ounces per cup
- 2.5 represents the number of cups

Step 1: Multiply both sides by 8:
   (x/8)×8 = 2.5×8

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 20

Result: You need 20 ounces of flour.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any unit conversion:
- A distance of 4 kilometers is x miles. If 1 km = 0.621 miles, x = 4×0.621 = 2.484 miles
- A temperature of 20°C is x°F. If 1°C = 1.8°F and freezing is 32°F, x = 1.8(20) + 32 = 68°F"}

The equation: x/6 = 24
- x represents the pencils per student
- 6 represents the number of students
- 24 represents the total pencils

Wait, this is actually x = 24/6 = 4. Let me rephrase:

The equation: 24/6 = x
- 24 represents the total pencils
- 6 represents the number of students
- x represents the pencils per student

Step 1: Divide 24 by 6:
   x = 24/6

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 4

Result: Each student gets 4 pencils.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any equal sharing situation:
- A $120 gift card is shared equally among 4 people. How much does each receive? 120/4 = 30 → each gets $30
- A 500-page book is read equally over 5 days. How many pages per day? 500/5 = 100 → 100 pages per day"}

Step 1: The equation is -4x = 20.
Step 2: To isolate x, divide both sides by -4 (inverse of multiplication by -4).
Step 3: (-4x)/(-4) = 20/(-4)
Step 4: Simplify: x = -5
Step 5: Check: -4×(-5) = 20 ✓

Answer: x = -5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Financial Debt){note:"Practical Application: Finding individual debt when sharing a total debt equally.

Scenario: 4 people share a debt of $20 equally. How much does each person owe?

Variables:
- x = the amount each person owes (what we want to find)
- 4 = the number of people
- -20 = the total debt (negative because it's money owed)

The equation: 4x = -20
- 4 represents the number of people
- x represents the amount each owes (a negative number)
- -20 represents the total debt

Step 1: Divide both sides by 4:
   4x/4 = -20/4

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = -5

Result: Each person owes -$5 (meaning they owe $5 each).

Real-world application: This equation applies to any shared debt situation:
- 3 people share a debt of $18 equally. How much does each person owe? 3x = -18 → x = -6
- 5 friends each owe money, total debt is -$125. How much does each owe? 5x = -125 → x = -25"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Temperature Decrease){note:"Practical Application: Finding total temperature change from a consistent rate.

Scenario: A city's temperature drops 3°C each night for 6 nights. What is the total temperature change?

Variables:
- x = the total temperature change (what we want to find)
- 3 = the temperature drop per night
- 6 = the number of nights

The equation: (-3)×6 = x
- -3 represents the drop per night
- 6 represents the number of nights
- x represents the total change

Step 1: Multiply:
   (-3)×6 = -18

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = -18

Result: The total temperature change is -18°C.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any consistent rate of change:
- A freezer cools at -2°C per minute for 8 minutes. What is the total change? (-2)×8 = -16
- A swimming pool cools -1°F per hour overnight for 12 hours. What is the total change? (-1)×12 = -12"}

- x = the total change in depth (what we want to find)
- -100 = the descent rate per minute (negative because going down)
- 5 = the number of minutes

The equation: (-100)×5 = x
- -100 represents the descent rate per minute
- 5 represents the number of minutes
- x represents the total change in depth

Step 1: Multiply:
   (-100)×5 = -500

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = -500

Result: The submarine descends -500 meters.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any consistent rate of change:
- An elevator descends at -2 floors per second for 8 seconds. Total change = -16 floors
- A plane descends -500 feet per minute for 4 minutes. Total descent = -2000 feet
- A mountain climber descends -200 meters per hour for 3 hours. Total change = -600 meters"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[8]Example 8 (Decimal){note:"Solve: x + 2.5 = 7.3

Step 1: The equation is x + 2.5 = 7.3.
Step 2: To isolate x, subtract 2.5 from both sides (inverse of addition).
Step 3: x + 2.5 - 2.5 = 7.3 - 2.5
Step 4: Simplify: x = 4.8
Step 5: Check: 4.8 + 2.5 = 7.3 ✓

Answer: x = 4.8"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[8]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Shopping with Tax){note:"Practical Application: Finding the price before tax was added.

Scenario: A meal costs $15.25 after adding a $2.00 tip. What was the meal price?

Variables:
- x = the original meal price (what we want to find)
- 2.00 = the tip amount
- 15.25 = the total cost after tip

The equation: x + 2.00 = 15.25
- x represents the original meal price
- 2.00 represents the tip
- 15.25 represents the total cost

Step 1: Subtract 2.00 from both sides:
   x + 2.00 - 2.00 = 15.25 - 2.00

Step 2: Simplify:
   x = 13.25

Result: The original meal price was $13.25.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any situation where an added amount increases the total:
- A purchase totals $48.75 after $3.75 in taxes. What was the pre-tax amount? x + 3.75 = 48.75 → x = 45.00
- A delivery fee of $4.50 makes the total $32.75. What was the original price? x + 4.50 = 32.75 → x = 28.25"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]Two-Step Equations{note:"Two-step equations require two operations to isolate the variable. The general form is ax + b = c. To solve, first add or subtract, then multiply or divide. The order of operations is reversed when solving: we undo addition/subtraction before multiplication/division.

Key steps:
1. Add or subtract to isolate the term with the variable (undo addition/subtraction)
2. Multiply or divide to solve for the variable (undo multiplication/division)

Common forms:
- ax + b = c (add/subtract then divide)
- x/a + b = c (add/subtract then multiply)
- a(x + b) = c (distribute first, then solve)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of two-step equations with step-by-step solutions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Addition First){note:"Solve: 2x + 5 = 13

Step 1: The equation is 2x + 5 = 13.
Step 2: Subtract 5 from both sides to isolate the term with x:
    2x + 5 - 5 = 13 - 5
    2x = 8
Step 3: Divide both sides by 2:
    2x/2 = 8/2
    x = 4
Step 4: Check: 2×4 + 5 = 8 + 5 = 13 ✓

Answer: x = 4"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Cab Fare){note:"Practical Application: Finding the distance traveled based on total cost with a base fare.

Scenario: A cab charges $5 base fare plus $2 per mile. The total fare is $13. How many miles were traveled?

Variables:
- x = the number of miles traveled (what we want to find)
- 5 = the base fare
- 2 = the cost per mile
- 13 = the total fare

The equation: 2x + 5 = 13
- 2x represents the cost for x miles ($2 per mile)
- 5 represents the base fare
- 13 represents the total fare

Step 1: Subtract 5 from both sides (isolate the term with x):
   2x + 5 - 5 = 13 - 5
   2x = 8

Step 2: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 = 8/2
   x = 4

Result: The cab traveled 4 miles.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "total cost = per-unit cost × quantity + fixed cost" situation:
- A delivery service charges $1.50 per mile plus $3 base. Total cost is $12. How many miles? 1.5x + 3 = 12 → 1.5x = 9 → x = 6
- A cell phone plan costs $30/month plus $0.05 per text. Total bill is $50. How many texts? 0.05x + 30 = 50 → 0.05x = 20 → x = 400"}

- x = the number of units ordered (what we want to find)
- 8 = the cost per unit
- 15 = the shipping fee
- 95 = the total cost

The equation: 8x + 15 = 95
- 8x represents the cost for x units
- 15 represents the shipping fee
- 95 represents the total cost

Step 1: Subtract 15 from both sides:
   8x + 15 - 15 = 95 - 15
   8x = 80

Step 2: Divide both sides by 8:
   8x/8 = 80/8
   x = 10

Result: 10 units were ordered.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "total = unit price × quantity + additional fee" situation:
- A printer charges $0.10 per page plus $3 setup. Total is $8.50. How many pages? 0.10x + 3 = 8.50 → 0.10x = 5.50 → x = 55

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Inventory Management){note:"Practical Application: Finding the original number of items per container after some were removed.

Variables:
- x = the number of items in each box (what we want to find)
- 3 = the number of boxes
- 7 = the number of items given away
- 20 = the number of items remaining

The equation: 3x - 7 = 20
- 3x represents the total items in all boxes
- 7 represents the items given away
- 20 represents the remaining items

Step 1: Add 7 to both sides (isolate the term with x):
   3x - 7 + 7 = 20 + 7
   3x = 27

Step 2: Divide both sides by 3:
   3x/3 = 27/3
   x = 9

Result: Each box originally had 9 items.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any "total - removed = remaining" situation:
- A warehouse has 8 pallets with x units each. After shipping 20 units, 60 remain. How many units per pallet? 8x - 20 = 60 → 8x = 80 → x = 10
- A factory produces 6 boxes per hour with x items per box. After quality control removes 18 items, 42 remain. How many items per box? 6x - 18 = 42 → 6x = 60 → x = 10"}

- 2 = the cups of flour in the original recipe

- 2x represents the flour needed for x servings (scaled proportionally)
- 5 represents the number of servings you want
- 2 represents the original amount per serving

   (2x/5)×5 = 2×5
   2x = 10

Step 2: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 = 10/2
   x = 5

Result: You need 5 cups of flour.

Step 1: The equation is (2/3)x + 4 = 8.

    (3/2)×(2/3)x = 4×(3/2)
    x = 6
Step 4: Check: (2/3)×6 + 4 = 4 + 4 = 8 ✓

Answer: x = 6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Recipe Adjustment){note:"Practical Application: Finding the original recipe quantity after adding extra ingredients.

Variables:
- x = the cups of sugar in the original recipe (what we want to find)
- 2/3 = the fraction representing the sugar portion
- 4 = the extra cups of other ingredients
- 8 = the total cups

The equation: (2/3)x + 4 = 8
- (2/3)x represents the sugar portion
- 4 represents the other ingredients
- 8 represents the total

Step 1: Subtract 4 from both sides:
   (2/3)x + 4 - 4 = 8 - 4
   (2/3)x = 4

Step 2: Multiply both sides by 3/2 (reciprocal of 2/3):
   (3/2)×(2/3)x = 4×(3/2)
   x = 6

Result: The original recipe uses 6 cups of sugar.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any recipe adjustment:
- A recipe uses 3/4 cup of oil per batch. If you add 2 cups of water, total liquid is 5 cups. How many batches? (3/4)x + 2 = 5 → (3/4)x = 3 → x = 4
- A mix uses 1/2 cup of milk per serving. If you add 1 cup of cream, total dairy is 4 cups. How many servings? (1/2)x + 1 = 4 → (1/2)x = 3 → x = 6"}

Real-world application: This equation applies to any inventory situation:
- A shelf has 1/2 of its books plus 6 new books. Total books is 18. How many books fit? (1/2)x + 6 = 18 → (1/2)x = 12 → x = 24
- A truck is 5/8 full plus 10 boxes. Total boxes is 50. What is the capacity? (5/8)x + 10 = 50 → (5/8)x = 40 → x = 64"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]Example 6 (Parentheses){note:"Solve: 3(x + 2) = 15

Step 1: The equation is 3(x + 2) = 15.
Step 2: Distribute the 3:
    3x + 6 = 15
Step 3: Subtract 6 from both sides:
    3x = 9
Step 4: Divide both sides by 3:
    x = 3
Step 5: Check: 3(3 + 2) = 3×5 = 15 ✓

Answer: x = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Event Planning){note:"Practical Application: Finding the base amount per person when there are extras.

Scenario: A teacher gives each student x pencils plus 2 extra pencils. For 3 students, the total is 15 pencils. How many pencils does each student get?

Variables:
- x = the pencils per student (what we want to find)
- 3 = the number of students
- 2 = the extra pencils per student
- 15 = the total pencils

The equation: 3(x + 2) = 15
- 3 represents the number of students
- (x+2) represents the pencils per student (base x plus 2 extras)
- 15 represents the total pencils

Step 1: Distribute the 3:
   3x + 6 = 15

Step 2: Subtract 6 from both sides:
   3x + 6 - 6 = 15 - 6
   3x = 9

Step 3: Divide both sides by 3:
   3x/3 = 9/3
   x = 3

Result: Each student gets 3 pencils (plus 2 extras, so 5 pencils total per student).

Real-world application: This equation applies to any event planning:
- A party planner gives each guest x balloons plus 3 extras. For 8 guests, total is 48 balloons. How many per guest? 8(x+3) = 48 → 8x+24=48 → 8x=24 → x=3
- A coach gives each player x minutes of playtime plus 5 minutes overtime. For 6 players, total is 48 minutes. How many minutes per player? 6(x+5)=48 → 6x+30=48 → 6x=18 → x=3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Shipping Cost){note:"Practical Application: Finding the base shipping rate per item when there's a handling fee.

Scenario: A courier charges a base rate per item plus a $5 handling fee. For 4 items, the total is $45. What is the base rate per item?

Variables:
- x = the base rate per item (what we want to find)
- 4 = the number of items
- 5 = the handling fee per item
- 45 = the total cost

The equation: 4(x + 5) = 45
- 4 represents the number of items
- (x+5) represents the cost per item (base + handling)
- 45 represents the total cost

Step 1: Distribute the 4:
   4x + 20 = 45

Step 2: Subtract 20 from both sides:
   4x + 20 - 20 = 45 - 20
   4x = 25

Step 3: Divide both sides by 4:
   4x/4 = 25/4
   x = 6.25

Result: The base rate per item is $6.25.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any shipping situation:
- A freight company charges x per pallet plus $10 per truck. For 6 pallets, total is $100. What is the per-pallet charge? 6(x+10)=100 → 6x+60=100 → 6x=40 → x=6.67
- A taxi charges x per mile plus $3 base. For 5 miles, total is $28. What is the per-mile charge? 5(x+3)=28 → 5x+15=28 → 5x=13 → x=2.60"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Construction){note:"Practical Application: Finding the number of rooms per building when there are extras.

    x = 8

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Shopping Cost){note:"Practical Application: Finding the number of items when you know the price per item and a fee.

- 1 = the packaging fee
- 6 = the total cost

The equation: 0.5x + 1 = 6
- 0.5x represents the cost of x ounces
- 1 represents the packaging fee
- 6 represents the total cost

Step 1: Subtract 1 from both sides:
   0.5x + 1 - 1 = 6 - 1
   0.5x = 5

Step 2: Divide both sides by 0.5:
   0.5x/0.5 = 5/0.5
   x = 10

Result: 10 ounces of candy were purchased.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any shopping situation with a fee:
- A gas station charges $0.50 per gallon plus a $2 service fee. Total cost is $12. How many gallons? 0.5x + 2 = 12 → 0.5x = 10 → x = 20
- A parking lot charges $0.75 per hour plus $2 entry fee. Total cost is $8. How many hours? 0.75x + 2 = 8 → 0.75x = 6 → x = 8"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]Multi-Step Equations{note:"Multi-step equations require more than two operations to solve. They may involve parentheses, fractions, decimals, and multiple terms on both sides. The key is to simplify each side first, then solve using inverse operations.

Key steps:
1. Simplify both sides (distribute, combine like terms)
2. Move variable terms to one side
3. Move constant terms to the other side
4. Divide by the coefficient

Common challenges:
- Distributing negative signs
- Combining like terms correctly
- Dealing with fractions by multiplying by LCD"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of multi-step equations with step-by-step solutions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Variables on Both Sides){note:"Solve: 4x + 7 = 3x + 12

Step 1: The equation is 4x + 7 = 3x + 12.
Step 2: Subtract 3x from both sides to get variables on one side:
    4x - 3x + 7 = 3x - 3x + 12
    x + 7 = 12
Step 3: Subtract 7 from both sides:
    x = 5
Step 4: Check: 4×5 + 7 = 20+7=27; 3×5+12=15+12=27 ✓

Answer: x = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Phone Plans){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two phone plans to find when costs are equal.

Scenario: Company A charges $4 per GB plus $7 base. Company B charges $3 per GB plus $12 base. At how many GB are the costs equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of GB used (what we want to find)
- 4 = the cost per GB for Company A
- 7 = the base cost for Company A
- 3 = the cost per GB for Company B
- 12 = the base cost for Company B

The equation: 4x + 7 = 3x + 12
- 4x + 7 represents Company A's cost
- 3x + 12 represents Company B's cost

Step 1: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   4x - 3x + 7 = 3x - 3x + 12
   x + 7 = 12

Step 2: Subtract 7 from both sides:
   x + 7 - 7 = 12 - 7
   x = 5

Result: The costs are equal at 5 GB.

Real-world application: This equation applies to comparing any two pricing plans:
- Streaming services: Service X costs $5/month + $0.50/movie, Service Y costs $3/month + $0.75/movie. How many movies for equal cost? 5 + 0.50x = 3 + 0.75x → 2 = 0.25x → x = 8
- Gym: $30/month + $5/class vs $45/month unlimited. How many classes for equal cost? 30 + 5x = 45 → 5x = 15 → x = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Break-Even Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Finding the break-even point for a business.

Scenario: A business has $7 fixed costs and $4 per unit variable cost vs $12 fixed and $3 per unit variable cost. At how many units do the costs equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of units (what we want to find)
- 7 = the fixed cost for Plan A
- 4 = the per-unit cost for Plan A
- 12 = the fixed cost for Plan B
- 3 = the per-unit cost for Plan B

The equation: 4x + 7 = 3x + 12
- 4x + 7 represents Plan A's total cost
- 3x + 12 represents Plan B's total cost

Step 1: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   4x - 3x + 7 = 3x - 3x + 12
   x + 7 = 12

Step 2: Subtract 7 from both sides:
   x = 5

Result: The costs are equal at 5 units.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any break-even analysis:
- A bakery has rent $100/day + $2/loaf vs $80/day + $3/loaf. How many loaves for equal cost? 100 + 2x = 80 + 3x → 20 = x → x = 20
- A contractor charges $200 + $50/hour vs $150 + $75/hour. How many hours for equal cost? 200 + 50x = 150 + 75x → 50 = 25x → x = 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Membership Comparison){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two membership options.

Scenario: Club A has a $7 sign-up fee and charges $4 per visit. Club B has a $12 sign-up fee and charges $3 per visit. How many visits make the total cost equal?

- 7 = the sign-up fee for Club A
- 4 = the cost per visit for Club A
- 12 = the sign-up fee for Club B
- 3 = the cost per visit for Club B

The equation: 4x + 7 = 3x + 12
- 4x + 7 represents Club A's total cost
- 3x + 12 represents Club B's total cost

Step 1: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   4x - 3x + 7 = 3x - 3x + 12
   x + 7 = 12

Step 2: Subtract 7 from both sides:
   x = 5

Result: The costs are equal at 5 visits.

Real-world application: This equation applies to comparing any membership:
- A bookstore: $10/year + $2/book vs $0/year + $4/book. How many books for equal cost? 10 + 2x = 4x → 10 = 2x → x = 5

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Parentheses){note:"Solve: 3(y-2) = 2y + 1

Step 1: The equation is 3(y-2) = 2y + 1.
Step 2: Distribute on the left side:
    3y - 6 = 2y + 1
Step 3: Subtract 2y from both sides:
    3y - 2y - 6 = 2y - 2y + 1
    y - 6 = 1
Step 4: Add 6 to both sides:
    y = 7
Step 5: Check: 3(7-2) = 3×5=15; 2×7+1=14+1=15 ✓

Answer: y = 7"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Distributive in Business){note:"Practical Application: Finding the original price when applying a discount to multiple items.

Scenario: A store sells 3 items at a discount of $2 each, and the total price equals twice the price of one item plus $5. What is the original price of one item?

Variables:
- x = the original price of one item (what we want to find)
- 3 = the number of items
- 2 = the discount per item
- 2 = the number of items in the comparison
- 5 = the additional amount

The equation: 3(x-2) = 2x + 5
- 3(x-2) represents the discounted price of 3 items
- 2x + 5 represents the comparison price

Step 1: Distribute the 3 on the left:
   3x - 6 = 2x + 5

Step 2: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   3x - 2x - 6 = 2x - 2x + 5
   x - 6 = 5

Step 3: Add 6 to both sides:
   x - 6 + 6 = 5 + 6
   x = 11

Result: The original price of one item is $11.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any discount situation:
- A store has 5 boxes with y items each, removes 3 items from each. Total remaining is 2 boxes plus 5 items. How many per box? 5(y-3) = 2y + 5 → 5y - 15 = 2y + 5 → 3y = 20 → y = 6.67
- A factory has 6 lines producing y units, each line loses 5 units to defects. Total good units is 4 lines plus 10 units. How many per line? 6(y-5) = 4y + 10 → 6y - 30 = 4y + 10 → 2y = 40 → y = 20"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Discount Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Finding the original price before a discount.

Scenario: A merchant sells 5 shirts at $x each with $4 off each. The total revenue equals 3 shirts at $x plus $8. What is x?

Variables:
- x = the original price per shirt (what we want to find)
- 5 = the number of shirts sold
- 4 = the discount per shirt
- 3 = the number of shirts in the comparison
- 8 = the additional revenue

The equation: 5(x-4) = 3x + 8
- 5(x-4) represents the revenue from 5 discounted shirts
- 3x + 8 represents the comparison revenue

Step 1: Distribute the 5 on the left:
   5x - 20 = 3x + 8

Step 2: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   5x - 3x - 20 = 3x - 3x + 8
   2x - 20 = 8

Step 3: Add 20 to both sides:
   2x - 20 + 20 = 8 + 20
   2x = 28

Step 4: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 = 28/2
   x = 14

Result: The original price per shirt is $14.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any discount calculation:
- A bookstore has 4 bestsellers at $x each with $3 discount. Total sales equal 2 bestsellers plus $15. What is x? 4(x-3) = 2x + 15 → 4x - 12 = 2x + 15 → 2x = 27 → x = 13.50
- A grocery store sells 6 pounds of apples at $x per pound, with $0.50 off per pound. Total equals 4 pounds plus $3. What is x? 6(x-0.50) = 4x + 3 → 6x - 3 = 4x + 3 → 2x = 6 → x = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Construction Cost){note:"Practical Application: Finding the cost per unit in construction.

Scenario: A contractor charges 4 hours of labor at $y per hour with $5 discount per hour. The total equals 3 hours plus $10. What is y?

- 5 = the discount per hour
- 3 = the number of hours in the comparison
- 10 = the additional amount

The equation: 4(y-5) = 3y + 10
- 4(y-5) represents the cost of 4 discounted hours
- 3y + 10 represents the comparison cost

   4y - 20 = 3y + 10

Step 2: Subtract 3y from both sides:
   4y - 3y - 20 = 3y - 3y + 10
   y - 20 = 10

Step 3: Add 20 to both sides:
   y - 20 + 20 = 10 + 20
   y = 30

Result: The hourly rate is $30.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any construction cost:
- A landscaper plants 6 trees at $z each with $2 discount, total equals 4 trees plus $16. What is z? 6(z-2) = 4z + 16 → 6z - 12 = 4z + 16 → 2z = 28 → z = 14

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Multiple Variable Terms){note:"Solve: 7x + 2 = 3x + 18

Step 1: The equation is 7x + 2 = 3x + 18.
Step 2: Subtract 3x from both sides:
    7x - 3x + 2 = 3x - 3x + 18
    4x + 2 = 18
Step 3: Subtract 2 from both sides:
    4x = 16
Step 4: Divide both sides by 4:
    x = 4
Step 5: Check: 7×4+2=28+2=30; 3×4+18=12+18=30 ✓

Answer: x = 4"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Inventory Pricing){note:"Practical Application: Finding the price per item when comparing total costs.

Scenario: A store's total cost for 7 items plus $2 equals the cost for 3 items plus $18. What is the price per item?

Variables:
- x = the price per item (what we want to find)
- 7 = the number of items in the first group
- 2 = the additional amount for the first group
- 3 = the number of items in the second group
- 18 = the additional amount for the second group

The equation: 7x + 2 = 3x + 18
- 7x + 2 represents the first group's total cost
- 3x + 18 represents the second group's total cost

Step 1: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   7x - 3x + 2 = 3x - 3x + 18
   4x + 2 = 18

Step 2: Subtract 2 from both sides:
   4x + 2 - 2 = 18 - 2
   4x = 16

Step 3: Divide both sides by 4:
   4x/4 = 16/4
   x = 4

Result: The price per item is $4.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any inventory pricing:
- A warehouse has 9 boxes of x items plus 3 extras, equals 6 boxes plus 27 items. How many per box? 9x + 3 = 6x + 27 → 3x = 24 → x = 8
- A factory produces 10 units of x per hour plus 4 units overtime, equals 7 units per hour plus 25 units. What is x? 10x + 4 = 7x + 25 → 3x = 21 → x = 7"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Financial Comparison){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two investment options to find the equal return point.

Scenario: Investment A returns 7% plus $2. Investment B returns 3% plus $18. At what balance do they give equal returns?

Variables:
- x = the investment balance (what we want to find)
- 0.07 = the rate for Investment A
- 2 = the fixed return for Investment A
- 0.03 = the rate for Investment B
- 18 = the fixed return for Investment B

The equation: 0.07x + 2 = 0.03x + 18
- 0.07x + 2 represents Investment A's return
- 0.03x + 18 represents Investment B's return

Step 1: Subtract 0.03x from both sides:
   0.07x - 0.03x + 2 = 0.03x - 0.03x + 18
   0.04x + 2 = 18

Step 2: Subtract 2 from both sides:
   0.04x + 2 - 2 = 18 - 2
   0.04x = 16

Step 3: Divide both sides by 0.04:
   0.04x/0.04 = 16/0.04
   x = 400

Result: Equal returns at a balance of $400.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any financial comparison:
- A savings account earns 5% plus $50 bonus vs 3% plus $70 bonus. What balance makes them equal? 0.05x + 50 = 0.03x + 70 → 0.02x = 20 → x = 1000
- A credit card offers 2% cashback plus $10 fee vs 1% cashback plus $20 fee. What spending makes them equal? 0.02x + 10 = 0.01x + 20 → 0.01x = 10 → x = 1000"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Retail Pricing){note:"Practical Application: Finding the price per item when comparing discounted vs regular pricing.

Scenario: A store's price for 5 items minus a $3 discount equals the price for 2 items plus $9. What is the price per item?

Variables:
- x = the price per item (what we want to find)
- 5 = the number of items in the first group
- 3 = the discount amount
- 2 = the number of items in the second group
- 9 = the additional amount for the second group

The equation: 5x - 3 = 2x + 9
- 5x - 3 represents the discounted price of 5 items
- 2x + 9 represents the regular price of 2 items plus $9

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   5x - 2x - 3 = 2x - 2x + 9
   3x - 3 = 9

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   3x - 3 + 3 = 9 + 3
   3x = 12

Step 3: Divide both sides by 3:
   3x/3 = 12/3
   x = 4

Result: The price per item is $4.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any retail pricing:
- A store sells 7 shirts at $x minus $5 coupon, equals 3 shirts plus $15. What is x? 7x - 5 = 3x + 15 → 4x = 20 → x = 5
- A restaurant has 8 meals at $x minus $2 special, equals 4 meals plus $10. What is x? 8x - 2 = 4x + 10 → 4x = 12 → x = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Profit Margin){note:"Practical Application: Comparing profits from different products.

Scenario: A product's profit is 5x - $3 vs 2x + $9. At what x are the profits equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of units (what we want to find)
- 5 = the profit per unit for Product A
- 3 = the fixed cost for Product A
- 2 = the profit per unit for Product B
- 9 = the fixed profit for Product B

The equation: 5x - 3 = 2x + 9
- 5x - 3 represents Product A's profit
- 2x + 9 represents Product B's profit

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   5x - 2x - 3 = 2x - 2x + 9
   3x - 3 = 9

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   3x - 3 + 3 = 9 + 3
   3x = 12

Step 3: Divide both sides by 3:
   3x/3 = 12/3
   x = 4

Result: Equal profits at 4 units.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any profit comparison:
- A business earns 8x - $20 from product A vs 3x + $30 from product B. Equal profit at x = 10: 8x - 20 = 3x + 30 → 5x = 50 → x = 10
- A company's revenue is 10x - $15 vs 6x + $45. Equal revenue at x = 15: 10x - 15 = 6x + 45 → 4x = 60 → x = 15"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Manufacturing){note:"Practical Application: Comparing output from two production lines.

Scenario: Production line A produces 5x - 3 units. Line B produces 2x + 9 units. At what x are the outputs equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of hours (what we want to find)
- 5 = the production rate for Line A
- 3 = the units lost for Line A
- 2 = the production rate for Line B
- 9 = the units gained for Line B

The equation: 5x - 3 = 2x + 9
- 5x - 3 represents Line A's output
- 2x + 9 represents Line B's output

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   5x - 2x - 3 = 2x - 2x + 9
   3x - 3 = 9

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   3x - 3 + 3 = 9 + 3
   3x = 12

Step 3: Divide both sides by 3:
   3x/3 = 12/3
   x = 4

Result: Equal output at 4 hours.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any manufacturing comparison:
- Factory A: 12x - 8 items vs Factory B: 8x + 16 items. Equal output at x = 6: 12x - 8 = 8x + 16 → 4x = 24 → x = 6
- Plant A: 15x - 12 products vs Plant B: 10x + 18 products. Equal output at x = 6: 15x - 12 = 10x + 18 → 5x = 30 → x = 6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Fractions){note:"Solve: (x+2)/3 = 5

Step 1: The equation is (x+2)/3 = 5.
Step 2: Multiply both sides by 3:
    3×(x+2)/3 = 5×3
    x+2 = 15
Step 3: Subtract 2 from both sides:
    x = 13
Step 4: Check: (13+2)/3 = 15/3 = 5 ✓

Answer: x = 13"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Average Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Finding an original number when the average is known.

Scenario: A student's average score after adding 5 points is 8. What was the original score?

Variables:
- x = the original score (what we want to find)
- 5 = the points added
- 8 = the average after adding
- 2 = the number of tests

The equation: (x+5)/2 = 8
- (x+5)/2 represents the average of the original score plus 5

Step 1: Multiply both sides by 2:
   (x+5)/2 × 2 = 8 × 2
   x+5 = 16

Step 2: Subtract 5 from both sides:
   x+5 - 5 = 16 - 5
   x = 11

Result: The original score was 11.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any average calculation:
- A group's average age after adding 3 years is 10. What was the average age? (x+3)/2 = 10 → x+3 = 20 → x = 17
- A team's average height after adding 4 inches is 12. What was the average height? (x+4)/2 = 12 → x+4 = 24 → x = 20"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Recipe Adjustment){note:"Practical Application: Finding the total amount of an ingredient when scaling a recipe.

Variables:
- x = the cups of flour (what we want to find)
- 3 = the cups of water
- 4 = the number of servings
- 6 = the cups per serving

The equation: (x+3)/4 = 6
- (x+3)/4 represents the cups per serving

Step 1: Multiply both sides by 4:
   (x+3)/4 × 4 = 6 × 4
   x+3 = 24

Step 2: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   x+3 - 3 = 24 - 3
   x = 21

Result: The recipe uses 21 cups of flour.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any recipe scaling:
- A cake mix uses x cups of flour plus 1 cup of sugar for 5 cakes, each cake uses 3 cups. How much flour? (x+1)/5 = 3 → x+1 = 15 → x = 14
- A soup recipe uses x ounces of broth plus 2 ounces of seasoning for 8 bowls, each bowl is 5 ounces. How much broth? (x+2)/8 = 5 → x+2 = 40 → x = 38"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Project Management){note:"Practical Application: Finding the number of tasks when scheduling a project.

- x = the number of tasks (what we want to find)
- 2 = the review tasks
- 4 = the number of teams
- 5 = the tasks per team

- (x+2)/4 represents the tasks per team

   (x+2)/4 × 4 = 5 × 4
   x+2 = 20

   x = 18

Result: The project has 18 tasks.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]Example 6 (Multiple Fractions){note:"Solve: x/2 + x/3 = 5

Step 1: The equation is x/2 + x/3 = 5.
Step 2: Find the LCD of 2 and 3, which is 6.
Step 3: Multiply both sides by 6:
    6×(x/2 + x/3) = 6×5
    6x/2 + 6x/3 = 30
    3x + 2x = 30
    5x = 30
Step 4: Divide both sides by 5:
    x = 6
Step 5: Check: 6/2 + 6/3 = 3 + 2 = 5 ✓

Answer: x = 6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Resource Allocation){note:"Practical Application: Finding the total budget when fractions are spent on different categories.

Scenario: A team spends 1/2 of the budget on marketing and 1/3 on development. The total spent is $5,000. What is the total budget?

Variables:
- x = the total budget (what we want to find)
- 1/2 = the fraction spent on marketing
- 1/3 = the fraction spent on development
- 5000 = the total amount spent

The equation: x/2 + x/3 = 5000
- x/2 represents the marketing portion
- x/3 represents the development portion

Step 1: Find LCD of 2 and 3, which is 6.
Step 2: Multiply both sides by 6:
   6×(x/2 + x/3) = 6×5000
   3x + 2x = 30000
   5x = 30000

Step 3: Divide both sides by 5:
   5x/5 = 30000/5
   x = 6000

Result: The total budget is $6,000.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any resource allocation:
- A project uses 1/5 of time for planning and 1/3 for execution, total time is 16 hours. What is total time? x/5 + x/3 = 16 → 3x/15 + 5x/15 = 16 → 8x/15 = 16 → x = 30
- A recipe uses 2/5 cup of sugar and 1/4 cup of oil, total is 13 cups. What is the total amount? 2x/5 + x/4 = 13 → 8x/20 + 5x/20 = 13 → 13x/20 = 13 → x = 20"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Distance Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Finding total trip distance when fractions of the trip are completed.

Scenario: A runner covers x/4 of a marathon and x/5 more, total is 9 miles. What is the marathon distance?

Variables:
- x = the total marathon distance (what we want to find)
- 1/4 = the first fraction of the distance
- 1/5 = the second fraction of the distance
- 9 = the total distance covered

The equation: x/4 + x/5 = 9
- x/4 represents the first portion
- x/5 represents the second portion

Step 1: Find LCD of 4 and 5, which is 20.
Step 2: Multiply both sides by 20:
   20×(x/4 + x/5) = 20×9
   5x + 4x = 180
   9x = 180

Step 3: Divide both sides by 9:
   9x/9 = 180/9
   x = 20

Result: The marathon distance is 20 miles.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any distance calculation:
- A plane travels x/3 of a route and x/4 more, total is 1,400 miles. What is the total route? x/3 + x/4 = 1400 → 4x/12 + 3x/12 = 1400 → 7x/12 = 1400 → x = 2400
- A hiker walks x/2 of a trail and x/3 more, total is 25 km. What is trail length? x/2 + x/3 = 25 → 3x/6 + 2x/6 = 25 → 5x/6 = 25 → x = 30"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]Example 7 (Distributive and Combining){note:"Solve: 2(x+3) + 3(x-1) = 18

Step 1: The equation is 2(x+3) + 3(x-1) = 18.
Step 2: Distribute:
    2x + 6 + 3x - 3 = 18
Step 3: Combine like terms:
    5x + 3 = 18
Step 4: Subtract 3 from both sides:
    5x = 15
Step 5: Divide both sides by 5:
    x = 3
Step 6: Check: 2(3+3)+3(3-1)=2×6+3×2=12+6=18 ✓

Answer: x = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Inventory Management){note:"Practical Application: Combining inventory from two suppliers.

Scenario: A store receives 2(x+3) items from supplier A and 3(x-1) items from supplier B. The total is 18 items. What is x?

Variables:
- x = the base number of items per shipment (what we want to find)
- 2 = the number of shipments from supplier A
- 3 = the extra items in each A shipment
- 3 = the number of shipments from supplier B
- 1 = the items deducted from each B shipment
- 18 = the total items received

The equation: 2(x+3) + 3(x-1) = 18
- 2(x+3) represents items from supplier A
- 3(x-1) represents items from supplier B

Step 1: Distribute:
   2x + 6 + 3x - 3 = 18

Step 2: Combine like terms:
   5x + 3 = 18

Step 3: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   5x + 3 - 3 = 18 - 3
   5x = 15

Step 4: Divide both sides by 5:
   5x/5 = 15/5
   x = 3

Result: The base number of items per shipment is 3.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any inventory combination:
- A warehouse gets 3(y+4) pallets and 5(y-1) pallets, total 37 pallets. What is y? 3(y+4) + 5(y-1) = 37 → 3y+12 + 5y-5 = 37 → 8y+7 = 37 → 8y = 30 → y = 3.75
- A factory produces 5(z+2) units and 4(z-3) units, total 58 units. What is z? 5(z+2) + 4(z-3) = 58 → 5z+10 + 4z-12 = 58 → 9z-2 = 58 → 9z = 60 → z = 6.67"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Business Revenue){note:"Practical Application: Combining revenue from two different sales channels.

Scenario: Product A revenue: 2(x+3). Product B revenue: 3(x-1). Total revenue is $18. What is x?

Variables:
- x = the base price (what we want to find)
- 2 = the number of Product A items
- 3 = the extra price for Product A
- 3 = the number of Product B items
- 1 = the discount for Product B
- 18 = the total revenue

The equation: 2(x+3) + 3(x-1) = 18
- 2(x+3) represents Product A revenue
- 3(x-1) represents Product B revenue

Step 1: Distribute:
   2x + 6 + 3x - 3 = 18

Step 2: Combine like terms:
   5x + 3 = 18

Step 3: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   5x + 3 - 3 = 18 - 3
   5x = 15

Step 4: Divide both sides by 5:
   5x/5 = 15/5
   x = 3

Result: The base price is $3.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any revenue combination:
- A store sells 3(x+5) items at $x and 2(x-2) items at $x, total revenue is $120. What is x? 3(x+5)x + 2(x-2)x = 120 → 3x²+15x + 2x²-4x = 120 → 5x²+11x-120=0
- A company has 4(x+6) employees in department A and 3(x-4) in department B, total 52 employees. What is x? 4(x+6) + 3(x-4) = 52 → 4x+24 + 3x-12 = 52 → 7x+12 = 52 → 7x = 40 → x = 5.71"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Construction Planning){note:"Practical Application: Combining units from multiple phases of construction.

- 2 = the number of Phase 1 units
- 3 = the extra houses in Phase 1
- 3 = the number of Phase 2 units
- 1 = the fewer houses in Phase 2
- 18 = the total houses

The equation: 2(x+3) + 3(x-1) = 18
- 2(x+3) represents Phase 1 houses
- 3(x-1) represents Phase 2 houses

Step 1: Distribute:
   2x + 6 + 3x - 3 = 18

Step 2: Combine like terms:
   5x + 3 = 18

Step 3: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   5x + 3 - 3 = 18 - 3
   5x = 15

Step 4: Divide both sides by 5:
   5x/5 = 15/5
   x = 3

Result: The base number of houses per phase is 3.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any construction planning:

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]Equations with Variables on Both Sides{note:"Equations with variables on both sides require moving all variable terms to one side and all constant terms to the other. The goal is to isolate the variable. This is a common form in algebra.

Key strategy:
1. Choose a side to move variables to (usually the left)
2. Use addition/subtraction to move variable terms
3. Use addition/subtraction to move constant terms
4. Divide by coefficient if needed

Special cases:
- If variable terms cancel and you get a true statement (e.g., 3 = 3), the equation has infinitely many solutions (identity)
- If variable terms cancel and you get a false statement (e.g., 3 = 5), the equation has no solution (contradiction)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of equations with variables on both sides."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Basic){note:"Solve: 3x + 4 = 2x + 10

Step 1: The equation is 3x + 4 = 2x + 10.
Step 2: Subtract 2x from both sides:
    3x - 2x + 4 = 2x - 2x + 10
    x + 4 = 10
Step 3: Subtract 4 from both sides:
    x = 6
Step 4: Check: 3×6+4=18+4=22; 2×6+10=12+10=22 ✓

Answer: x = 6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Competing Plans){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two service plans to find when costs are equal.

Scenario: Service A charges $3 per month plus $4 setup. Service B charges $2 per month plus $10 setup. At how many months are the costs equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of months (what we want to find)
- 3 = the monthly cost for Service A
- 4 = the setup fee for Service A
- 2 = the monthly cost for Service B
- 10 = the setup fee for Service B

The equation: 3x + 4 = 2x + 10
- 3x + 4 represents Service A's total cost
- 2x + 10 represents Service B's total cost

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   3x - 2x + 4 = 2x - 2x + 10
   x + 4 = 10

Step 2: Subtract 4 from both sides:
   x + 4 - 4 = 10 - 4
   x = 6

Result: The costs are equal at 6 months.

Real-world application: This equation applies to comparing any plans:
- A gym charges $5/month + $30 membership vs $8/month + $12 membership. How many months for equal cost? 5x + 30 = 8x + 12 → 30 - 12 = 8x - 5x → 18 = 3x → x = 6
- A cell phone plan: $0.10/min + $15/month vs $0.15/min + $10/month. How many minutes for equal cost? 0.10x + 15 = 0.15x + 10 → 15 - 10 = 0.15x - 0.10x → 5 = 0.05x → x = 100"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Break-Even Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Finding the break-even point between two pricing options.

Scenario: A company has $4 fixed costs plus $3 per unit vs $10 fixed plus $2 per unit. At what number of units do the costs equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of units (what we want to find)
- 4 = the fixed cost for Option A
- 3 = the per-unit cost for Option A
- 10 = the fixed cost for Option B
- 2 = the per-unit cost for Option B

The equation: 3x + 4 = 2x + 10
- 3x + 4 represents Option A's total cost
- 2x + 10 represents Option B's total cost

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   3x - 2x + 4 = 2x - 2x + 10
   x + 4 = 10

Step 2: Subtract 4 from both sides:
   x + 4 - 4 = 10 - 4
   x = 6

Result: Equal costs at 6 units.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any break-even analysis:
- A bakery: $50/day + $2/loaf vs $80/day + $1.50/loaf. How many loaves for equal cost? 50 + 2x = 80 + 1.50x → 0.50x = 30 → x = 60
- A coffee shop: $5/hour + $3/cup vs $0/hour + $4/cup. How many cups for equal cost? 5 + 3x = 4x → 5 = x → x = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Pricing Strategy){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two pricing strategies.

Scenario: A store charges $3 per item plus $4 handling vs $2 per item plus $10 handling. At what number of items do they cost the same?

Variables:
- x = the number of items (what we want to find)
- 3 = the price per item for Strategy A
- 4 = the handling fee for Strategy A
- 2 = the price per item for Strategy B
- 10 = the handling fee for Strategy B

The equation: 3x + 4 = 2x + 10
- 3x + 4 represents Strategy A's total cost
- 2x + 10 represents Strategy B's total cost

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   3x - 2x + 4 = 2x - 2x + 10
   x + 4 = 10

Step 2: Subtract 4 from both sides:
   x + 4 - 4 = 10 - 4
   x = 6

Result: Equal costs at 6 items.

Real-world application: This equation applies to comparing pricing strategies:
- A merchant: $15/item + $5 shipping vs $12/item + $23 shipping. How many items for equal cost? 15x + 5 = 12x + 23 → 3x = 18 → x = 6
- A ticket seller: $8/ticket + $2 fee vs $6/ticket + $12 fee. How many tickets for equal cost? 8x + 2 = 6x + 12 → 2x = 10 → x = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Negative Variables){note:"Solve: 5y - 3 = 2y + 9

Step 1: The equation is 5y - 3 = 2y + 9.
Step 2: Subtract 2y from both sides:
    5y - 2y - 3 = 2y - 2y + 9
    3y - 3 = 9
Step 3: Add 3 to both sides:
    3y = 12
Step 4: Divide by 3:
    y = 4
Step 5: Check: 5×4-3=20-3=17; 2×4+9=8+9=17 ✓

Answer: y = 4"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Profit Maximization){note:"Practical Application: Finding the number of units where two profit functions are equal.

Scenario: Business A earns $5 per item but loses $3 per item in returns. Business B earns $2 per item plus $9 fixed profit. At how many items are the profits equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of items (what we want to find)
- 5 = the profit per item for Business A
- 3 = the loss per item for Business A
- 2 = the profit per item for Business B
- 9 = the fixed profit for Business B

The equation: 5x - 3 = 2x + 9
- 5x - 3 represents Business A's total profit
- 2x + 9 represents Business B's total profit

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   5x - 2x - 3 = 2x - 2x + 9
   3x - 3 = 9

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   3x - 3 + 3 = 9 + 3
   3x = 12

Step 3: Divide by 3:
   3x/3 = 12/3
   x = 4

Result: Equal profits at 4 items.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any profit comparison:
- A company: 8x - $5 profit vs 3x + $10 profit. How many units for equal profit? 8x - 5 = 3x + 10 → 5x = 15 → x = 3
- A store: 6x - $4 revenue vs 2x + $8 revenue. How many items for equal revenue? 6x - 4 = 2x + 8 → 4x = 12 → x = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Tax Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two tax calculation methods.

Scenario: Tax method A: 5% - $3 deduction. Tax method B: 2% + $9 deduction. At what income do both methods give the same tax?

Variables:
- x = the income amount (what we want to find)
- 0.05 = the rate for Method A
- 3 = the deduction for Method A
- 0.02 = the rate for Method B
- 9 = the addition for Method B

The equation: 0.05x - 3 = 0.02x + 9
- 0.05x - 3 represents Method A's tax
- 0.02x + 9 represents Method B's tax

Step 1: Subtract 0.02x from both sides:
   0.05x - 0.02x - 3 = 0.02x - 0.02x + 9
   0.03x - 3 = 9

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   0.03x - 3 + 3 = 9 + 3
   0.03x = 12

Step 3: Divide both sides by 0.03:
   0.03x/0.03 = 12/0.03
   x = 400

Result: Equal tax at an income of $400.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any tax comparison:
- A commission: 10% - $100 vs 6% + $200. What sales gives equal commission? 0.10x - 100 = 0.06x + 200 → 0.04x = 300 → x = 7500
- A tip calculation: 15% - $2 vs 20% + $1. What bill gives equal tip? 0.15x - 2 = 0.20x + 1 → -3 = 0.05x → x = -60 (no solution)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Employee Compensation){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two employee compensation plans.

Scenario: Employee A: $5/hour - $3 commission. Employee B: $2/hour + $9 commission. At how many hours are the pay rates equal?

Variables:
- x = the number of hours (what we want to find)
- 5 = the hourly rate for Employee A
- 3 = the commission loss for Employee A
- 2 = the hourly rate for Employee B
- 9 = the commission gain for Employee B

The equation: 5x - 3 = 2x + 9
- 5x - 3 represents Employee A's pay
- 2x + 9 represents Employee B's pay

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   5x - 2x - 3 = 2x - 2x + 9
   3x - 3 = 9

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   3x - 3 + 3 = 9 + 3
   3x = 12

Step 3: Divide by 3:
   3x/3 = 12/3
   x = 4

Result: Equal pay at 4 hours.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any compensation comparison:
- A salesperson: $20/hour - $50 commission vs $15/hour + $10 commission. How many hours for equal pay? 20x - 50 = 15x + 10 → 5x = 60 → x = 12
- A freelancer: $50/hour - $100 expenses vs $35/hour + $50 expenses. How many hours for equal pay? 50x - 100 = 35x + 50 → 15x = 150 → x = 10"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Variables on Right){note:"Solve: 8x - 3 = 5x + 12

Step 1: The equation is 8x - 3 = 5x + 12.
Step 2: Subtract 5x from both sides:
    8x - 5x - 3 = 5x - 5x + 12
    3x - 3 = 12
Step 3: Add 3 to both sides:
    3x = 15
Step 4: Divide by 3:
    x = 5
Step 5: Check: 8×5-3=40-3=37; 5×5+12=25+12=37 ✓

Answer: x = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Inventory Management){note:"Practical Application: Finding the number of items per shipment when total items are known.

Scenario: A warehouse has 8 boxes of x items minus 8 damaged equals 6 boxes plus 12 items. How many items per box?

Variables:
- x = the items per box (what we want to find)
- 8 = the number of boxes in the first shipment
- 8 = the damaged items
- 6 = the number of boxes in the second shipment
- 12 = the items in the second shipment

The equation: 8x - 8 = 6x + 12
- 8x - 8 represents the first shipment (minus damaged)
- 6x + 12 represents the second shipment

Step 1: Subtract 6x from both sides:
   8x - 6x - 8 = 6x - 6x + 12
   2x - 8 = 12

Step 2: Add 8 to both sides:
   2x - 8 + 8 = 12 + 8
   2x = 20

Step 3: Divide by 2:
   2x/2 = 20/2
   x = 10

Result: Each box has 10 items.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any inventory management:
- A factory produces 12 units of x minus 5 defects, equals 7 units plus 20 items. How many per unit? 12x - 5 = 7x + 20 → 5x = 25 → x = 5
- A store has 15 shelves of x items minus 10 sold, equals 8 shelves plus 25 items. How many per shelf? 15x - 10 = 8x + 25 → 7x = 35 → x = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Fleet Management){note:"Practical Application: Comparing capacity of different sized vehicles.

- x = the capacity per truck (what we want to find)
- 8 = the number of trucks in the first fleet
- 3 = the tons lost in the first fleet
- 5 = the number of trucks in the second fleet
- 12 = the tons in the second fleet

The equation: 8x - 3 = 5x + 12
- 8x - 3 represents the first fleet's capacity
- 5x + 12 represents the second fleet's capacity

Step 1: Subtract 5x from both sides:
   8x - 5x - 3 = 5x - 5x + 12
   3x - 3 = 12

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   3x - 3 + 3 = 12 + 3
   3x = 15

Step 3: Divide by 3:
   3x/3 = 15/3
   x = 5

Result: Each truck has a capacity of 5 tons.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any fleet management:
- A delivery service has 10 vans with x packages minus 4 returns, equals 6 vans plus 16 packages. How many per van? 10x - 4 = 6x + 16 → 4x = 20 → x = 5
- A bus company has 12 buses with x passengers minus 7 empty seats, equals 8 buses plus 13 passengers. How many per bus? 12x - 7 = 8x + 13 → 4x = 20 → x = 5"}

- x = the production rate (what we want to find)

- 5 = the rate for the second run
- 12 = the units gained in the second run

- 8x - 3 represents the first run's output
- 5x + 12 represents the second run's output

   8x - 5x - 3 = 5x - 5x + 12
   3x - 3 = 12

Step 2: Add 3 to both sides:
   3x - 3 + 3 = 12 + 3
   3x = 15

Step 3: Divide by 3:
   3x/3 = 15/3
   x = 5

Result: Equal output at rate x = 5.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any production planning:

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Distributing First){note:"Solve: 2(x+3) = 3x - 4

Step 1: The equation is 2(x+3) = 3x - 4.
Step 2: Distribute on the left:
    2x + 6 = 3x - 4
Step 3: Subtract 2x from both sides:
    2x - 2x + 6 = 3x - 2x - 4
    6 = x - 4
Step 4: Add 4 to both sides:
    10 = x
Step 5: Check: 2(10+3)=2×13=26; 3×10-4=30-4=26 ✓

Answer: x = 10"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Retail Pricing){note:"Practical Application: Finding the original price when a discount is applied.

Scenario: A store offers a deal: 2 times (price + $3) equals 3 times price minus $4. What is the price?

Variables:
- x = the price (what we want to find)
- 2 = the multiplier for the first expression
- 3 = the added amount in the first expression
- 3 = the multiplier for the second expression
- 4 = the subtracted amount in the second expression

The equation: 2(x+3) = 3x - 4
- 2(x+3) represents the first price expression
- 3x - 4 represents the second price expression

Step 1: Distribute:
   2x + 6 = 3x - 4

Step 2: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   2x - 2x + 6 = 3x - 2x - 4
   6 = x - 4

Step 3: Add 4 to both sides:
   6 + 4 = x - 4 + 4
   10 = x

Result: The price is $10.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any retail pricing:
- A store sells a product: 3(x + $5) = 4x - $2. What is x? 3x + 15 = 4x - 2 → 17 = x → x = 17
- A restaurant: 5(x + $2) = 4x + $15. What is x? 5x + 10 = 4x + 15 → x = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Employee Scheduling){note:"Practical Application: Finding the hours per shift when total hours are given.

- x = the base hours per shift (what we want to find)
- 2 = the number of shifts in the first schedule
- 3 = the extra hours in the first schedule
- 3 = the number of shifts in the second schedule
- 4 = the hours deducted in the second schedule

The equation: 2(x+3) = 3x - 4
- 2(x+3) represents the first schedule's total hours
- 3x - 4 represents the second schedule's total hours

Step 1: Distribute:
   2x + 6 = 3x - 4

Step 2: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   2x - 2x + 6 = 3x - 2x - 4
   6 = x - 4

Step 3: Add 4 to both sides:
   6 + 4 = x - 4 + 4
   10 = x

Result: The base hours per shift is 10.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any scheduling:
- A project: 4 weeks of (x+5) hours = 3 weeks of x hours + 30 hours. What is x? 4x + 20 = 3x + 30 → x = 10
- A conference: 5 days of (x+2) sessions = 4 days of x sessions + 10 sessions. What is x? 5x + 10 = 4x + 10 → x = 0"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Identity - Infinite Solutions){note:"Solve: 2x + 6 = 2(x + 3)

Step 1: The equation is 2x + 6 = 2(x + 3).
Step 2: Distribute on the right:
    2x + 6 = 2x + 6
Step 3: Subtract 2x from both sides:
    2x - 2x + 6 = 2x - 2x + 6
    6 = 6
Step 4: This is a true statement for all values of x.
Answer: Infinitely many solutions (identity)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Identical Business Costs){note:"Practical Application: Two businesses have identical cost structures. They are equal for any quantity, representing identical pricing plans.

Scenario: Business A: 2x + 6, Business B: 2(x+3). Both simplify to 2x+6.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (any number)
- 2 = the cost per unit
- 6 = the fixed cost

The equation: 2x + 6 = 2(x+3)
- 2x + 6 represents Business A's cost
- 2(x+3) represents Business B's cost

Step 1: Distribute on the right:
   2x + 6 = 2x + 6

Step 2: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   2x - 2x + 6 = 2x - 2x + 6
   6 = 6

Result: This is true for any value of x.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any identical pricing:
- Two stores have same pricing: 3x + 9 = 3(x+3). Identical for any x.
- Two rental companies: 4x + 8 = 4(x+2). Equal for all x.
- Two service providers: 5x + 15 = 5(x+3). Equal for all x."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Infinite Solutions){note:"Practical Application: A mathematical identity means any value of x works. This happens when both sides are equivalent expressions.

Scenario: A store's total cost formula: 2x + 4 = 2(x+2). Any quantity gives same total.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (any number)
- 2 = the cost per unit
- 4 = the fixed cost

The equation: 2x + 4 = 2(x+2)
- 2x + 4 represents one cost formula
- 2(x+2) represents another cost formula

Step 1: Distribute on the right:
   2x + 4 = 2x + 4

Step 2: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   2x - 2x + 4 = 2x - 2x + 4
   4 = 4

Result: This is true for any x.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any equivalent expressions:
- A salary calculation: 3x + 12 = 3(x+4). Works for any x.
- A measurement formula: 6x + 18 = 6(x+3). Any value works."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Equivalent Expressions){note:"Practical Application: Equivalent expressions like 2x+6 and 2(x+3) are always equal. This is useful for simplifying and checking work.

Scenario: An algebraic manipulation: 4x + 8 = 4(x+2). These are equivalent expressions.

Variables:
- x = the variable (any value)
- 4 = the coefficient
- 8 = the constant

The equation: 4x + 8 = 4(x+2)
- 4x + 8 represents the expanded form
- 4(x+2) represents the factored form

Step 1: Distribute on the right:
   4x + 8 = 4x + 8

Step 2: Subtract 4x from both sides:
   4x - 4x + 8 = 4x - 4x + 8
   8 = 8

Result: This is true for any x.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any equivalent expressions:
- A formula rewrite: 3x + 9 = 3(x+3). Equivalent forms.
- A distribution check: 5x + 15 = 5(x+3). Both sides represent same quantity."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]Example 6 (Contradiction - No Solution){note:"Solve: 2x + 5 = 2x + 10

Step 1: The equation is 2x + 5 = 2x + 10.
Step 2: Subtract 2x from both sides:
    2x - 2x + 5 = 2x - 2x + 10
    5 = 10
Step 3: This is a false statement.
Answer: No solution (contradiction)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Conflicting Pricing){note:"Practical Application: Two pricing plans where one always costs $5 more than the other regardless of quantity. They will never be equal.

Scenario: Plan A: 2x + 5, Plan B: 2x + 10. No quantity makes them equal.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (any number)
- 2 = the cost per unit (same for both)
- 5 = the fixed cost for Plan A
- 10 = the fixed cost for Plan B

The equation: 2x + 5 = 2x + 10
- 2x + 5 represents Plan A's cost
- 2x + 10 represents Plan B's cost

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   2x - 2x + 5 = 2x - 2x + 10
   5 = 10

Step 2: This is false, so no solution.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any conflicting pricing:
- Two tax calculations: 0.08x + 20 = 0.08x + 30. No amount gives equal tax.
- Two interest formulas: 0.05x + 100 = 0.05x + 200. Never equal.
- Two cost functions: 3x + 7 = 3x + 12. No quantity balances them."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]Example 7 (Fractions){note:"Solve: (2x+1)/3 = (x+4)/2

Step 1: The equation is (2x+1)/3 = (x+4)/2.
Step 2: Cross multiply:
    2(2x+1) = 3(x+4)
Step 3: Distribute:
    4x + 2 = 3x + 12
Step 4: Subtract 3x from both sides:
    4x - 3x + 2 = 3x - 3x + 12
    x + 2 = 12
Step 5: Subtract 2 from both sides:
    x = 10
Step 6: Check: (2×10+1)/3 = 21/3 = 7; (10+4)/2 = 14/2 = 7 ✓

Answer: x = 10"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Proportional Relationships){note:"Practical Application: A mixture problem where two proportions are equal.

Scenario: A recipe has a ratio of (2x+1) to 3 equals (x+4) to 2. What is x?

Variables:
- x = the unknown quantity (what we want to find)
- 2 = the numerator coefficient in the first ratio
- 1 = the constant in the first ratio
- 3 = the denominator in the first ratio
- 1 = the coefficient in the second ratio
- 4 = the constant in the second ratio
- 2 = the denominator in the second ratio

The equation: (2x+1)/3 = (x+4)/2

Step 1: Cross multiply:
   2(2x+1) = 3(x+4)

Step 2: Distribute:
   4x + 2 = 3x + 12

Step 3: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   4x - 3x + 2 = 3x - 3x + 12
   x + 2 = 12

Step 4: Subtract 2 from both sides:
   x + 2 - 2 = 12 - 2
   x = 10

Result: x = 10.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any proportional relationship:
- Two mixtures: (3x+2)/4 = (2x+5)/3. What is x? 3(3x+2) = 4(2x+5) → 9x+6 = 8x+20 → x = 14
- Two concentrations: (4x+3)/5 = (3x+2)/4. What is x? 4(4x+3) = 5(3x+2) → 16x+12 = 15x+10 → x = -2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Ratio Problems){note:"Practical Application: A ratio problem where a fraction of a number plus 1 over 3 equals the number plus 4 over 2.

Scenario: If a fraction of a number plus 1 over 3 equals the number plus 4 over 2, what is the number?

Variables:
- x = the unknown number (what we want to find)
- 2 = the numerator coefficient
- 1 = the constant
- 3 = the first denominator
- 1 = the coefficient
- 4 = the constant
- 2 = the second denominator

The equation: (2x+1)/3 = (x+4)/2

Step 1: Cross multiply:
   2(2x+1) = 3(x+4)

Step 2: Distribute:
   4x + 2 = 3x + 12

Step 3: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   4x - 3x + 2 = 3x - 3x + 12
   x + 2 = 12

Step 4: Subtract 2 from both sides:
   x + 2 - 2 = 12 - 2
   x = 10

Result: The number is 10.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any ratio problem:
- A ratio problem: (3x+5)/4 = (2x-1)/3. What is x? 3(3x+5) = 4(2x-1) → 9x+15 = 8x-4 → x = -19
- A proportion: (2x+3)/5 = (x-1)/2. What is x? 2(2x+3) = 5(x-1) → 4x+6 = 5x-5 → x = 11"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Scaling Problems){note:"Practical Application: In a recipe scaling problem, when (2x+1)/3 = (x+4)/2, x=10 represents the scaling factor.

- x = the scaling factor (what we want to find)
- 2 = the numerator coefficient
- 1 = the constant
- 3 = the denominator
- 1 = the coefficient
- 4 = the constant
- 2 = the denominator

The equation: (2x+1)/3 = (x+4)/2

Step 1: Cross multiply:
   2(2x+1) = 3(x+4)

Step 2: Distribute:
   4x + 2 = 3x + 12

Step 3: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   4x - 3x + 2 = 3x - 3x + 12
   x + 2 = 12

   x + 2 - 2 = 12 - 2
   x = 10

Result: The scaling factor is 10.

- A map scaling: (3x+2)/4 = (x+5)/2. What is x? 2(3x+2) = 4(x+5) → 6x+4 = 4x+20 → 2x = 16 → x = 8

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]Literal Equations{note:"Literal equations are equations that contain more than one variable. They are solved for one variable in terms of the others. Also called formulas. Examples: A = lw (area of rectangle), d = rt (distance), F = (9/5)C + 32 (temperature conversion).

Key strategy:
1. Identify the variable to solve for
2. Use inverse operations to isolate it
3. Treat other variables as constants
4. The result is an expression in terms of the other variables"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of literal equations solved for different variables."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Area of Rectangle){note:"Solve A = lw for l (length)

Step 1: The equation is A = lw.
Step 2: To isolate l, divide both sides by w:
    A/w = (lw)/w
Step 3: Simplify:
    l = A/w

Answer: l = A/w"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Carpentry){note:"Practical Application: Finding the length of a rectangular board when you know its area and width.

Scenario: A carpenter has a rectangular board with area 120 sq ft and width 10 ft. What is the length?

Variables:
- l = the length (what we want to find)
- A = the area (120 sq ft)
- w = the width (10 ft)

The formula: A = lw
- A represents the area
- l represents the length
- w represents the width

Step 1: Solve the formula for l by dividing both sides by w:
   l = A/w

Step 2: Substitute the known values:
   l = 120/10

Step 3: Simplify:
   l = 12

Result: The length is 12 ft.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any rectangular area calculation:
- A garden has area 200 sq ft and length 25 ft, width = 200/25 = 8 ft
- A carpet covers 180 sq ft with length 15 ft, width = 180/15 = 12 ft
- A field has area 1,200 sq ft and width 30 ft, length = 1200/30 = 40 ft"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Interior Design){note:"Practical Application: Finding the width of a rectangular room when you know its area and length.

Scenario: A rectangular room has area 48 sq ft and length 12 ft. What is the width?

Variables:
- w = the width (what we want to find)
- A = the area (48 sq ft)
- l = the length (12 ft)

The formula: A = lw
- A represents the area
- l represents the length
- w represents the width

Step 1: Solve the formula for w by dividing both sides by l:
   w = A/l

Step 2: Substitute the known values:
   w = 48/12

Step 3: Simplify:
   w = 4

Result: The width is 4 ft.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any interior design:
- A rectangular rug has area 72 sq ft and length 12 ft, width = 72/12 = 6 ft
- A rectangular floor has area 150 sq ft and width 10 ft, length = 150/10 = 15 ft
- A rectangular table has area 36 sq ft and length 9 ft, width = 36/9 = 4 ft"}
