lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Distance Formula){note:"Solve d = rt for t (time)

Step 1: The equation is d = rt.
Step 2: To isolate t, divide both sides by r:
    d/r = (rt)/r
Step 3: Simplify:
    t = d/r

Answer: t = d/r"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Temperature Conversion){note:"Solve F = (9/5)C + 32 for C

Step 1: The equation is F = (9/5)C + 32.
Step 2: Subtract 32 from both sides:
    F - 32 = (9/5)C
Step 3: Multiply both sides by 5/9:
    (5/9)(F - 32) = (5/9)×(9/5)C
Step 4: Simplify:
    C = (5/9)(F - 32)

Answer: C = (5/9)(F - 32)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Weather Conversion){note:"Practical Application: Converting Fahrenheit to Celsius for weather.

Scenario: A weather forecast says it will be 68°F. What is this in Celsius?

Variables:
- C = the temperature in Celsius (what we want to find)
- F = the temperature in Fahrenheit (68°F)

The formula: F = (9/5)C + 32
- F represents Fahrenheit
- C represents Celsius

Step 1: Solve the formula for C:
   C = (5/9)(F - 32)

Step 2: Substitute the known value:
   C = (5/9)(68 - 32)

Step 3: Simplify:
   C = (5/9)(36)
   C = 20

Result: 68°F is 20°C.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any temperature conversion:
- Convert 86°F to Celsius: C = (5/9)(86-32) = (5/9)×54 = 30°C
- Convert 50°F to Celsius: C = (5/9)(50-32) = (5/9)×18 = 10°C
- Convert 104°F to Celsius: C = (5/9)(104-32) = (5/9)×72 = 40°C"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Linear Regression){note:"Practical Application: Finding the y-intercept of a line given a point and slope.

Scenario: A line passes through the point (2, 5) with slope 3. What is the y-intercept?

Variables:
- b = the y-intercept (what we want to find)
- y = the y-coordinate of the point (5)
- m = the slope (3)
- x = the x-coordinate of the point (2)

The formula: y = mx + b
- y represents the y-coordinate
- m represents the slope
- x represents the x-coordinate
- b represents the y-intercept

Step 1: Solve the formula for b:
   b = y - mx

Step 2: Substitute the known values:
   b = 5 - 3×2

Step 3: Simplify:
   b = 5 - 6
   b = -1

Result: The y-intercept is -1.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any linear regression:
- A line passes through (4, 10) with slope 2, y-intercept = 10 - 2×4 = 2
- A line passes through (3, 8) with slope -1, y-intercept = 8 - (-1)×3 = 11
- A line passes through (5, 12) with slope 0.5, y-intercept = 12 - 0.5×5 = 9.5"}

Variables:
- P = the present value (what we want to find)
- A = the future value ($10,000)
- r = the interest rate (6% = 0.06)
- t = the time (5 years)

   P = A/(1 + r)^t

Step 2: Substitute the known values:
   P = 10000/(1 + 0.06)^5

Step 3: Simplify:
   P = 10000/(1.06)^5

Step 4: Calculate:
   P = 10000/1.3382
   P = 7472.58

Result: You need to invest $7,472.58 today.

Step 2: To isolate h, divide both sides by πr²:

    h = V/(πr²)

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[1]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Tank Design){note:"Practical Application: Finding the height of a cylindrical tank given its volume and radius.

- V = the volume (1 m³)
- r = the radius (0.5 m)
- π ≈ 3.14159

The formula: V = πr²h

Step 1: Solve for h:
   h = V/(πr²)

Step 2: Substitute the known values:
   h = 1/(π×0.5²)

Step 3: Simplify:
   h = 1/(π×0.25)

Step 4: Calculate:
   h = 1/0.7854
   h = 1.273

Result: The height is 1.273 m.

Real-world application: This equation applies to any tank design:
- A cylindrical tank has volume 500 L and radius 0.4 m, height = 0.5/(π×0.16) = 0.995 m
- A cylindrical silo has volume 2000 m³ and radius 5 m, height = 2000/(π×25) = 25.46 m
- A cylindrical pipe has volume 3 m³ and radius 0.3 m, height = 3/(π×0.09) = 10.61 m"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]Linear Inequalities{note:"Linear inequalities are statements that compare two expressions using <, >, ≤, or ≥. Solving inequalities is similar to solving equations, but with one key difference: when multiplying or dividing by a negative number, the inequality sign reverses.

Key properties:
- Addition and subtraction: same as equations
- Multiplication and division by positive: same as equations
- Multiplication and division by negative: reverse the inequality sign
- The solution is typically a range of values (interval)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]Solving Inequalities{note:"Solving inequalities involves isolating the variable using inverse operations. The solution is an interval or set of values. The key difference from equations is that the inequality sign must be flipped when multiplying or dividing by a negative number.

Key steps:
1. Treat like an equation (use inverse operations)
2. Flip the inequality sign when multiplying/dividing by a negative
3. Express the solution in interval notation or on a number line
4. Check by testing a value in the solution set"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of solving inequalities."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Less Than){note:"Solve: 2x + 3 < 13

Step 1: The inequality is 2x + 3 < 13.
Step 2: Subtract 3 from both sides:
    2x < 10
Step 3: Divide both sides by 2 (positive, no flip):
    x < 5
Step 4: Solution: x < 5 or (-∞, 5)
Check: x=4 → 2×4+3=11 < 13 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Budgeting){note:"Practical Application: A budget constraint where spending must be below a maximum.

Scenario: You have $13 and spend $3 on fees. Each item costs $2. How many items can you buy?

Variables:
- x = the number of items (what we want to find)
- 2 = the cost per item
- 3 = the fees
- 13 = the total budget

The inequality: 2x + 3 < 13
- 2x represents the cost of x items
- 3 represents the fees
- 13 represents the budget

Step 1: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   2x + 3 - 3 < 13 - 3
   2x < 10

Step 2: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 < 10/2
   x < 5

Result: You can buy fewer than 5 items (4 or fewer).

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any budget constraint:
- A budget of $25 with $5 tax, each item costs $2. How many items? 2x + 5 ≤ 25 → 2x ≤ 20 → x ≤ 10
- A $50 gift card with $2 delivery, each item costs $3. How many items? 3x + 2 ≤ 50 → 3x ≤ 48 → x ≤ 16
- A monthly budget of $100 with $15 base, each kWh costs $0.50. How many kWh? 0.5x + 15 ≤ 100 → 0.5x ≤ 85 → x ≤ 170"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Time Management){note:"Practical Application: A time constraint where total hours must be less than a maximum.

Scenario: You have 13 hours total for a project. Setup takes 3 hours, and each task takes 2 hours. How many tasks can you complete?

Variables:
- x = the number of tasks (what we want to find)
- 2 = the hours per task
- 3 = the setup hours
- 13 = the total hours available

The inequality: 2x + 3 < 13
- 2x represents the hours for x tasks
- 3 represents the setup hours
- 13 represents the total hours

Step 1: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   2x + 3 - 3 < 13 - 3
   2x < 10

Step 2: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 < 10/2
   x < 5

Result: You can complete fewer than 5 tasks (4 or fewer).

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any time management:
- You have 10 hours, 1 hour for breaks, each subject takes 1.5 hours. How many subjects? 1.5x + 1 ≤ 10 → 1.5x ≤ 9 → x ≤ 6
- A team has 40 hours, 5 hours for meetings, each task takes 3 hours. How many tasks? 3x + 5 ≤ 40 → 3x ≤ 35 → x ≤ 11.67"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Resource Allocation){note:"Practical Application: A resource constraint where usage must be below capacity.

Scenario: A warehouse has 13 units of storage capacity. Aisles take 3 units, and each pallet uses 2 units. How many pallets can fit?

Variables:
- x = the number of pallets (what we want to find)
- 2 = the units per pallet
- 3 = the units for aisles
- 13 = the total storage capacity

The inequality: 2x + 3 < 13
- 2x represents the storage for x pallets
- 3 represents the aisles
- 13 represents the capacity

Step 1: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   2x + 3 - 3 < 13 - 3
   2x < 10

Step 2: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 < 10/2
   x < 5

Result: Fewer than 5 pallets (4 or fewer) can fit.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any resource allocation:
- A budget of $200, $25 delivery, each item costs $15. How many items? 15x + 25 ≤ 200 → 15x ≤ 175 → x ≤ 11.67
- A truck has 1000 lb capacity, 50 lb for driver, each box weighs 25 lb. How many boxes? 25x + 50 ≤ 1000 → 25x ≤ 950 → x ≤ 38"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Greater Than or Equal){note:"Solve: 3y - 7 ≥ 8

Step 1: The inequality is 3y - 7 ≥ 8.
Step 2: Add 7 to both sides:
    3y ≥ 15
Step 3: Divide both sides by 3:
    y ≥ 5
Step 4: Solution: y ≥ 5 or [5, ∞)
Check: y=6 → 3×6-7=18-7=11 ≥ 8 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Minimum Requirements){note:"Practical Application: A minimum score requirement where points must be at least a certain value.

Scenario: A student needs at least 8 points to pass. They have 7 penalty points, and each correct answer is worth 3 points. How many correct answers are needed?

Variables:
- y = the number of correct answers (what we want to find)
- 3 = the points per correct answer
- 7 = the penalty points
- 8 = the minimum points needed

The inequality: 3y - 7 ≥ 8
- 3y represents the points from correct answers
- 7 represents the penalty
- 8 represents the minimum points

Step 1: Add 7 to both sides:
   3y - 7 + 7 ≥ 8 + 7
   3y ≥ 15

Step 2: Divide both sides by 3:
   3y/3 ≥ 15/3
   y ≥ 5

Result: At least 5 correct answers are needed.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any minimum requirement:
- A project needs at least 80% completion. Base is 20%, each phase is 15%. How many phases? 15x + 20 ≥ 80 → 15x ≥ 60 → x ≥ 4
- A driver needs at least 100 points. Base is 30, each race gives 25. How many races? 25x + 30 ≥ 100 → 25x ≥ 70 → x ≥ 2.8 (need 3 races)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Production Goals){note:"Practical Application: A production target where output must meet or exceed a goal.

Scenario: A factory needs at least 8 units of production per day. Regular production is 7 units, and each machine produces 3 units. How many machines are needed?

Variables:
- y = the number of machines (what we want to find)
- 3 = the units per machine
- 7 = the regular production
- 8 = the minimum units needed

The inequality: 3y + 7 ≥ 8
- 3y represents the units from machines
- 7 represents the regular production
- 8 represents the minimum needed

Step 1: Subtract 7 from both sides:
   3y + 7 - 7 ≥ 8 - 7
   3y ≥ 1

Step 2: Divide both sides by 3:
   3y/3 ≥ 1/3
   y ≥ 1/3

Result: At least 1 machine is needed.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any production goal:
- A team needs at least 15 sales. Base is 7, each rep makes 2. How many reps? 2x + 7 ≥ 15 → 2x ≥ 8 → x ≥ 4
- A farm needs at least 20 tons. Base is 6, each field yields 3.5 tons. How many fields? 3.5x + 6 ≥ 20 → 3.5x ≥ 14 → x ≥ 4"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Quality Control){note:"Practical Application: A quality standard where score must be at least a minimum.

Scenario: A product must score at least 8 in quality testing. A base score is 7, and each component adds 3 points. How many components are needed?

Variables:
- y = the number of components (what we want to find)
- 3 = the points per component
- 7 = the base score
- 8 = the minimum score

The inequality: 3y + 7 ≥ 8
- 3y represents the points from components
- 7 represents the base score
- 8 represents the minimum

Step 1: Subtract 7 from both sides:
   3y + 7 - 7 ≥ 8 - 7
   3y ≥ 1

Step 2: Divide both sides by 3:
   3y/3 ≥ 1/3
   y ≥ 1/3

Result: At least 1 component is needed.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any quality control:
- A product needs at least 90%. Base is 20%, each component adds 15%. How many components? 15x + 20 ≥ 90 → 15x ≥ 70 → x ≥ 4.67 (need 5)
- A service needs at least 85%. Base is 15%, each feature adds 10%. How many features? 10x + 15 ≥ 85 → 10x ≥ 70 → x ≥ 7"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Negative Multiplication){note:"Solve: 5 - 2z > 11

Step 1: The inequality is 5 - 2z > 11.
Step 2: Subtract 5 from both sides:
    -2z > 6
Step 3: Divide both sides by -2 (negative, flip sign):
    z < -3
Step 4: Solution: z < -3 or (-∞, -3)
Check: z=-4 → 5-2(-4)=5+8=13 > 11 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Temperature Constraints){note:"Practical Application: A temperature constraint where the condition requires the variable to be less than a value.

Scenario: A chemical reaction requires the temperature condition 5 - 2T > 11 to proceed. What must the temperature be?

Variables:
- T = the temperature (what we want to find)
- 2 = the coefficient
- 5 = the constant
- 11 = the comparison value

The inequality: 5 - 2T > 11

Step 1: Subtract 5 from both sides:
   5 - 2T - 5 > 11 - 5
   -2T > 6

Step 2: Divide both sides by -2 (flip the inequality sign):
   (-2T)/(-2) < 6/(-2)
   T < -3

Result: The temperature must be less than -3°C.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any temperature constraint:
- A chemical process requires 10 - 3T > 5. Temperature must be T < 5/3
- A storage condition requires 8 - 4T > 2. Temperature must be T < 1.5
- A reaction requires 12 - 5T > 7. Temperature must be T < 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Financial Loss){note:"Practical Application: A business condition where profit must exceed a threshold.

Scenario: A company's profit is 5 - 2x. This must be greater than 11 for the company to be profitable. What must x be?

Variables:
- x = the debt (what we want to find)
- 2 = the debt multiplier
- 5 = the revenue
- 11 = the profit threshold

The inequality: 5 - 2x > 11

Step 1: Subtract 5 from both sides:
   5 - 2x - 5 > 11 - 5
   -2x > 6

Step 2: Divide both sides by -2 (flip sign):
   (-2x)/(-2) < 6/(-2)
   x < -3

Result: Debt must be less than -3 (meaning the company has credit).

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any financial condition:
- A company's profit: 10 - 3x > 4. Debt must be x < 2
- A store's loss: 7 - 2x > 1. x < 3
- A bank's balance: 6 - 5x > 1. x < 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Inventory Management){note:"Practical Application: An inventory condition where remaining stock must be greater than a threshold.

Scenario: A warehouse has 5 - 2x items. If this must be greater than 11, what must x be?

Variables:
- x = the number of days (what we want to find)
- 2 = the items used per day
- 5 = the starting inventory
- 11 = the minimum required inventory

The inequality: 5 - 2x > 11

Step 1: Subtract 5 from both sides:
   5 - 2x - 5 > 11 - 5
   -2x > 6

Step 2: Divide both sides by -2 (flip sign):
   (-2x)/(-2) < 6/(-2)
   x < -3

Result: Days must be less than -3 (impossible, meaning stock is adequate).

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any inventory condition:
- A warehouse has 20 - 3x > 5 units. x < 5 days
- A factory has 30 - 4x > 10 items. x < 5 days
- A store has 25 - 5x > 0 items. x < 5 days"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Less Than or Equal){note:"Solve: 4x + 1 ≤ 9

Step 1: The inequality is 4x + 1 ≤ 9.
Step 2: Subtract 1 from both sides:
    4x ≤ 8
Step 3: Divide both sides by 4:
    x ≤ 2
Step 4: Solution: x ≤ 2 or (-∞, 2]
Check: x=1 → 4×1+1=5 ≤ 9 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Shopping Budget){note:"Practical Application: A budget constraint where cost must be at most a maximum.

Scenario: A store charges $4 per item plus $1 shipping. The budget is $9. How many items can you buy?

Variables:
- x = the number of items (what we want to find)
- 4 = the cost per item
- 1 = the shipping
- 9 = the budget

The inequality: 4x + 1 ≤ 9
- 4x represents the cost of x items
- 1 represents the shipping
- 9 represents the budget

Step 1: Subtract 1 from both sides:
   4x + 1 - 1 ≤ 9 - 1
   4x ≤ 8

Step 2: Divide both sides by 4:
   4x/4 ≤ 8/4
   x ≤ 2

Result: You can buy at most 2 items.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any shopping budget:
- 3 items plus $2 shipping, budget $14: 3x + 2 ≤ 14 → 3x ≤ 12 → x ≤ 4
- 5 items plus $5 delivery, budget $30: 5x + 5 ≤ 30 → 5x ≤ 25 → x ≤ 5
- 6 meals plus $8 tax, budget $50: 6x + 8 ≤ 50 → 6x ≤ 42 → x ≤ 7"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Weight Capacity){note:"Practical Application: A weight capacity constraint where total weight must be at most a maximum.

- 4 = the weight per item
- 1 = the weight of the box
- 9 = the maximum weight

The inequality: 4x + 1 ≤ 9
- 4x represents the weight of x items
- 1 represents the box weight
- 9 represents the maximum

Step 1: Subtract 1 from both sides:
   4x + 1 - 1 ≤ 9 - 1
   4x ≤ 8

Step 2: Divide both sides by 4:
   4x/4 ≤ 8/4
   x ≤ 2

Result: At most 2 items can fit.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any weight capacity:
- A suitcase: 3x + 2 ≤ 20 → 3x ≤ 18 → x ≤ 6
- A truck: 100x + 200 ≤ 1000 → 100x ≤ 800 → x ≤ 8

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Multiple Steps){note:"Solve: 5x - 2 < 3x + 8

Step 1: The inequality is 5x - 2 < 3x + 8.
Step 2: Subtract 3x from both sides:
    5x - 3x - 2 < 3x - 3x + 8
    2x - 2 < 8
Step 3: Add 2 to both sides:
    2x < 10
Step 4: Divide both sides by 2:
    x < 5
Step 5: Solution: x < 5 or (-∞, 5)
Check: x=4 → 5×4-2=18; 3×4+8=20; 18<20 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Business Pricing){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two pricing plans to find when one is cheaper.

Scenario: Plan A: 5x - 2, Plan B: 3x + 8. Plan A is cheaper when x is less than what value?

Variables:
- x = the quantity (what we want to find)
- 5 = the rate for Plan A
- 2 = the discount for Plan A
- 3 = the rate for Plan B
- 8 = the fixed cost for Plan B

The inequality: 5x - 2 < 3x + 8
- 5x - 2 represents Plan A's cost
- 3x + 8 represents Plan B's cost

Step 1: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   5x - 3x - 2 < 3x - 3x + 8
   2x - 2 < 8

Step 2: Add 2 to both sides:
   2x - 2 + 2 < 8 + 2
   2x < 10

Step 3: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 < 10/2
   x < 5

Result: Plan A is cheaper when x < 5.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any business pricing:
- Plan A: 7x - 3, Plan B: 4x + 9. A is cheaper when x < 4 (7x-3 < 4x+9 → 3x < 12 → x < 4)
- Plan A: 6x - 5, Plan B: 2x + 15. A is cheaper when x < 5 (6x-5 < 2x+15 → 4x < 20 → x < 5)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Cost Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two suppliers to find when one is cheaper.

Scenario: Supplier A: 5x - 2, Supplier B: 3x + 8. Supplier A is cheaper when x is less than what value?

Variables:
- x = the quantity (what we want to find)
- 5 = the rate for Supplier A
- 2 = the discount for Supplier A
- 3 = the rate for Supplier B
- 8 = the fixed cost for Supplier B

The inequality: 5x - 2 < 3x + 8

Step 1: Subtract 3x from both sides:
   5x - 3x - 2 < 3x - 3x + 8
   2x - 2 < 8

Step 2: Add 2 to both sides:
   2x - 2 + 2 < 8 + 2
   2x < 10

Step 3: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 < 10/2
   x < 5

Result: Supplier A is cheaper when x < 5.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any cost analysis:
- Supplier A: 4x - 3, Supplier B: 2x + 9. A is cheaper for x < 6 (4x-3 < 2x+9 → 2x < 12 → x < 6)
- Supplier A: 6x - 4, Supplier B: 3x + 11. A is cheaper for x < 5 (6x-4 < 3x+11 → 3x < 15 → x < 5)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Profit Comparison){note:"Practical Application: Comparing two products to find when one is more profitable.

Scenario: Product X: 6x - 4, Product Y: 2x + 12. Product X is more profitable when x is greater than what value?

Variables:
- x = the quantity (what we want to find)
- 6 = the rate for Product X
- 4 = the cost for Product X
- 2 = the rate for Product Y
- 12 = the cost for Product Y

The inequality: 6x - 4 > 2x + 12
- 6x - 4 represents Product X's profit
- 2x + 12 represents Product Y's profit

Step 1: Subtract 2x from both sides:
   6x - 2x - 4 > 2x - 2x + 12
   4x - 4 > 12

Step 2: Add 4 to both sides:
   4x - 4 + 4 > 12 + 4
   4x > 16

Step 3: Divide both sides by 4:
   4x/4 > 16/4
   x > 4

Result: Product X is more profitable when x > 4.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any profit comparison:
- Product X: 4x - 2, Product Y: x + 10. X is more profitable for x > 4 (4x-2 > x+10 → 3x > 12 → x > 4)
- Product X: 7x - 5, Product Y: 4x + 7. X is more profitable for x > 4 (7x-5 > 4x+7 → 3x > 12 → x > 4)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]Example 6 (Fractions){note:"Solve: (2x+1)/3 > 5

Step 1: The inequality is (2x+1)/3 > 5.
Step 2: Multiply both sides by 3:
    2x + 1 > 15
Step 3: Subtract 1 from both sides:
    2x > 14
Step 4: Divide both sides by 2:
    x > 7
Step 5: Solution: x > 7 or (7, ∞)
Check: x=8 → (2×8+1)/3 = 17/3 ≈ 5.67 > 5 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Average Requirements){note:"Practical Application: An average score requirement where the average must exceed a threshold.

Scenario: A student's average score is (2x+1)/3. This must exceed 5 to pass. What must x be?

Variables:
- x = the raw score (what we want to find)
- 2 = the coefficient
- 1 = the constant
- 3 = the divisor
- 5 = the minimum average

The inequality: (2x+1)/3 > 5

Step 1: Multiply both sides by 3:
   (2x+1)/3 × 3 > 5 × 3
   2x + 1 > 15

Step 2: Subtract 1 from both sides:
   2x + 1 - 1 > 15 - 1
   2x > 14

Step 3: Divide both sides by 2:
   2x/2 > 14/2
   x > 7

Result: The raw score must be greater than 7.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any average requirement:
- A student's average: (3x+2)/4 > 8. x > 10 (3x+2 > 32 → 3x > 30 → x > 10)
- A factory's production rate: (4x+3)/5 > 7. x > 8 (4x+3 > 35 → 4x > 32 → x > 8)
- A team's performance: (2x+5)/3 > 6. x > 6.5 (2x+5 > 18 → 2x > 13 → x > 6.5)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Temperature Range){note:"Practical Application: A temperature constraint where the variable must be at least a minimum.

Scenario: A chemical reaction requires 3 - 4x ≤ 15 to proceed. What must x be?

Variables:
- x = the temperature (what we want to find)
- 4 = the coefficient
- 3 = the constant
- 15 = the maximum value

The inequality: 3 - 4x ≤ 15

Step 1: Subtract 3 from both sides:
   3 - 4x - 3 ≤ 15 - 3
   -4x ≤ 12

Step 2: Divide both sides by -4 (flip sign):
   (-4x)/(-4) ≥ 12/(-4)
   x ≥ -3

Result: The temperature must be at least -3°C.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any temperature constraint:
- A chemical reaction requires 5 - 3x ≤ 20. x ≥ -5 (5-3x ≤ 20 → -3x ≤ 15 → x ≥ -5)
- A storage condition requires 8 - 2x ≤ 12. x ≥ -2 (8-2x ≤ 12 → -2x ≤ 4 → x ≥ -2)
- A manufacturing process requires 10 - 5x ≤ 15. x ≥ -1 (10-5x ≤ 15 → -5x ≤ 5 → x ≥ -1)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Financial Limits){note:"Practical Application: A budget constraint where a variable must be at least a minimum.

Scenario: A budget constraint: 3 - 4x ≤ 15. What must x be?

Variables:
- x = the amount (what we want to find)
- 4 = the coefficient
- 3 = the constant
- 15 = the maximum

The inequality: 3 - 4x ≤ 15

Step 1: Subtract 3:
   3 - 4x - 3 ≤ 15 - 3
   -4x ≤ 12

Step 2: Divide by -4 (flip sign):
   (-4x)/(-4) ≥ 12/(-4)
   x ≥ -3

Result: The amount must be at least -3.

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any financial limit:
- A company's revenue: 10 - 3x ≤ 25. x ≥ -5 (10-3x ≤ 25 → -3x ≤ 15 → x ≥ -5)
- A bank's balance: 6 - 2x ≤ 10. x ≥ -2 (6-2x ≤ 10 → -2x ≤ 4 → x ≥ -2)
- A store's profit: 8 - 5x ≤ 13. x ≥ -1 (8-5x ≤ 13 → -5x ≤ 5 → x ≥ -1)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Inventory Management){note:"Practical Application: An inventory constraint where stock must be at least a minimum.

Scenario: A warehouse has 3 - 4x ≤ 15 units. What must x be?

Variables:
- x = the number of days (what we want to find)
- 4 = the usage per day
- 3 = the starting inventory
- 15 = the maximum stock

The inequality: 3 - 4x ≤ 15

Step 1: Subtract 3:
   3 - 4x - 3 ≤ 15 - 3
   -4x ≤ 12

Step 2: Divide by -4 (flip sign):
   (-4x)/(-4) ≥ 12/(-4)
   x ≥ -3

Result: Days must be at least -3 (meaning stock is adequate).

Real-world application: This inequality applies to any inventory constraint:
- A store has 20 - 3x ≤ 35 items. x ≥ -5 (20-3x ≤ 35 → -3x ≤ 15 → x ≥ -5)
- A factory has 30 - 4x ≤ 50 units. x ≥ -5 (30-4x ≤ 50 → -4x ≤ 20 → x ≥ -5)
- A warehouse has 25 - 5x ≤ 40 items. x ≥ -3 (25-5x ≤ 40 → -5x ≤ 15 → x ≥ -3)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[2]Graphing Inequalities on Number Lines{note:"Graphing inequalities on a number line visually represents the solution set. The graph shows all values that satisfy the inequality.

Key rules:
- Open circle (○) for < or > (not included)
- Closed circle (●) for ≤ or ≥ (included)
- Arrow to the right for > or ≥
- Arrow to the left for < or ≤"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of graphing inequalities on number lines."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (x > 3){note:"Graph: x > 3

Step 1: Open circle at 3 (not included).
Step 2: Arrow to the right (all numbers greater than 3).
Step 3: The graph shows all values > 3.
Interval notation: (3, ∞)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[3]Compound Inequalities{note:"Compound inequalities combine two inequalities using 'and' (intersection) or 'or' (union). They can be written as a single statement like a < x < b or as separate statements connected by 'and' or 'or'.

Key types:
- 'and': x must satisfy both inequalities (overlapping region)
- 'or': x must satisfy at least one inequality (union of regions)
- Double inequality: a < x < b means a < x and x < b"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of compound inequalities."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 ('and' Inequality){note:"Solve: -3 < 2x + 1 < 7

Step 1: The inequality is -3 < 2x + 1 < 7.
Step 2: Subtract 1 from all three parts:
    -3 - 1 < 2x + 1 - 1 < 7 - 1
    -4 < 2x < 6
Step 3: Divide all three parts by 2:
    -2 < x < 3
Step 4: Solution: -2 < x < 3 or (-2, 3)
Check: x=0 → -3 < 1 < 7 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Temperature Control){note:"Practical Application: Temperature must be between -3 and 7 after adjusting for a 1-degree offset. Original temperature range is -2 to 3.

Scenario: A thermostat maintains temperature between -3 and 7°C with a 1°C offset.
Equation: -3 < 2x + 1 < 7 → -2 < x < 3

A chemical process requires -5 < 3x + 2 < 10. The range is -7/3 < x < 8/3.
Equation: -5 < 3x + 2 < 10 → -7 < 3x < 8 → -7/3 < x < 8/3

A manufacturing process requires -4 < 2x - 1 < 9. The range is -3/2 < x < 5.
Equation: -4 < 2x - 1 < 9 → -3 < 2x < 10 → -3/2 < x < 5"}

Equation: -5 < 2x + 3 < 7 → -8 < 2x < 4 → -4 < x < 2

Equation: -6 < 3x - 2 < 10 → -4 < 3x < 12 → -4/3 < x < 4

Equation: -8 < 4x + 1 < 9 → -9 < 4x < 8 → -9/4 < x < 2"}

    Inequality 2: 2x + 1 < 9
    2x < 8
    x < 4

Step 2: Solution is x > 3 or x < 4.
Step 3: This is all real numbers (since 3 < 4, the union covers everything).
Step 4: Solution: all real numbers or (-∞, ∞)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]Absolute Value Inequalities{note:"Absolute value inequalities involve expressions inside absolute value bars. The solution depends on the inequality symbol.

Key rules:
- |x| < a → -a < x < a (and)
- |x| ≤ a → -a ≤ x ≤ a (and)
- |x| > a → x < -a or x > a (or)
- |x| ≥ a → x ≤ -a or x ≥ a (or)
- For |ax + b|, isolate the absolute value first"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of absolute value inequalities."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Less Than){note:"Solve: |x| < 3

Step 1: The inequality is |x| < 3.
Step 2: This means -3 < x < 3.
Step 3: Solution: -3 < x < 3 or (-3, 3)
Check: x=0 → |0| < 3 ✓; x=4 → |4| < 3 false"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Linear Expression){note:"Solve: |x - 3| < 2

Step 1: The inequality is |x - 3| < 2.
Step 2: This means -2 < x - 3 < 2.
Step 3: Add 3 to all parts:
    -2 + 3 < x - 3 + 3 < 2 + 3
    1 < x < 5
Step 4: Solution: 1 < x < 5 or (1, 5)
Check: x=2 → |2-3|=1 < 2 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Temperature Tolerance){note:"Practical Application: The temperature deviation from 3°C must be less than 2°C. Acceptable range is 1°C to 5°C.

A machine operates within 2°C of 3°C.
Equation: |x - 3| < 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Quality Control){note:"Practical Application: Product weight must be within 2 grams of the target weight of 3 grams.

Scenario: A scale must read within 2 units of 3.
Equation: |x - 3| < 2

A measurement must be within 2 of 3.
Equation: |x - 3| < 2

A tolerance is 2 units from the target of 3.
Equation: |x - 3| < 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Process Control){note:"Practical Application: A manufacturing process must maintain the value within 2 units of the set point 3.

Scenario: A control system keeps x within 2 of 3.
Equation: |x - 3| < 2

A feedback loop maintains x within 2 of 3.
Equation: |x - 3| < 2

A quality check requires x within 2 of 3.
Equation: |x - 3| < 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]Example 6 (Linear Expression with Greater Than){note:"Solve: |2x + 1| ≥ 5

Step 1: The inequality is |2x + 1| ≥ 5.
Step 2: This means 2x + 1 ≤ -5 or 2x + 1 ≥ 5.
Step 3: Solve each:
    2x + 1 ≤ -5 → 2x ≤ -6 → x ≤ -3
    2x + 1 ≥ 5 → 2x ≥ 4 → x ≥ 2
Step 4: Solution: x ≤ -3 or x ≥ 2 or (-∞, -3] ∪ [2, ∞)
Check: x=-4 → |-8+1|=7 ≥ 5 ✓; x=3 → |6+1|=7 ≥ 5 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Deviation Tolerance){note:"Practical Application: The deviation of 2x+1 from zero must be at least 5 units. x must be ≤ -3 or ≥ 2.

Scenario: A machine's output 2x+1 must be at least 5 units from zero.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5

A process requires 2x+1 to deviate at least 5 units.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5

A measurement of 2x+1 must be at least 5 away.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Separation Requirement){note:"Practical Application: Two quantities must be separated by at least 5 units. The expression 2x+1 must be ≥5 or ≤-5.

Scenario: Two items must be at least 5 units apart: 2x+1 ≥ 5 or ≤ -5.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5

A gap must be at least 5 units: |2x+1| ≥ 5.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5

A distance must be at least 5: |2x+1| ≥ 5.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Safety Margin){note:"Practical Application: A safety margin requires the value 2x+1 to be at least 5 units away from zero.

Scenario: A safety system requires |2x+1| ≥ 5.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5

A clearance requires |2x+1| ≥ 5.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5

A tolerance requires |2x+1| ≥ 5.
Equation: |2x+1| ≥ 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]Example 7 (Special Cases){note:"Solve: |x| < -2

Step 1: The inequality is |x| < -2.
Step 2: Absolute value is always non-negative, so it cannot be less than -2.
Step 3: Solution: No solution
Solve: |x| > -3

Step 1: The inequality is |x| > -3.
Step 2: Absolute value is always ≥ 0, so it is always > -3.
Step 3: Solution: All real numbers"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Impossible Constraints){note:"Practical Application: Some constraints are impossible (like distance less than -2) or always satisfied (like distance greater than -3).

Scenario: A condition requiring |x| < -2 is impossible.
Equation: |x| < -2 → No solution

A condition requiring |x| > -3 is always true.
Equation: |x| > -3 → All real numbers

A measurement error less than -2 units is impossible.
Equation: |x| < -2 → No solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Always True){note:"Practical Application: Some absolute value inequalities are always true because absolute value is always non-negative.

Scenario: |x| > -3 is always true.
Equation: |x| > -3 → All real numbers

|x| + 2 > 0 is always true.
Equation: |x| + 2 > 0 → All real numbers

|x| ≥ -1 is always true.
Equation: |x| ≥ -1 → All real numbers"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[2]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[7]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Never True){note:"Practical Application: Some absolute value inequalities are never true because absolute value is always non-negative.

Scenario: |x| < -5 is never true.
Equation: |x| < -5 → No solution

|x| + 2 < 0 is never true.
Equation: |x| + 2 < 0 → No solution

|x| ≤ -1 is never true.
Equation: |x| ≤ -1 → No solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]Systems of Linear Equations{note:"A system of linear equations is a set of two or more linear equations with the same variables. The solution is the point(s) that satisfy all equations simultaneously. Systems can have one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions.

Key methods:
- Substitution method
- Elimination method
- Graphical method

Types of systems:
- Consistent and independent: one solution (lines intersect)
- Consistent and dependent: infinitely many solutions (same line)
- Inconsistent: no solution (parallel lines)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]Substitution Method{note:"The substitution method solves a system by solving one equation for one variable and substituting into the other equation.

Key steps:
1. Solve one equation for one variable
2. Substitute into the other equation
3. Solve for the remaining variable
4. Back-substitute to find the other variable
5. Check the solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of the substitution method."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Basic){note:"Solve: x + y = 10 and x - y = 4

Step 1: Solve the second equation for x:
    x - y = 4 → x = y + 4
Step 2: Substitute into the first equation:
    (y + 4) + y = 10
    2y + 4 = 10
    2y = 6
    y = 3
Step 3: Back-substitute: x = 3 + 4 = 7
Step 4: Check: 7 + 3 = 10 ✓; 7 - 3 = 4 ✓
Answer: (7, 3)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Pricing Comparison){note:"Practical Application: A store sells two items. The total price of one of each is $10, and one costs $4 more than the other. The items cost $7 and $3.

Scenario: A restaurant sells two combos. Combo A + Combo B = $25, and Combo A is $5 more than Combo B. What are the prices?
Equation: x + y = 25, x - y = 5 → x = 15, y = 10

A store sells two products. Total price is $50, one costs $10 more. What are the prices?
Equation: x + y = 50, x - y = 10 → x = 30, y = 20

A movie theater sells adult and child tickets. Two adult + one child = $35, adult is $5 more than child. What are the prices?
Equation: 2x + y = 35, x - y = 5 → x = 10 (child), y = 15 (adult)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Break-Even){note:"Practical Application: A business has two products. Total sales are $10, and one product sells 4 more units than the other.

Scenario: Two departments have total employees 10, one has 4 more than the other.
Equation: x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → x = 7, y = 3

Two teams have total points 10, one has 4 more points than the other.
Equation: x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → x = 7, y = 3

Two stores have total revenue $10, one has $4 more than the other.
Equation: x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → x = 7, y = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Classroom Distribution){note:"Practical Application: There are 10 students in two classes. One class has 4 more students than the other.

Scenario: Two groups have total 10 items, one group has 4 more.
Equation: x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → x = 7, y = 3

Two teams have total 10 players, one team has 4 more.
Equation: x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → x = 7, y = 3

Two boxes have total 10 items, one box has 4 more.
Equation: x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → x = 7, y = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (One Variable Isolated){note:"Solve: y = 2x + 5 and 3x - y = 10

Step 1: y is already isolated: y = 2x + 5.
Step 2: Substitute into the second equation:
    3x - (2x + 5) = 10
    3x - 2x - 5 = 10
    x - 5 = 10
    x = 15
Step 3: Back-substitute: y = 2×15 + 5 = 35
Step 4: Check: 3×15 - 35 = 45 - 35 = 10 ✓
Answer: (15, 35)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Cost Function){note:"Practical Application: A company's cost function is y = 2x + 5 (where x is units and y is cost). Revenue is 3x - y = 10. Break-even at x=15 units, y=$35.

Scenario: A business has cost y = 3x + 4 and revenue 5x - y = 8. Break-even?
Equation: y = 3x + 4, 5x - y = 8 → 5x - (3x+4) = 8 → 2x = 12 → x = 6, y = 22

A product has cost y = 4x + 2 and revenue 6x - y = 10. Break-even?
Equation: y = 4x + 2, 6x - y = 10 → 6x - (4x+2) = 10 → 2x = 12 → x = 6, y = 26

A service has cost y = 5x + 3 and revenue 7x - y = 12. Break-even?
Equation: y = 5x + 3, 7x - y = 12 → 7x - (5x+3) = 12 → 2x = 15 → x = 7.5, y = 40.5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Supply and Demand){note:"Practical Application: Supply function y = 2x + 5, demand function 3x - y = 10. Equilibrium at x=15, y=35.

Scenario: Supply: y = 3x + 4, Demand: 5x - y = 8. Equilibrium?
Equation: y = 3x + 4, 5x - y = 8 → 5x - (3x+4) = 8 → 2x = 12 → x = 6, y = 22

Supply: y = 4x + 2, Demand: 6x - y = 10. Equilibrium?
Equation: y = 4x + 2, 6x - y = 10 → 6x - (4x+2) = 10 → 2x = 12 → x = 6, y = 26

Supply: y = 5x + 3, Demand: 7x - y = 12. Equilibrium?
Equation: y = 5x + 3, 7x - y = 12 → 7x - (5x+3) = 12 → 2x = 15 → x = 7.5, y = 40.5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Profit Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Profit function y = 2x + 5, where x is units sold. Revenue constraint 3x - y = 10. Break-even at 15 units.

Scenario: Profit = 3x + 4, Revenue = 5x - y = 8. Break-even?
Equation: y = 3x + 4, 5x - y = 8 → 5x - (3x+4) = 8 → 2x = 12 → x = 6, y = 22

Profit = 4x + 2, Revenue = 6x - y = 10. Break-even?
Equation: y = 4x + 2, 6x - y = 10 → 6x - (4x+2) = 10 → 2x = 12 → x = 6, y = 26

Profit = 5x + 3, Revenue = 7x - y = 12. Break-even?
Equation: y = 5x + 3, 7x - y = 12 → 7x - (5x+3) = 12 → 2x = 15 → x = 7.5, y = 40.5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Fractions){note:"Solve: x/2 + y/3 = 2 and x - y = 6

Step 1: Solve the second equation for x:
    x = y + 6
Step 2: Substitute into the first equation:
    (y + 6)/2 + y/3 = 2
Step 3: Multiply by 6 (LCD):
    3(y + 6) + 2y = 12
    3y + 18 + 2y = 12
    5y + 18 = 12
    5y = -6
    y = -6/5
Step 4: Back-substitute: x = -6/5 + 6 = (-6+30)/5 = 24/5
Step 5: Check: 24/5 - (-6/5) = 30/5 = 6 ✓
Answer: (24/5, -6/5)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Mixture Problem){note:"Practical Application: A mixture problem: half of quantity x plus a third of quantity y equals 2, and x is 6 more than y. The quantities are 4.8 and -1.2.

Scenario: A chemical mixture: x/3 + y/4 = 3, x - y = 8. What are x and y?
Equation: x/3 + y/4 = 3, x - y = 8 → x = y + 8, (y+8)/3 + y/4 = 3 → 4y+32+3y=36 → 7y=4 → y=4/7, x=60/7

A financial portfolio: x/4 + y/5 = 6, x - y = 10. What are x and y?
Equation: x/4 + y/5 = 6, x - y = 10 → x = y + 10, (y+10)/4 + y/5 = 6 → 5y+50+4y=120 → 9y=70 → y=70/9, x=160/9

A manufacturing process: x/5 + y/6 = 4, x - y = 12. What are x and y?
Equation: x/5 + y/6 = 4, x - y = 12 → x = y + 12, (y+12)/5 + y/6 = 4 → 6y+72+5y=120 → 11y=48 → y=48/11, x=180/11"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Recipe Scaling){note:"Practical Application: A recipe uses half of ingredient x plus a third of ingredient y for 2 servings, and x is 6 more than y.

Scenario: A cake recipe: x/2 + y/3 = 2, x - y = 6. Quantities are x=4.8, y=-1.2 (not realistic, but shows method).

A construction plan: x/3 + y/4 = 5, x - y = 8. What are x and y?
Equation: x/3 + y/4 = 5, x - y = 8 → x = y + 8, (y+8)/3 + y/4 = 5 → 4y+32+3y=60 → 7y=28 → y=4, x=12

A budget allocation: x/4 + y/5 = 6, x - y = 10. What are x and y?
Equation: x/4 + y/5 = 6, x - y = 10 → x = y + 10, (y+10)/4 + y/5 = 6 → 5y+50+4y=120 → 9y=70 → y=70/9, x=160/9"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Resource Allocation){note:"Practical Application: Resources x and y are allocated such that x/2 + y/3 = 2 and x is 6 more than y.

Scenario: A project uses x/3 + y/4 = 5 and x - y = 8. Allocation: x=12, y=4.
Equation: x/3 + y/4 = 5, x - y = 8 → x = y + 8, (y+8)/3 + y/4 = 5 → 4y+32+3y=60 → 7y=28 → y=4, x=12

A team uses x/4 + y/5 = 6 and x - y = 10. Allocation: x=160/9, y=70/9.
Equation: x/4 + y/5 = 6, x - y = 10 → x = y + 10, (y+10)/4 + y/5 = 6 → 5y+50+4y=120 → 9y=70 → y=70/9, x=160/9

A budget uses x/5 + y/6 = 4 and x - y = 12. Allocation: x=180/11, y=48/11.
Equation: x/5 + y/6 = 4, x - y = 12 → x = y + 12, (y+12)/5 + y/6 = 4 → 6y+72+5y=120 → 11y=48 → y=48/11, x=180/11"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (No Solution - Inconsistent){note:"Solve: y = 2x + 3 and y = 2x - 5

Step 1: y is already isolated in both equations.
Step 2: Set them equal:
    2x + 3 = 2x - 5
    2x - 2x = -5 - 3
    0 = -8
Step 3: This is a contradiction.
Answer: No solution (inconsistent system)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Parallel Costs){note:"Practical Application: Two companies have the same variable cost ($2 per unit) but different fixed costs ($3 vs -$5). They will never have the same total cost.

Scenario: Company A: y = 3x + 4, Company B: y = 3x - 2. Never equal.
Equation: 3x + 4 = 3x - 2 → 4 = -2 (false)

A: y = 4x + 5, B: y = 4x + 1. Never equal.
Equation: 4x + 5 = 4x + 1 → 5 = 1 (false)

A: y = 2x + 7, B: y = 2x - 3. Never equal.
Equation: 2x + 7 = 2x - 3 → 7 = -3 (false)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Parallel Supply/Demand){note:"Practical Application: Two supply lines with same slope but different intercepts will never intersect.

Scenario: Supply 1: y = 2x + 3, Supply 2: y = 2x - 5. No equilibrium.
Equation: 2x + 3 = 2x - 5 → 3 = -5 (false)

Demand 1: y = -x + 4, Demand 2: y = -x + 1. No intersection.
Equation: -x + 4 = -x + 1 → 4 = 1 (false)

Cost 1: y = 5x + 2, Cost 2: y = 5x - 3. No equal cost.
Equation: 5x + 2 = 5x - 3 → 2 = -3 (false)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Conflicting Strategies){note:"Practical Application: Two business strategies have the same variable cost but different fixed costs, so they never break even.

Scenario: Strategy A: y = 3x + 5, Strategy B: y = 3x + 1. Never equal.
Equation: 3x + 5 = 3x + 1 → 5 = 1 (false)

Plan A: y = 4x + 6, Plan B: y = 4x + 2. Never equal.
Equation: 4x + 6 = 4x + 2 → 6 = 2 (false)

Option 1: y = 2x + 8, Option 2: y = 2x - 2. Never equal.
Equation: 2x + 8 = 2x - 2 → 8 = -2 (false)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Infinitely Many - Dependent){note:"Solve: 2x + 3y = 6 and 4x + 6y = 12

Step 1: Solve the first equation for y:
    2x + 3y = 6 → 3y = -2x + 6 → y = (-2/3)x + 2
Step 2: Substitute into the second equation:
    4x + 6[(-2/3)x + 2] = 12
    4x + (-4x + 12) = 12
    12 = 12
Step 3: This is an identity.
Answer: Infinitely many solutions (dependent system)
The equations represent the same line."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Identical Cost Structures){note:"Practical Application: Two different expressions for the same cost structure. They are identical for all quantities.

Scenario: Company A: 3x + 2y = 6, Company B: 6x + 4y = 12. Same line.
Equation: 3x + 2y = 6, 6x + 4y = 12 → 2(3x+2y)=12 → 3x+2y=6 (same)

A: 2x + 3y = 8, B: 4x + 6y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 4x + 6y = 16 → 2(2x+3y)=16 → 2x+3y=8 (same)

A: x + 2y = 5, B: 2x + 4y = 10. Same line.
Equation: 2x + 4y = 10 → 2(x+2y)=10 → x+2y=5 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Equivalent Relationships){note:"Practical Application: Two equations representing the same relationship between variables.

Scenario: Relationship 1: 2x + 3y = 6, Relationship 2: 4x + 6y = 12. Same line.
Equation: 4x + 6y = 12 → 2(2x+3y)=12 → 2x+3y=6 (same)

Relation 1: 3x + 2y = 8, Relation 2: 6x + 4y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 6x + 4y = 16 → 2(3x+2y)=16 → 3x+2y=8 (same)

Relation 1: x + 3y = 4, Relation 2: 2x + 6y = 8. Same line.
Equation: 2x + 6y = 8 → 2(x+3y)=8 → x+3y=4 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Scaled Equations){note:"Practical Application: Two equations where one is a scalar multiple of the other represent the same line.

Scenario: Eq1: 2x + 3y = 6, Eq2: 4x + 6y = 12 (multiply Eq1 by 2).
Equation: 4x + 6y = 12 → 2(2x+3y)=12 → 2x+3y=6 (same)

Eq1: x + 2y = 5, Eq2: 3x + 6y = 15 (multiply Eq1 by 3).
Equation: 3x + 6y = 15 → 3(x+2y)=15 → x+2y=5 (same)

Eq1: 2x - y = 3, Eq2: 6x - 3y = 9 (multiply Eq1 by 3).
Equation: 6x - 3y = 9 → 3(2x-y)=9 → 2x-y=3 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]Example 6 (Three Variables){note:"Solve: x + y + z = 6, x - y + z = 2, 2x + y - z = 3

Step 1: Solve the first equation for x:
    x = 6 - y - z
Step 2: Substitute into the second and third equations:
    (6 - y - z) - y + z = 2 → 6 - 2y = 2 → -2y = -4 → y = 2
    2(6 - y - z) + y - z = 3 → 12 - 2y - 2z + y - z = 3
    → 12 - y - 3z = 3 → -y - 3z = -9
Step 3: Substitute y = 2:
    -2 - 3z = -9 → -3z = -7 → z = 7/3
Step 4: Back-substitute: x = 6 - 2 - 7/3 = 4 - 7/3 = (12-7)/3 = 5/3
Answer: (5/3, 2, 7/3)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Resource Allocation){note:"Practical Application: Three people share costs totaling $6. The differences in their shares give the solution.

Scenario: Three departments share a budget of $6. Dept A is $2 more than Dept C, and Dept B is $3 less than Dept A. What are the budgets?
Equation: A + B + C = 6, A - B = 2, B - C = 1 → A=3, B=1, C=2

Three products total cost $6. Product A is $2 more than B, and C is $1 less than B.
Equation: A + B + C = 6, A - B = 2, B - C = 1 → A=3, B=1, C=2

Three ingredients total 6 cups. Flour is 2 cups more than sugar, and milk is 1 cup less than sugar.
Equation: F + S + M = 6, F - S = 2, S - M = 1 → F=3, S=1, M=2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Financial Distribution){note:"Practical Application: Three people share costs totaling $6. Person 1 pays $2 more than Person 2, and Person 3 pays $1 less than Person 2.

Scenario: Three investors put in $6 total. Investor 1 puts in $2 more than Investor 2, and Investor 3 puts in $1 less than Investor 2.
Equation: I1 + I2 + I3 = 6, I1 - I2 = 2, I2 - I3 = 1 → I1=3, I2=1, I3=2

Three departments total $6. Dept A is $2 more than Dept B, Dept B is $1 more than Dept C.
Equation: A + B + C = 6, A - B = 2, B - C = 1 → A=3, B=1, C=2

Three accounts total $6. Account 1 is $2 more than Account 2, Account 2 is $1 less than Account 3.
Equation: A1 + A2 + A3 = 6, A1 - A2 = 2, A3 - A2 = 1 → A1=3, A2=1, A3=2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Inventory Distribution){note:"Practical Application: Three warehouses total 6 units. Warehouse 1 has 2 more units than Warehouse 2, and Warehouse 3 has 1 less than Warehouse 2.

Scenario: Three stores total 6 items. Store A has 2 more than Store B, Store C has 1 less than Store B.
Equation: A + B + C = 6, A - B = 2, B - C = 1 → A=3, B=1, C=2

Three boxes total 6 items. Box 1 has 2 more than Box 2, Box 3 has 1 less than Box 2.
Equation: B1 + B2 + B3 = 6, B1 - B2 = 2, B2 - B3 = 1 → B1=3, B2=1, B3=2

Three shelves total 6 books. Shelf A has 2 more than Shelf B, Shelf C has 1 less than Shelf B.
Equation: A + B + C = 6, A - B = 2, B - C = 1 → A=3, B=1, C=2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]Elimination Method{note:"The elimination method solves a system by adding or subtracting equations to eliminate one variable. Also called the addition method.

Key steps:
1. Multiply one or both equations to make coefficients of one variable opposites
2. Add the equations to eliminate that variable
3. Solve for the remaining variable
4. Back-substitute to find the other variable
5. Check the solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of the elimination method."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Already Opposites){note:"Solve: 2x + 3y = 7 and x - 3y = -4

Step 1: Notice the y terms have opposite coefficients (+3 and -3).
Step 2: Add the equations:
    (2x + 3y) + (x - 3y) = 7 + (-4)
    3x = 3
    x = 1
Step 3: Back-substitute into either equation:
    2(1) + 3y = 7 → 2 + 3y = 7 → 3y = 5 → y = 5/3
Step 4: Check: 2(1)+3(5/3)=2+5=7 ✓; 1-3(5/3)=1-5=-4 ✓
Answer: (1, 5/3)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Supply and Demand){note:"Practical Application: Two equations representing supply and demand where the y coefficients cancel.

Scenario: Supply: 2x + 3y = 7, Demand: x - 3y = -4. Equilibrium at x=1, y=5/3.

A market: 3x + 2y = 8, 2x - 2y = 4. What is equilibrium?
Equation: 3x + 2y = 8, 2x - 2y = 4 → 5x = 12 → x = 12/5, y = ?

A store: 4x + 5y = 10, 3x - 5y = 2. What is solution?
Equation: 4x + 5y = 10, 3x - 5y = 2 → 7x = 12 → x = 12/7

A business: 5x + 2y = 11, 4x - 2y = 3. What is solution?
Equation: 5x + 2y = 11, 4x - 2y = 3 → 9x = 14 → x = 14/9"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Chemical Mixture){note:"Practical Application: Two chemical mixtures with opposite coefficients for one component.

Scenario: Mixture A: 2x + 3y = 7, Mixture B: x - 3y = -4. Solve for concentrations.

A solution: 3x + 4y = 10, 2x - 4y = 2. What are x and y?
Equation: 3x + 4y = 10, 2x - 4y = 2 → 5x = 12 → x = 12/5

A blend: 4x + 5y = 12, 3x - 5y = 4. What are x and y?
Equation: 4x + 5y = 12, 3x - 5y = 4 → 7x = 16 → x = 16/7

A mixture: 5x + 2y = 9, 4x - 2y = 3. What are x and y?
Equation: 5x + 2y = 9, 4x - 2y = 3 → 9x = 12 → x = 4/3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: Two equations with opposite coefficients appear in physics force balance problems.

Scenario: Force balance: 2x + 3y = 7, x - 3y = -4. Solve for forces.

Circuit equations: 3I1 + 4I2 = 10, 2I1 - 4I2 = 2. Find currents.
Equation: 3I1 + 4I2 = 10, 2I1 - 4I2 = 2 → 5I1 = 12 → I1 = 12/5

Motion equations: 4v + 5a = 12, 3v - 5a = 4. Find v and a.
Equation: 4v + 5a = 12, 3v - 5a = 4 → 7v = 16 → v = 16/7

Energy equations: 5E + 2F = 9, 4E - 2F = 3. Find E and F.
Equation: 5E + 2F = 9, 4E - 2F = 3 → 9E = 12 → E = 4/3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Multiply One Equation){note:"Solve: 3x + 2y = 11 and x - 3y = -8

Step 1: Multiply the second equation by -3 to eliminate x:
    -3(x - 3y) = -3(-8) → -3x + 9y = 24
Step 2: Add to the first equation:
    (3x + 2y) + (-3x + 9y) = 11 + 24
    11y = 35
    y = 35/11
Step 3: Back-substitute: 3x + 2(35/11) = 11
    3x + 70/11 = 11 → 3x = 121/11 - 70/11 = 51/11 → x = 17/11
Answer: (17/11, 35/11)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Business Equations){note:"Practical Application: Two business equations where multiplying one equation helps eliminate a variable.

Scenario: A company: 3x + 2y = 11, x - 3y = -8. Solve for equilibrium.

A store: 4x + 3y = 13, 2x - 3y = 1. What are x and y?
Equation: 4x + 3y = 13, 2x - 3y = 1 → 6x = 14 → x = 7/3

A factory: 5x + 2y = 14, 3x - 2y = 2. What are x and y?
Equation: 5x + 2y = 14, 3x - 2y = 2 → 8x = 16 → x = 2

A restaurant: 2x + 3y = 10, 4x - 3y = 2. What are x and y?
Equation: 2x + 3y = 10, 4x - 3y = 2 → 6x = 12 → x = 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Economic Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Economic equilibrium where multiplying one equation eliminates a variable.

Scenario: Demand: 3x + 2y = 11, Supply: x - 3y = -8. Equilibrium at (17/11, 35/11).

Market: 4x + 3y = 13, 2x - 3y = 1. Equilibrium at (7/3, 11/9?).
Equation: 4x + 3y = 13, 2x - 3y = 1 → 6x = 14 → x = 7/3

Trade: 5x + 2y = 14, 3x - 2y = 2. Equilibrium at (2, 2).
Equation: 5x + 2y = 14, 3x - 2y = 2 → 8x = 16 → x = 2

Investment: 2x + 3y = 10, 4x - 3y = 2. Equilibrium at (2, 2).
Equation: 2x + 3y = 10, 4x - 3y = 2 → 6x = 12 → x = 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Engineering Systems){note:"Practical Application: Engineering systems where eliminating a variable simplifies the solution.

Scenario: Structural: 3x + 2y = 11, x - 3y = -8. Solve for forces.

Electrical: 4I1 + 3I2 = 13, 2I1 - 3I2 = 1. Find currents.
Equation: 4I1 + 3I2 = 13, 2I1 - 3I2 = 1 → 6I1 = 14 → I1 = 7/3

Mechanical: 5F1 + 2F2 = 14, 3F1 - 2F2 = 2. Find forces.
Equation: 5F1 + 2F2 = 14, 3F1 - 2F2 = 2 → 8F1 = 16 → F1 = 2

Thermal: 2T1 + 3T2 = 10, 4T1 - 3T2 = 2. Find temperatures.
Equation: 2T1 + 3T2 = 10, 4T1 - 3T2 = 2 → 6T1 = 12 → T1 = 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Multiply Both Equations){note:"Solve: 2x + 3y = 8 and 3x + 4y = 11

Step 1: Multiply first by 3 and second by -2:
    3(2x + 3y) = 3(8) → 6x + 9y = 24
    -2(3x + 4y) = -2(11) → -6x - 8y = -22
Step 2: Add the equations:
    (6x + 9y) + (-6x - 8y) = 24 + (-22)
    y = 2
Step 3: Back-substitute: 2x + 3(2) = 8 → 2x + 6 = 8 → 2x = 2 → x = 1
Answer: (1, 2)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Pricing Strategies){note:"Practical Application: Finding the intersection of two lines representing different pricing strategies.

Scenario: Strategy A: 2x + 3y = 8, Strategy B: 3x + 4y = 11. Intersection at (1,2).

Company X: 3x + 2y = 10, Company Y: 2x + 3y = 8. What is intersection?
Equation: 3x + 2y = 10, 2x + 3y = 8 → 9x + 6y = 30, 4x + 6y = 16 → 5x = 14 → x = 14/5

Plan 1: 4x + 3y = 12, Plan 2: 3x + 4y = 11. What is intersection?
Equation: 4x + 3y = 12, 3x + 4y = 11 → 16x + 12y = 48, 9x + 12y = 33 → 7x = 15 → x = 15/7

Option A: 5x + 2y = 9, Option B: 2x + 5y = 12. What is intersection?
Equation: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x + 5y = 12 → 25x + 10y = 45, 4x + 10y = 24 → 21x = 21 → x = 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Optimization){note:"Practical Application: Optimizing two variables by finding their intersection point.

Scenario: Cost minimization: 2x + 3y = 8, 3x + 4y = 11. Optimal at (1,2).

Resource allocation: 3x + 2y = 10, 2x + 3y = 8. Optimal at (14/5, ?).
Equation: 3x + 2y = 10, 2x + 3y = 8 → 9x + 6y = 30, 4x + 6y = 16 → 5x = 14 → x = 14/5

Production planning: 4x + 3y = 12, 3x + 4y = 11. Optimal at (15/7, ?).
Equation: 4x + 3y = 12, 3x + 4y = 11 → 16x + 12y = 48, 9x + 12y = 33 → 7x = 15 → x = 15/7

Budget allocation: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x + 5y = 12. Optimal at (1, 2).
Equation: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x + 5y = 12 → 25x + 10y = 45, 4x + 10y = 24 → 21x = 21 → x = 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Engineering Design){note:"Practical Application: Engineering design where two constraints intersect at the optimal point.

Scenario: Structural constraints: 2x + 3y = 8, 3x + 4y = 11. Design point (1,2).

Material constraints: 3x + 2y = 10, 2x + 3y = 8. Design point (14/5, ?).
Equation: 3x + 2y = 10, 2x + 3y = 8 → 9x + 6y = 30, 4x + 6y = 16 → 5x = 14 → x = 14/5

Safety constraints: 4x + 3y = 12, 3x + 4y = 11. Design point (15/7, ?).
Equation: 4x + 3y = 12, 3x + 4y = 11 → 16x + 12y = 48, 9x + 12y = 33 → 7x = 15 → x = 15/7

Performance constraints: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x + 5y = 12. Design point (1, 2).
Equation: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x + 5y = 12 → 25x + 10y = 45, 4x + 10y = 24 → 21x = 21 → x = 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Eliminate y Instead){note:"Solve: 4x - 3y = 17 and 2x + 5y = 13

Step 1: Multiply first by 5 and second by 3 to eliminate y:
    5(4x - 3y) = 5(17) → 20x - 15y = 85
    3(2x + 5y) = 3(13) → 6x + 15y = 39
Step 2: Add the equations:
    20x - 15y + 6x + 15y = 85 + 39
    26x = 124
    x = 124/26 = 62/13
Step 3: Back-substitute: 4(62/13) - 3y = 17
    248/13 - 3y = 17 → 248/13 - 221/13 = 3y → 27/13 = 3y → y = 9/13
Answer: (62/13, 9/13)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Market Equilibrium){note:"Practical Application: Finding equilibrium in a market where prices and quantities are unknown.

Scenario: Supply: 4x - 3y = 17, Demand: 2x + 5y = 13. Equilibrium at (62/13, 9/13).

Market 1: 5x - 2y = 10, 3x + 4y = 8. What is equilibrium?
Equation: 5x - 2y = 10, 3x + 4y = 8 → 20x - 8y = 40, 6x + 8y = 16 → 26x = 56 → x = 28/13

Market 2: 6x - 3y = 15, 4x + 2y = 6. What is equilibrium?
Equation: 6x - 3y = 15, 4x + 2y = 6 → 12x - 6y = 30, 12x + 6y = 18 → 24x = 48 → x = 2

Market 3: 8x - 4y = 20, 3x + 2y = 7. What is equilibrium?
Equation: 8x - 4y = 20, 3x + 2y = 7 → 16x - 8y = 40, 12x + 8y = 28 → 28x = 68 → x = 17/7"}

Equation: 5x - 2y = 10, 3x + 4y = 8 → 20x - 8y = 40, 6x + 8y = 16 → 26x = 56 → x = 28/13

Chemical C: 6x - 3y = 15, Chemical D: 4x + 2y = 6. Equilibrium at (2, -1?).
Equation: 6x - 3y = 15, 4x + 2y = 6 → 12x - 6y = 30, 12x + 6y = 18 → 24x = 48 → x = 2

Chemical E: 8x - 4y = 20, Chemical F: 3x + 2y = 7. Equilibrium at (17/7, ?).
Equation: 8x - 4y = 20, 3x + 2y = 7 → 16x - 8y = 40, 12x + 8y = 28 → 28x = 68 → x = 17/7"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Economic Planning){note:"Practical Application: Economic planning where two constraints intersect.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (No Solution - Inconsistent){note:"Solve: 2x + 3y = 6 and 4x + 6y = 10

Step 1: Multiply the first equation by -2:
    -2(2x + 3y) = -2(6) → -4x - 6y = -12
Step 2: Add to the second equation:
    (-4x - 6y) + (4x + 6y) = -12 + 10
    0 = -2
Step 3: This is a contradiction.
Answer: No solution (inconsistent system)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Conflicting Costs){note:"Practical Application: Two businesses with proportional costs but different fixed amounts. They never break even together.

Scenario: Company A: 2x + 3y = 6, Company B: 4x + 6y = 10. No solution.

Factory 1: 3x + 2y = 8, Factory 2: 6x + 4y = 18. No solution.
Equation: 3x + 2y = 8, 6x + 4y = 18 → 2(3x+2y)=6x+4y=16, but RHS is 18 → no solution

Store A: 4x + 3y = 12, Store B: 8x + 6y = 28. No solution.
Equation: 4x + 3y = 12, 8x + 6y = 28 → 2(4x+3y)=8x+6y=24, but RHS is 28 → no solution

Plant 1: 5x + 2y = 10, Plant 2: 10x + 4y = 22. No solution.
Equation: 5x + 2y = 10, 10x + 4y = 22 → 2(5x+2y)=10x+4y=20, but RHS is 22 → no solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Parallel Supply/Demand){note:"Practical Application: Two supply/demand lines with same slope but different intercepts.

Scenario: Supply: 2x + 3y = 6, Demand: 4x + 6y = 10. No equilibrium.

Supply 1: 3x + 2y = 8, Supply 2: 6x + 4y = 18. No intersection.
Equation: 3x + 2y = 8, 6x + 4y = 18 → 2(3x+2y)=6x+4y=16, but RHS is 18 → no solution

Demand 1: 4x + 3y = 12, Demand 2: 8x + 6y = 28. No intersection.
Equation: 4x + 3y = 12, 8x + 6y = 28 → 2(4x+3y)=8x+6y=24, but RHS is 28 → no solution

Cost 1: 5x + 2y = 10, Cost 2: 10x + 4y = 22. No intersection.
Equation: 5x + 2y = 10, 10x + 4y = 22 → 2(5x+2y)=10x+4y=20, but RHS is 22 → no solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Inconsistent Planning){note:"Practical Application: Two plans that are inconsistent and cannot be satisfied simultaneously.

Scenario: Plan A: 2x + 3y = 6, Plan B: 4x + 6y = 10. No solution.

Plan 1: 3x + 2y = 8, Plan 2: 6x + 4y = 18. Inconsistent.
Equation: 3x + 2y = 8, 6x + 4y = 18 → 2(3x+2y)=6x+4y=16, but RHS is 18 → no solution

Plan X: 4x + 3y = 12, Plan Y: 8x + 6y = 28. Inconsistent.
Equation: 4x + 3y = 12, 8x + 6y = 28 → 2(4x+3y)=8x+6y=24, but RHS is 28 → no solution

Plan M: 5x + 2y = 10, Plan N: 10x + 4y = 22. Inconsistent.
Equation: 5x + 2y = 10, 10x + 4y = 22 → 2(5x+2y)=10x+4y=20, but RHS is 22 → no solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]Example 6 (Infinitely Many - Dependent){note:"Solve: 2x + 3y = 6 and 4x + 6y = 12

Step 1: Multiply the first equation by -2:
    -2(2x + 3y) = -2(6) → -4x - 6y = -12
Step 2: Add to the second equation:
    (-4x - 6y) + (4x + 6y) = -12 + 12
    0 = 0
Step 3: This is an identity.
Answer: Infinitely many solutions (dependent system)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Identical Cost Structures){note:"Practical Application: Two identical business equations representing the same cost structure.

Scenario: Company A: 2x + 3y = 6, Company B: 4x + 6y = 12. Same line.

Store 1: 3x + 2y = 8, Store 2: 6x + 4y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 6x + 4y = 16 → 2(3x+2y)=16 → 3x+2y=8 (same)

Factory A: 4x + 3y = 12, Factory B: 8x + 6y = 24. Same line.
Equation: 8x + 6y = 24 → 2(4x+3y)=24 → 4x+3y=12 (same)

Plan 1: 5x + 2y = 10, Plan 2: 10x + 4y = 20. Same line.
Equation: 10x + 4y = 20 → 2(5x+2y)=20 → 5x+2y=10 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Equivalent Relationships){note:"Practical Application: Two different expressions for the same relationship between variables.

Scenario: Relationship 1: 2x + 3y = 6, Relationship 2: 4x + 6y = 12. Same line.

Relation 1: 3x + 2y = 8, Relation 2: 6x + 4y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 6x + 4y = 16 → 2(3x+2y)=16 → 3x+2y=8 (same)

Relation 1: 4x + 3y = 12, Relation 2: 8x + 6y = 24. Same line.
Equation: 8x + 6y = 24 → 2(4x+3y)=24 → 4x+3y=12 (same)

Relation 1: 5x + 2y = 10, Relation 2: 10x + 4y = 20. Same line.
Equation: 10x + 4y = 20 → 2(5x+2y)=20 → 5x+2y=10 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[6]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Scaled Systems){note:"Practical Application: Two equations where one is a scalar multiple of the other.

Scenario: Eq1: 2x + 3y = 6, Eq2: 4x + 6y = 12 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.

Eq1: 3x + 2y = 8, Eq2: 6x + 4y = 16 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.
Equation: 6x + 4y = 16 → 2(3x+2y)=16 → 3x+2y=8 (same)

Eq1: 4x + 3y = 12, Eq2: 8x + 6y = 24 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.
Equation: 8x + 6y = 24 → 2(4x+3y)=24 → 4x+3y=12 (same)

Eq1: 5x + 2y = 10, Eq2: 10x + 4y = 20 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.
Equation: 10x + 4y = 20 → 2(5x+2y)=20 → 5x+2y=10 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]Graphical Method{note:"The graphical method solves a system by graphing both equations on the same coordinate plane. The intersection point(s) are the solution(s).

Key steps:
1. Graph each equation (using slope-intercept or intercepts)
2. Identify the intersection point
3. Verify the solution by substituting into both equations

Types of solutions visually:
- Intersecting lines: one solution (consistent, independent)
- Same line: infinitely many solutions (consistent, dependent)
- Parallel lines: no solution (inconsistent)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of the graphical method."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (One Solution){note:"Solve graphically: y = 2x + 1 and y = -x + 4

Step 1: Graph both lines.
Line 1: y = 2x + 1 (slope 2, y-intercept 1)
Line 2: y = -x + 4 (slope -1, y-intercept 4)

Step 2: The lines intersect at (1, 3).
Step 3: Check: 3 = 2(1)+1 ✓; 3 = -1+4 ✓
Answer: (1, 3)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Break-Even Point){note:"Practical Application: Finding the break-even point where two cost functions intersect.

Scenario: Cost A: y = 2x + 1, Cost B: y = -x + 4. Break-even at (1,3).

Company X: y = 3x + 2, Company Y: y = -x + 6. Intersection?
Equation: 3x + 2 = -x + 6 → 4x = 4 → x = 1, y = 5

Store A: y = 4x + 1, Store B: y = -2x + 7. Intersection?
Equation: 4x + 1 = -2x + 7 → 6x = 6 → x = 1, y = 5

Plan 1: y = 5x + 3, Plan 2: y = -3x + 11. Intersection?
Equation: 5x + 3 = -3x + 11 → 8x = 8 → x = 1, y = 8"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Supply and Demand){note:"Practical Application: Supply and demand curves intersecting at market equilibrium.

Scenario: Supply: y = 2x + 1, Demand: y = -x + 4. Equilibrium at (1,3).

Supply: y = 3x + 2, Demand: y = -x + 6. Equilibrium at (1,5).
Equation: 3x + 2 = -x + 6 → 4x = 4 → x = 1, y = 5

Supply: y = 4x + 1, Demand: y = -2x + 7. Equilibrium at (1,5).
Equation: 4x + 1 = -2x + 7 → 6x = 6 → x = 1, y = 5

Supply: y = 5x + 3, Demand: y = -3x + 11. Equilibrium at (1,8).
Equation: 5x + 3 = -3x + 11 → 8x = 8 → x = 1, y = 8"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Profit Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Two profit functions intersect at the break-even point.

Scenario: Profit A: y = 2x + 1, Profit B: y = -x + 4. Break-even at (1,3).

Product X: y = 3x + 2, Product Y: y = -x + 6. Break-even at (1,5).
Equation: 3x + 2 = -x + 6 → 4x = 4 → x = 1, y = 5

Product A: y = 4x + 1, Product B: y = -2x + 7. Break-even at (1,5).
Equation: 4x + 1 = -2x + 7 → 6x = 6 → x = 1, y = 5

Product M: y = 5x + 3, Product N: y = -3x + 11. Break-even at (1,8).
Equation: 5x + 3 = -3x + 11 → 8x = 8 → x = 1, y = 8"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (One Solution with Standard Form){note:"Solve graphically: 2x + y = 4 and x - y = 1

Step 1: Convert to slope-intercept form:
    2x + y = 4 → y = -2x + 4
    x - y = 1 → y = x - 1

Step 2: Graph both lines.
Line 1: y = -2x + 4 (slope -2, y-intercept 4)
Line 2: y = x - 1 (slope 1, y-intercept -1)

Step 3: The lines intersect at (5/3, 2/3).
Step 4: Check: 2(5/3)+2/3 = 10/3+2/3=12/3=4 ✓; 5/3-2/3=3/3=1 ✓
Answer: (5/3, 2/3)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Business Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Finding the intersection of two lines in business analysis.

Scenario: Budget: 2x + y = 4, Sales: x - y = 1. Intersection at (5/3, 2/3).

Cost: 3x + y = 6, Revenue: x - y = 2. Intersection?
Equation: 3x + y = 6, x - y = 2 → 4x = 8 → x = 2, y = 0

Investment: 4x + y = 8, Return: x - y = 2. Intersection?
Equation: 4x + y = 8, x - y = 2 → 5x = 10 → x = 2, y = 0

Expense: 5x + y = 10, Income: x - y = 2. Intersection?
Equation: 5x + y = 10, x - y = 2 → 6x = 12 → x = 2, y = 0"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Engineering Design){note:"Practical Application: Engineering constraints intersect at the design point.

Safety: 3x + y = 6, Performance: x - y = 2. Design point (2,0).
Equation: 3x + y = 6, x - y = 2 → 4x = 8 → x = 2, y = 0

Material: 4x + y = 8, Weight: x - y = 2. Design point (2,0).
Equation: 4x + y = 8, x - y = 2 → 5x = 10 → x = 2, y = 0

Cost: 5x + y = 10, Quality: x - y = 2. Design point (2,0).
Equation: 5x + y = 10, x - y = 2 → 6x = 12 → x = 2, y = 0"}

Step 1: Both lines have slope 2.
Line 1: y = 2x + 3 (y-intercept 3)
Line 2: y = 2x - 1 (y-intercept -1)

Step 2: The lines are parallel and never intersect.
Step 3: Answer: No solution (inconsistent system)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Parallel Business Costs){note:"Practical Application: Two companies with identical variable costs ($2 per unit) but different fixed costs ($3 vs -$1). They never have equal total costs.

Scenario: Company A: y = 2x + 3, Company B: y = 2x - 1. No intersection.

Factory 1: y = 3x + 4, Factory 2: y = 3x - 2. No intersection.
Equation: 3x + 4 = 3x - 2 → 4 = -2 (false)

Store A: y = 4x + 5, Store B: y = 4x + 1. No intersection.
Equation: 4x + 5 = 4x + 1 → 5 = 1 (false)

Plant 1: y = 5x + 6, Plant 2: y = 5x - 3. No intersection.
Equation: 5x + 6 = 5x - 3 → 6 = -3 (false)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Parallel Supply Lines){note:"Practical Application: Two supply lines with same slope but different intercepts.

Scenario: Supply 1: y = 2x + 3, Supply 2: y = 2x - 1. No equilibrium.

Supply A: y = 3x + 4, Supply B: y = 3x - 2. No equilibrium.
Equation: 3x + 4 = 3x - 2 → 4 = -2 (false)

Supply X: y = 4x + 5, Supply Y: y = 4x + 1. No equilibrium.
Equation: 4x + 5 = 4x + 1 → 5 = 1 (false)

Supply M: y = 5x + 6, Supply N: y = 5x - 3. No equilibrium.
Equation: 5x + 6 = 5x - 3 → 6 = -3 (false)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Conflicting Pricing){note:"Practical Application: Two pricing strategies with same variable cost but different fixed costs.

Scenario: Plan A: y = 2x + 3, Plan B: y = 2x - 1. Never equal.

Strategy X: y = 3x + 4, Strategy Y: y = 3x - 2. Never equal.
Equation: 3x + 4 = 3x - 2 → 4 = -2 (false)

Option 1: y = 4x + 5, Option 2: y = 4x + 1. Never equal.
Equation: 4x + 5 = 4x + 1 → 5 = 1 (false)

Plan M: y = 5x + 6, Plan N: y = 5x - 3. Never equal.
Equation: 5x + 6 = 5x - 3 → 6 = -3 (false)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Infinitely Many - Same Line){note:"Solve graphically: 2x + y = 4 and 4x + 2y = 8

Step 1: Simplify the second equation:
    4x + 2y = 8 → 2x + y = 4 (same as first)

Step 2: Both lines are identical.
Step 3: Answer: Infinitely many solutions (dependent system)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Identical Cost Functions){note:"Practical Application: Two different expressions for the same cost function.

Scenario: Cost A: 2x + y = 4, Cost B: 4x + 2y = 8. Same line.

Store 1: 3x + y = 6, Store 2: 6x + 2y = 12. Same line.
Equation: 6x + 2y = 12 → 2(3x+y)=12 → 3x+y=6 (same)

Factory A: 4x + y = 8, Factory B: 8x + 2y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 8x + 2y = 16 → 2(4x+y)=16 → 4x+y=8 (same)

Plan 1: 5x + y = 10, Plan 2: 10x + 2y = 20. Same line.
Equation: 10x + 2y = 20 → 2(5x+y)=20 → 5x+y=10 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Equivalent Equations){note:"Practical Application: Two equations that represent the same line.

Scenario: Eq1: 2x + y = 4, Eq2: 4x + 2y = 8. Same line.

Relation 1: 3x + y = 6, Relation 2: 6x + 2y = 12. Same line.
Equation: 6x + 2y = 12 → 2(3x+y)=12 → 3x+y=6 (same)

Relation A: 4x + y = 8, Relation B: 8x + 2y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 8x + 2y = 16 → 2(4x+y)=16 → 4x+y=8 (same)

Relation X: 5x + y = 10, Relation Y: 10x + 2y = 20. Same line.
Equation: 10x + 2y = 20 → 2(5x+y)=20 → 5x+y=10 (same)"}

Scenario: Plan A: 2x + y = 4, Plan B: 4x + 2y = 8 (multiply Plan A by 2). Same line.

Plan X: 3x + y = 6, Plan Y: 6x + 2y = 12 (multiply Plan X by 2). Same line.
Equation: 6x + 2y = 12 → 2(3x+y)=12 → 3x+y=6 (same)

Plan M: 4x + y = 8, Plan N: 8x + 2y = 16 (multiply Plan M by 2). Same line.
Equation: 8x + 2y = 16 → 2(4x+y)=16 → 4x+y=8 (same)

Plan P: 5x + y = 10, Plan Q: 10x + 2y = 20 (multiply Plan P by 2). Same line.
Equation: 10x + 2y = 20 → 2(5x+y)=20 → 5x+y=10 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]Consistent vs Inconsistent Systems{note:"Consistent systems have at least one solution. Inconsistent systems have no solution.

Definitions:
- Consistent: at least one solution exists
- Inconsistent: no solution exists

Types of consistent systems:
- Consistent and independent: exactly one solution
- Consistent and dependent: infinitely many solutions

How to determine:
- Use any method (substitution, elimination, graphing)
- If you get a true statement (e.g., 3 = 3), consistent dependent
- If you get a false statement (e.g., 3 = 5), inconsistent"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of consistent and inconsistent systems."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Consistent Independent){note:"Solve: 2x + y = 7 and x - y = 5

Step 1: Add the equations:
    2x + y = 7
    x - y = 5
    3x = 12 → x = 4
Step 2: Back-substitute: 4 - y = 5 → y = -1
Step 3: Check: 2(4)+(-1)=8-1=7 ✓; 4-(-1)=5 ✓
Answer: (4, -1) - one solution (consistent independent)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Unique Solution){note:"Practical Application: Unique break-even point where two lines intersect.

Scenario: Profit: 2x + y = 7, Cost: x - y = 5. Intersection at (4,-1).

Revenue: 3x + y = 10, Expenses: x - y = 4. Intersection?
Equation: 3x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → 4x = 14 → x = 3.5, y = -0.5

Sales: 4x + y = 12, Costs: x - y = 3. Intersection?
Equation: 4x + y = 12, x - y = 3 → 5x = 15 → x = 3, y = 0

Supply: 5x + y = 15, Demand: x - y = 2. Intersection?
Equation: 5x + y = 15, x - y = 2 → 6x = 17 → x = 17/6, y = 5/6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Market Equilibrium){note:"Practical Application: Market equilibrium where supply and demand intersect.

Scenario: Supply: 2x + y = 7, Demand: x - y = 5. Equilibrium at (4,-1).

Supply: 3x + y = 10, Demand: x - y = 4. Equilibrium at (3.5, -0.5).
Equation: 3x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → 4x = 14 → x = 3.5, y = -0.5

Supply: 4x + y = 12, Demand: x - y = 3. Equilibrium at (3,0).
Equation: 4x + y = 12, x - y = 3 → 5x = 15 → x = 3, y = 0

Supply: 5x + y = 15, Demand: x - y = 2. Equilibrium at (17/6, 5/6).
Equation: 5x + y = 15, x - y = 2 → 6x = 17 → x = 17/6, y = 5/6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Optimization){note:"Practical Application: Optimization point where two constraints intersect.

Scenario: Constraint 1: 2x + y = 7, Constraint 2: x - y = 5. Optimal at (4,-1).

Budget: 3x + y = 10, Space: x - y = 4. Optimal at (3.5, -0.5).
Equation: 3x + y = 10, x - y = 4 → 4x = 14 → x = 3.5, y = -0.5

Time: 4x + y = 12, Cost: x - y = 3. Optimal at (3,0).

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Consistent Dependent){note:"Solve: 2x + y = 4 and 4x + 2y = 8

Step 1: Multiply the first by -2:
    -4x - 2y = -8
Step 2: Add to the second:
    -4x - 2y = -8
    4x + 2y = 8
    0 = 0
Step 3: This is true for all values.
Answer: Infinitely many solutions (consistent dependent)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Identical Plans){note:"Practical Application: Two identical business plans with the same cost structure.
