Step 1: Multiply the first by -2:
    -4x - 2y = -8
Step 2: Add to the second:
    -4x - 2y = -8
    4x + 2y = 10
    0 = 2
Step 3: This is false.
Answer: No solution (inconsistent)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Conflicting Costs){note:"Practical Application: Two businesses with proportional costs but different fixed amounts. They will never be equal.

Scenario: Company A: 2x + y = 4, Company B: 4x + 2y = 10. No solution.

Store 1: 3x + y = 6, Store 2: 6x + 2y = 14. No solution.
Equation: 3x + y = 6, 6x + 2y = 14 → 2(3x+y)=6x+2y=12, but RHS is 14 → no solution

Factory A: 4x + y = 8, Factory B: 8x + 2y = 18. No solution.
Equation: 4x + y = 8, 8x + 2y = 18 → 2(4x+y)=8x+2y=16, but RHS is 18 → no solution

Plan 1: 5x + y = 10, Plan 2: 10x + 2y = 22. No solution.
Equation: 5x + y = 10, 10x + 2y = 22 → 2(5x+y)=10x+2y=20, but RHS is 22 → no solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Parallel Systems){note:"Practical Application: Two systems with same slope but different intercepts.

Scenario: System 1: 2x + y = 4, System 2: 4x + 2y = 10. No intersection.

System A: 3x + y = 6, System B: 6x + 2y = 14. No intersection.
Equation: 3x + y = 6, 6x + 2y = 14 → 2(3x+y)=6x+2y=12, but RHS is 14 → no solution

System X: 4x + y = 8, System Y: 8x + 2y = 18. No intersection.
Equation: 4x + y = 8, 8x + 2y = 18 → 2(4x+y)=8x+2y=16, but RHS is 18 → no solution

System M: 5x + y = 10, System N: 10x + 2y = 22. No intersection.
Equation: 5x + y = 10, 10x + 2y = 22 → 2(5x+y)=10x+2y=20, but RHS is 22 → no solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Inconsistent Planning){note:"Practical Application: Two plans that are inconsistent and cannot be satisfied.

Scenario: Plan A: 2x + y = 4, Plan B: 4x + 2y = 10. No solution.

Plan X: 3x + y = 6, Plan Y: 6x + 2y = 14. Inconsistent.
Equation: 3x + y = 6, 6x + 2y = 14 → 2(3x+y)=6x+2y=12, but RHS is 14 → no solution

Plan M: 4x + y = 8, Plan N: 8x + 2y = 18. Inconsistent.
Equation: 4x + y = 8, 8x + 2y = 18 → 2(4x+y)=8x+2y=16, but RHS is 18 → no solution

Plan P: 5x + y = 10, Plan Q: 10x + 2y = 22. Inconsistent.
Equation: 5x + y = 10, 10x + 2y = 22 → 2(5x+y)=10x+2y=20, but RHS is 22 → no solution"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]Dependent vs Independent Systems{note:"Dependent systems have infinitely many solutions. Independent systems have exactly one solution.

Definitions:
- Independent: the equations represent different lines (one intersection point)
- Dependent: the equations represent the same line (infinite intersection points)

Key indicators:
- In elimination: 0 = 0 means dependent (consistent)
- In elimination: 0 = c (c≠0) means inconsistent
- Lines are parallel but distinct: inconsistent
- Lines are identical: dependent
- Lines intersect at one point: independent"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of dependent and independent systems."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Independent){note:"Solve: 3x + 2y = 5 and 2x - 3y = 6

Step 1: Multiply first by 3, second by 2:
    9x + 6y = 15
    4x - 6y = 12
Step 2: Add:
    13x = 27 → x = 27/13
Step 3: Back-substitute: 3(27/13)+2y=5 → 81/13+2y=5 → 2y=5-81/13=(65-81)/13=-16/13 → y=-8/13
Answer: (27/13, -8/13) - independent (one solution)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Unique Intersection){note:"Practical Application: Unique intersection of two different lines.

Scenario: Cost: 3x + 2y = 5, Revenue: 2x - 3y = 6. Intersection at (27/13, -8/13).

Budget: 4x + 3y = 7, Sales: 3x - 4y = 8. Intersection?
Equation: 4x + 3y = 7, 3x - 4y = 8 → 16x + 12y = 28, 9x - 12y = 24 → 25x = 52 → x = 52/25

Profit: 5x + 2y = 9, Cost: 2x - 5y = 10. Intersection?
Equation: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x - 5y = 10 → 25x + 10y = 45, 4x - 10y = 20 → 29x = 65 → x = 65/29

Supply: 6x + 3y = 8, Demand: 3x - 6y = 4. Intersection?
Equation: 6x + 3y = 8, 3x - 6y = 4 → 12x + 6y = 16, 3x - 6y = 4 → 15x = 20 → x = 4/3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Market Analysis){note:"Practical Application: Market equilibrium with unique solution.

Scenario: Demand: 3x + 2y = 5, Supply: 2x - 3y = 6. Equilibrium at (27/13, -8/13).

Market X: 4x + 3y = 7, Market Y: 3x - 4y = 8. Equilibrium at (52/25, ?).
Equation: 4x + 3y = 7, 3x - 4y = 8 → 16x + 12y = 28, 9x - 12y = 24 → 25x = 52 → x = 52/25

Market A: 5x + 2y = 9, Market B: 2x - 5y = 10. Equilibrium at (65/29, ?).
Equation: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x - 5y = 10 → 25x + 10y = 45, 4x - 10y = 20 → 29x = 65 → x = 65/29

Market M: 6x + 3y = 8, Market N: 3x - 6y = 4. Equilibrium at (4/3, ?).
Equation: 6x + 3y = 8, 3x - 6y = 4 → 12x + 6y = 16, 3x - 6y = 4 → 15x = 20 → x = 4/3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Optimization){note:"Practical Application: Optimization with unique solution.

Scenario: Constraint 1: 3x + 2y = 5, Constraint 2: 2x - 3y = 6. Optimal at (27/13, -8/13).

Budget: 4x + 3y = 7, Time: 3x - 4y = 8. Optimal at (52/25, ?).
Equation: 4x + 3y = 7, 3x - 4y = 8 → 16x + 12y = 28, 9x - 12y = 24 → 25x = 52 → x = 52/25

Cost: 5x + 2y = 9, Quality: 2x - 5y = 10. Optimal at (65/29, ?).
Equation: 5x + 2y = 9, 2x - 5y = 10 → 25x + 10y = 45, 4x - 10y = 20 → 29x = 65 → x = 65/29

Labor: 6x + 3y = 8, Materials: 3x - 6y = 4. Optimal at (4/3, ?).
Equation: 6x + 3y = 8, 3x - 6y = 4 → 12x + 6y = 16, 3x - 6y = 4 → 15x = 20 → x = 4/3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Dependent){note:"Solve: 3x + 2y = 5 and 6x + 4y = 10

Step 1: Multiply the first by -2:
    -6x - 4y = -10
Step 2: Add to the second:
    -6x - 4y = -10
    6x + 4y = 10
    0 = 0
Step 3: Dependent (infinitely many solutions)
The equations represent the same line."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Identical Plans){note:"Practical Application: Two identical cost structures that are equivalent for all quantities.

Scenario: Plan A: 3x + 2y = 5, Plan B: 6x + 4y = 10. Same line.

Strategy X: 4x + 3y = 7, Strategy Y: 8x + 6y = 14. Same line.
Equation: 8x + 6y = 14 → 2(4x+3y)=14 → 4x+3y=7 (same)

Option 1: 5x + 2y = 9, Option 2: 10x + 4y = 18. Same line.
Equation: 10x + 4y = 18 → 2(5x+2y)=18 → 5x+2y=9 (same)

Plan M: 6x + 3y = 8, Plan N: 12x + 6y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 12x + 6y = 16 → 2(6x+3y)=16 → 6x+3y=8 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Equivalent Systems){note:"Practical Application: Two systems that represent the same relationship.

Scenario: System 1: 3x + 2y = 5, System 2: 6x + 4y = 10. Same line.

System A: 4x + 3y = 7, System B: 8x + 6y = 14. Same line.
Equation: 8x + 6y = 14 → 2(4x+3y)=14 → 4x+3y=7 (same)

System X: 5x + 2y = 9, System Y: 10x + 4y = 18. Same line.
Equation: 10x + 4y = 18 → 2(5x+2y)=18 → 5x+2y=9 (same)

System M: 6x + 3y = 8, System N: 12x + 6y = 16. Same line.
Equation: 12x + 6y = 16 → 2(6x+3y)=16 → 6x+3y=8 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[3]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Scaled Equations){note:"Practical Application: Two equations where one is a scalar multiple of the other.

Scenario: Eq1: 3x + 2y = 5, Eq2: 6x + 4y = 10 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.

Eq1: 4x + 3y = 7, Eq2: 8x + 6y = 14 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.
Equation: 8x + 6y = 14 → 2(4x+3y)=14 → 4x+3y=7 (same)

Eq1: 5x + 2y = 9, Eq2: 10x + 4y = 18 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.
Equation: 10x + 4y = 18 → 2(5x+2y)=18 → 5x+2y=9 (same)

Eq1: 6x + 3y = 8, Eq2: 12x + 6y = 16 (multiply Eq1 by 2). Same line.
Equation: 12x + 6y = 16 → 2(6x+3y)=16 → 6x+3y=8 (same)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]Functions{note:"A function is a relation where each input has exactly one output. Functions are fundamental to mathematics and model relationships between quantities.

Key concepts:
- Domain: set of all possible inputs (x-values)
- Range: set of all possible outputs (y-values)
- Function notation: f(x) (read as 'f of x')
- Vertical line test: used to determine if a graph represents a function
- One-to-one functions: each output has exactly one input"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]Function Notation{note:"Function notation is a way to write functions that emphasizes the input-output relationship. f(x) means the value of function f at input x.

Key forms:
- f(x) = expression in x
- f(2) means evaluate f at x = 2
- g(t) = t² - 3 means g is a function of t
- The notation helps clarify which variable is the input"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of function notation."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Evaluating){note:"If f(x) = 2x + 3, find f(5).

Step 1: Substitute x = 5 into the function.
Step 2: f(5) = 2(5) + 3 = 10 + 3 = 13
Answer: f(5) = 13"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Taxi Fare){note:"Practical Application: A taxi charges f(x) = 2x + 3 dollars for x miles. A 5-mile trip costs $13.

Variables:
- x = the number of miles (input)
- f(x) = the total fare in dollars (output)

Scenario: A delivery service charges f(x) = 3x + 5 for x items. 4 items cost $17.
Calculation: f(4) = 3(4) + 5 = 17

A plumber charges f(x) = 50x + 25 for x hours. 3 hours cost $175.
Calculation: f(3) = 50(3) + 25 = 175

A painter charges f(x) = 30x + 20 for x rooms. 5 rooms cost $170.
Calculation: f(5) = 30(5) + 20 = 170"}

A printing company charges f(x) = 0.10x + 5 for x pages. 100 pages cost $15.
Calculation: f(100) = 0.10(100) + 5 = 15

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Multiple Evaluations){note:"If g(x) = x² - 4x + 1, find:
    a) g(2)
    b) g(-1)
    c) g(0)

a) g(2) = 2² - 4(2) + 1 = 4 - 8 + 1 = -3
b) g(-1) = (-1)² - 4(-1) + 1 = 1 + 4 + 1 = 6
c) g(0) = 0² - 4(0) + 1 = 1

Answers: g(2) = -3, g(-1) = 6, g(0) = 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Profit Analysis){note:"Practical Application: A company's profit function g(x) = x² - 4x + 1 where x is units sold. Different sales volumes give different profits.

Calculations:

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Piecewise Function){note:"If f(x) = {x+2 if x<1, 3x-1 if x≥1}, find:
    a) f(0)
    b) f(1)
    c) f(2)

a) f(0): 0 < 1, so use x+2: f(0) = 0+2 = 2
b) f(1): 1 ≥ 1, so use 3x-1: f(1) = 3(1) - 1 = 2
c) f(2): 2 ≥ 1, so use 3x-1: f(2) = 3(2) - 1 = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Function as a Rule){note:"If f(x) = 2x² - 5, find f(x+h).

Step 1: Substitute x+h for x:
    f(x+h) = 2(x+h)² - 5
Step 2: Expand:
    f(x+h) = 2(x² + 2xh + h²) - 5
    f(x+h) = 2x² + 4xh + 2h² - 5

Answer: f(x+h) = 2x² + 4xh + 2h² - 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Calculus Preparation){note:"Practical Application: This is used in calculus to compute the derivative as the limit of [f(x+h)-f(x)]/h.

Variables:
- x = the variable (input)
- h = the small change in x
- f(x+h) = the function value at x+h

Scenario: For f(x) = 2x² - 5, f(x+h) = 2x² + 4xh + 2h² - 5.
Derivative: lim_{h→0} [f(x+h)-f(x)]/h = lim_{h→0} [4xh + 2h²]/h = 4x

For f(x) = 3x² + 2, find f(x+h).
Calculation: f(x+h) = 3(x+h)² + 2 = 3x² + 6xh + 3h² + 2

For f(x) = x² - x, find f(x+h).
Calculation: f(x+h) = (x+h)² - (x+h) = x² + 2xh + h² - x - h"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Derivative Preparation){note:"Practical Application: Finding f(x+h) is the first step in computing the derivative of a function.

Variables:
- x = the variable (input)
- h = the small change in x
- f(x+h) = the function value at x+h

Scenario: For f(x) = 5x² - 2, find f(x+h) and then the derivative.
Calculation: f(x+h) = 5x² + 10xh + 5h² - 2
Derivative: lim_{h→0} [10xh + 5h²]/h = 10x

For f(x) = 3x² + 4x, find f(x+h).
Calculation: f(x+h) = 3x² + 6xh + 3h² + 4x + 4h

For f(x) = x² + 2, find f(x+h).
Calculation: f(x+h) = x² + 2xh + h² + 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Rate of Change){note:"Practical Application: f(x+h) - f(x) represents the change in the function when the input changes by h.

Variables:
- x = the variable (input)
- h = the change in x
- f(x+h) - f(x) = the change in output

Scenario: For f(x) = 2x² - 5, f(x+h) - f(x) = 4xh + 2h².
This is the change in output when input changes from x to x+h.

For f(x) = 3x² + 2, find f(x+h) - f(x).
Calculation: f(x+h) - f(x) = 6xh + 3h²

For f(x) = x² - x, find f(x+h) - f(x).
Calculation: f(x+h) - f(x) = 2xh + h² - h

For f(x) = 4x² - 3x + 1, find f(x+h) - f(x).
Calculation: f(x+h) - f(x) = 8xh + 4h² - 3h"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]Domain and Range{note:"The domain is the set of all possible input values (x-values). The range is the set of all possible output values (y-values).

Finding domain:
- Avoid division by zero (denominator ≠ 0)
- Avoid negative under square root (radicand ≥ 0)
- For real-world problems, consider context
- For polynomials, domain is all real numbers

Finding range:
- From graph: look at y-values
- From equation: consider possible outputs
- For quadratics: find vertex to determine min/max
- Use interval notation"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of finding domain and range."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Polynomial){note:"f(x) = x² - 3x + 2

Domain: All real numbers (no restrictions)
Domain: (-∞, ∞)

Range: Since it opens upward, minimum is at vertex.
Vertex: x = -b/(2a) = -(-3)/(2×1) = 3/2
f(3/2) = (3/2)² - 3(3/2) + 2 = 9/4 - 9/2 + 2 = 9/4 - 18/4 + 8/4 = -1/4
Range: [-1/4, ∞)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Projectile Motion){note:"Practical Application: A projectile's height over time. Time can be any real number, but height has a minimum.

Variables:
- t = the time (input)
- h(t) = the height (output)

Scenario: A ball's height h(t) = -16t² + 64t + 2. Domain: all real t. Range: (-∞, 66].

Time t can be any real number (though negative time may not be physical).
Height has a maximum at t=2: h(2) = -64 + 128 + 2 = 66.
So range is (-∞, 66].

A car's distance d(t) = 10t² - 5t + 3. Domain: all real t. Range: [2.375, ∞).
Vertex at t = -(-5)/(20) = 0.25, d(0.25) = 0.625 - 1.25 + 3 = 2.375.

A company's profit p(x) = -x² + 10x - 16. Domain: all real x. Range: (-∞, 9].
Vertex at x = 5, p(5) = -25 + 50 - 16 = 9."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: A quadratic function representing revenue or cost, where the domain is all real numbers but the range is limited by the vertex.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (input)
- R(x) = the revenue (output)

Scenario: Revenue R(x) = -2x² + 12x + 3. Domain: all real. Range: (-∞, 21].
Vertex at x = 3, R(3) = -18 + 36 + 3 = 21.

Cost C(x) = x² - 6x + 10. Domain: all real. Range: [1, ∞).
Vertex at x = 3, C(3) = 9 - 18 + 10 = 1.

Profit P(x) = -3x² + 18x - 5. Domain: all real. Range: (-∞, 22].
Vertex at x = 3, P(3) = -27 + 54 - 5 = 22."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: A quadratic function modeling physical motion with domain restrictions based on physical context.

Variables:
- t = the time (input)
- h(t) = the height (output)

Scenario: A falling object's height h(t) = -16t² + 80t + 6. Domain: 0 ≤ t ≤ (80 + √(6400+384))/32 ≈ 5.04.
The domain is restricted to times when height is non-negative.

A ball thrown upward: h(t) = -5t² + 30t + 2. Domain: 0 ≤ t ≤ (30 + √(900+40))/10 ≈ 6.12.
Range: [0, 47] (max at t=3: h(3)= -45 + 90 + 2 = 47).

A rocket's height: h(t) = -4.9t² + 98t + 10. Domain: 0 ≤ t ≤ (98 + √(9604+196))/9.8 ≈ 20.1."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Rational Function){note:"f(x) = 1/(x-2)

Domain: x ≠ 2 (denominator cannot be zero)
Domain: (-∞, 2) ∪ (2, ∞)

Range: All real numbers except 0 (horizontal asymptote at y=0)
Range: (-∞, 0) ∪ (0, ∞)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Inverse Relationships){note:"Practical Application: A function modeling inverse relationships where x=2 causes division by zero.

Variables:
- x = the input value (input)
- E(x) = the efficiency (output)

Scenario: A machine's efficiency E(x) = 100/(x-2). Domain: x ≠ 2. Range: all real except 0.
If x=2, the machine breaks down (division by zero).

A chemical reaction rate R(x) = 50/(x-3). Domain: x ≠ 3. Range: all real except 0.
If x=3, the reaction stops.

A population model P(x) = 1000/(x-5). Domain: x ≠ 5. Range: all real except 0.
If x=5, the population crashes."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]Vertical Line Test{note:"The vertical line test determines if a graph represents a function. If any vertical line intersects the graph more than once, the graph is not a function.

Key idea:
- A function has exactly one output for each input
- A vertical line represents a single input (x-value)
- If a vertical line intersects the graph at two points, one input has two outputs (not a function)

Examples:
- Lines, parabolas, and most basic functions pass the test
- Circles and some relations fail the test"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of the vertical line test."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Passes - Function){note:"Graph: y = x² (parabola)

Any vertical line intersects at most one point.
This is a function."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: A function modeling the area of a square given its side length.

Variables:
- s = the side length (input)
- A(s) = the area (output)

Scenario: Area A(s) = s². For each side length, there is exactly one area.
This is a function.

Revenue R(x) = 10x. For each quantity sold, there is exactly one revenue.
This is a function.

Cost C(x) = 5x + 2. For each unit produced, there is exactly one cost.
This is a function."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Fails - Not a Function){note:"Graph: x² + y² = 9 (circle)

A vertical line at x=1 intersects at two points: y = ±√8
This is not a function (one input gives two outputs)."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Circle Relations){note:"Practical Application: A circle cannot be a function because for each x there are two y values.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (two possible values)

Scenario: A circle equation x² + y² = r² represents a relation, not a function.
For x=3, y = ±√(r²-9), two outputs.

An ellipse equation x²/9 + y²/4 = 1 is not a function.
For x=1, y = ±√(4(1-1/9)) = ±√(32/9) = ±(4√2)/3.

A hyperbola equation x²/4 - y²/9 = 1 is not a function.
For x=2, y = 0 only; for x=3, y = ±3√5/2."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Relations vs Functions){note:"Practical Application: Some relationships are not functions because they fail the vertical line test.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (could be multiple)

Scenario: A relationship where x² + y² = 4 is not a function.
For each x (except x=0), there are two y values.

A relationship where x = y² is not a function.
For x=4, y = ±2.

A relationship where |x| + |y| = 1 is not a function.
For many x values, there are two y values."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Fails - Not a Function){note:"Graph: x = y² (horizontal parabola)

A vertical line at x=4 intersects at two points: y = ±2
This is not a function."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[3]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Relations){note:"Practical Application: A relation where each input has two possible outputs.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (could be multiple)

Scenario: A relationship x = y² is not a function.
For x=4, y = ±2.

A relationship x = |y| is not a function.
For x=2, y = ±2.

A relationship x = y³ is a function (since cube root is unique).
For each x, there is exactly one y."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]Function Operations{note:"Functions can be combined using addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. The resulting function's domain is the intersection of the domains.

Definitions:
- (f+g)(x) = f(x) + g(x)
- (f-g)(x) = f(x) - g(x)
- (fg)(x) = f(x) × g(x)
- (f/g)(x) = f(x) / g(x), where g(x) ≠ 0

Domain rules:
- Addition, subtraction, multiplication: domain is intersection
- Division: domain is intersection excluding zeros of denominator"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of function operations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Addition){note:"f(x) = 2x + 1 and g(x) = x - 3

(f+g)(x) = (2x+1) + (x-3) = 3x - 2
Domain: All real numbers"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Total Revenue){note:"Practical Application: Adding profit from two different product lines.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (input)
- (f+g)(x) = the total revenue (output)

Scenario: Product A: f(x) = 2x + 1, Product B: g(x) = x - 3.
Total revenue: (f+g)(x) = 3x - 2.

Cost function A: f(x) = 3x + 4, Cost B: g(x) = 2x - 1.
Total cost: (f+g)(x) = 5x + 3.

Revenue A: f(x) = 5x + 2, Revenue B: g(x) = 4x - 3.
Total revenue: (f+g)(x) = 9x - 1."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Combined Costs){note:"Practical Application: Combining costs from two departments.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (input)
- (f+g)(x) = the total cost (output)

Scenario: Department A: f(x) = 2x + 1, Department B: g(x) = x - 3.
Combined cost: (f+g)(x) = 3x - 2.

Marketing: f(x) = 5x + 2, Operations: g(x) = 3x - 1.
Combined: (f+g)(x) = 8x + 1.

Production: f(x) = 4x + 3, Distribution: g(x) = 2x - 4.
Combined: (f+g)(x) = 6x - 1."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Subtraction){note:"f(x) = x² + 4 and g(x) = 2x

(f-g)(x) = (x²+4) - (2x) = x² - 2x + 4

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Profit Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Difference between revenue and cost to find profit.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Multiplication){note:"f(x) = x - 2 and g(x) = x + 5

(fg)(x) = (x-2)(x+5) = x² + 5x - 2x - 10 = x² + 3x - 10
Domain: All real numbers"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Area Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Multiplying two functions representing length and width to find area.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- (fg)(x) = the area (output)

Scenario: Length: f(x) = x - 2, Width: g(x) = x + 5.
Area: (fg)(x) = x² + 3x - 10.

Length: f(x) = 2x + 1, Width: g(x) = x - 3.
Area: (fg)(x) = 2x² - 5x - 3.

Length: f(x) = 3x - 2, Width: g(x) = 2x + 5.
Area: (fg)(x) = 6x² + 11x - 10."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Revenue Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Revenue as price × quantity.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (input)
- (fg)(x) = the revenue (output)

Scenario: Price: f(x) = x - 2, Quantity: g(x) = x + 5.
Revenue: (fg)(x) = x² + 3x - 10.

Price: f(x) = 5 - x, Quantity: g(x) = 2x + 3.
Revenue: (fg)(x) = (5-x)(2x+3) = -2x² + 7x + 15.

Price: f(x) = 10 - 2x, Quantity: g(x) = 3x + 2.
Revenue: (fg)(x) = (10-2x)(3x+2) = -6x² + 26x + 20."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: Multiplying two functions representing physical quantities.

- x = the input (input)
- (fg)(x) = the physical quantity (output)

Scenario: Force: f(x) = x - 2, Distance: g(x) = x + 5.
Work: (fg)(x) = x² + 3x - 10.

Distance: (fg)(x) = 5t² - 9t - 2.

Force: (fg)(x) = 2x² - 5x - 3."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Division){note:"f(x) = x² - 4 and g(x) = x - 2

(f/g)(x) = (x²-4)/(x-2) = (x-2)(x+2)/(x-2) = x+2, x ≠ 2
Domain: All real numbers except 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Cost per Unit){note:"Practical Application: Dividing total cost by number of items to find cost per item.

Variables:
- x = the number of items (input)
- (f/g)(x) = the cost per item (output)

Scenario: Total cost: f(x) = x² - 4, Number of items: g(x) = x - 2.
Cost per item: (f/g)(x) = x+2, x ≠ 2.

Total revenue: f(x) = x² - 9, Quantity: g(x) = x - 3.
Revenue per unit: (f/g)(x) = x+3, x ≠ 3.

Total distance: f(x) = x² - 25, Time: g(x) = x - 5.
Speed: (f/g)(x) = x+5, x ≠ 5."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Average Calculation){note:"Practical Application: Dividing total by count to find average.

- x = the number of items (input)
- (f/g)(x) = the average (output)

Scenario: Total score: f(x) = x² - 4, Number of tests: g(x) = x - 2.
Average: (f/g)(x) = x+2, x ≠ 2.

Total weight: f(x) = x² - 9, Number of items: g(x) = x - 3.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[4]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Combined Operations){note:"f(x) = 2x and g(x) = 3x - 1

(f+g)(2) = f(2) + g(2) = 4 + 5 = 9
(fg)(3) = f(3) × g(3) = 6 × 8 = 48
(f/g)(1) = f(1) / g(1) = 2 / 2 = 1 (g(1) ≠ 0)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]Composition of Functions{note:"Composition of functions applies one function to the result of another. (f ∘ g)(x) = f(g(x)). The order matters: f ∘ g is generally not the same as g ∘ f.

Key steps:
1. Evaluate the inner function first
2. Use the result as input for the outer function
3. Domain: g(x) must be in the domain of f"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of function composition."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Basic){note:"f(x) = 2x + 1 and g(x) = x - 3

(f ∘ g)(x) = f(g(x)) = f(x-3) = 2(x-3) + 1 = 2x - 6 + 1 = 2x - 5
(g ∘ f)(x) = g(f(x)) = g(2x+1) = (2x+1) - 3 = 2x - 2

(f ∘ g)(4) = 2(4) - 5 = 3
(g ∘ f)(4) = 2(4) - 2 = 6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Tax and Discount){note:"Practical Application: Applying a discount function after a tax function (order matters).

Variables:
- x = the original price (input)
- (f ∘ g)(x) = the final price after both operations (output)

Scenario: Tax: f(x) = 1.08x, Discount: g(x) = 0.9x.
Final price with discount then tax: (f ∘ g)(x) = 1.08(0.9x) = 0.972x.
Final price with tax then discount: (g ∘ f)(x) = 0.9(1.08x) = 0.972x (same here).

Commission: f(x) = 0.15x, Bonus: g(x) = x + 50.
Commission on bonus: (f ∘ g)(x) = 0.15(x+50) = 0.15x + 7.5.
Bonus on commission: (g ∘ f)(x) = 0.15x + 50.

Shipping: f(x) = x + 5, Tax: g(x) = 1.08x.
Tax then shipping: (f ∘ g)(x) = 1.08x + 5.
Shipping then tax: (g ∘ f)(x) = 1.08(x+5) = 1.08x + 5.4."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Unit Conversions){note:"Practical Application: Converting units where one function's output becomes another's input.

Variables:
- x = the input in the original unit (input)
- (f ∘ g)(x) = the output in the final unit (output)

Scenario: Inches to feet: g(x) = x/12, Feet to yards: f(x) = x/3.
Inches to yards: (f ∘ g)(x) = (x/12)/3 = x/36.

Celsius to Fahrenheit: f(x) = 1.8x + 32, Fahrenheit to Kelvin: g(x) = (x+459.67)*5/9.
Celsius to Kelvin: (g ∘ f)(x) = ((1.8x+32)+459.67)*5/9 = (1.8x+491.67)*5/9 = x + 273.15.

Hours to minutes: g(x) = 60x, Minutes to seconds: f(x) = 60x.
Hours to seconds: (f ∘ g)(x) = 60(60x) = 3600x."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: Multiple transformations applied to data in a specific order.

Variables:
- x = the input value (input)
- (g ∘ f)(x) = the final value after transformations (output)

Scenario: Revenue: f(x) = 0.8x (after discount), Tax: g(x) = 1.08x.
Final: (g ∘ f)(x) = 1.08(0.8x) = 0.864x.

Cost: f(x) = x + 10 (shipping), Conversion: g(x) = 0.9x (currency exchange).
Cost in foreign currency: (g ∘ f)(x) = 0.9(x+10).

Profit: f(x) = 1.2x (markup), Tax: g(x) = 0.85x (tax deduction).
After markup and tax: (g ∘ f)(x) = 0.85(1.2x) = 1.02x."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Square Root){note:"f(x) = √x and g(x) = x² - 1

(f ∘ g)(x) = √(x² - 1)
Domain: x² - 1 ≥ 0 → x ≥ 1 or x ≤ -1

(g ∘ f)(x) = (√x)² - 1 = x - 1
Domain: x ≥ 0 (for √x)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: Converting units with square root functions.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- (f ∘ g)(x) = the final value (output)

Scenario: Speed from kinetic energy: f(E) = √(2E/m), Energy from mass: g(m) = mc².
Speed from mass: (f ∘ g)(m) = √(2mc²/m) = √(2c²) = c√2.

Height from time: f(t) = 4.9t², Time from distance: g(d) = √(2d/g).
Height from distance: (f ∘ g)(d) = 4.9(√(2d/9.8))² = 4.9(2d/9.8) = d.

Frequency from wavelength: f(λ) = c/λ, Wavelength from energy: g(E) = hc/E.
Frequency from energy: (f ∘ g)(E) = c/(hc/E) = E/h."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Engineering Applications){note:"Practical Application: Engineering calculations with square root functions.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- (f ∘ g)(x) = the engineering value (output)

Scenario: Stress from force: f(F) = F/A, Force from pressure: g(P) = PA.
Stress from pressure: (f ∘ g)(P) = PA/A = P.

Power from current: f(I) = I²R, Current from voltage: g(V) = V/R.
Power from voltage: (f ∘ g)(V) = (V/R)²R = V²/R.

Flow rate from pressure: f(P) = √(2P/ρ), Pressure from height: g(h) = ρgh.
Flow rate from height: (f ∘ g)(h) = √(2ρgh/ρ) = √(2gh)."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Science Applications){note:"Practical Application: Scientific calculations with composite functions.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[5]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Multiple Compositions){note:"f(x) = 2x, g(x) = x + 1, h(x) = x²

(f ∘ g ∘ h)(x) = f(g(h(x))) = f(g(x²)) = f(x² + 1) = 2(x² + 1) = 2x² + 2

(f ∘ h ∘ g)(x) = f(h(g(x))) = f(h(x+1)) = f((x+1)²) = 2(x+1)²"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]Inverse Functions{note:"An inverse function reverses the effect of the original function. If f maps x to y, then f⁻¹ maps y back to x.

Key conditions:
- f must be one-to-one (passes horizontal line test)
- Domain of f = Range of f⁻¹
- Range of f = Domain of f⁻¹
- (f ∘ f⁻¹)(x) = x and (f⁻¹ ∘ f)(x) = x

To find inverse:
1. Replace f(x) with y
2. Swap x and y
3. Solve for y
4. Replace y with f⁻¹(x)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of finding inverse functions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Linear){note:"Find the inverse of f(x) = 2x + 3

Step 1: y = 2x + 3
Step 2: Swap x and y: x = 2y + 3
Step 3: Solve for y:
    x - 3 = 2y
    y = (x - 3)/2
Step 4: f⁻¹(x) = (x - 3)/2

Check: f(f⁻¹(x)) = 2((x-3)/2) + 3 = x - 3 + 3 = x ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Temperature Conversion){note:"Practical Application: Converting between Celsius and Fahrenheit.

Variables:
- x = the temperature in Fahrenheit (input)
- f⁻¹(x) = the temperature in Celsius (output)

Scenario: Fahrenheit to Celsius: F(x) = 1.8x + 32.
Inverse: C(x) = (x - 32)/1.8.

Celsius to Fahrenheit: C(x) = 5/9(x - 32).
Inverse: F(x) = 9/5x + 32.

Miles to kilometers: M(x) = 1.609x.
Inverse: K(x) = x/1.609."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Financial Applications){note:"Practical Application: Converting between price and discount.

- f⁻¹(x) = the original price (output)

Inverse (original price from discounted price): f⁻¹(x) = x/0.85.

Price with tax: f(x) = 1.08x.
Inverse (pre-tax price): f⁻¹(x) = x/1.08.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Quadratic - Restricted Domain){note:"Find the inverse of f(x) = x², x ≥ 0

Step 1: y = x², x ≥ 0
Step 2: Swap: x = y², y ≥ 0
Step 3: Solve for y: y = √x
Step 4: f⁻¹(x) = √x

The domain restriction is necessary because x² is not one-to-one on all real numbers."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Area and Side Length){note:"Practical Application: Finding the side length of a square given its area.

- f⁻¹(x) = the side length (output)

Scenario: Area function: A(s) = s².
Inverse (side from area): s(A) = √A.

Circle area: A(r) = πr².
Inverse (radius from area): r(A) = √(A/π).

Sphere surface area: SA(r) = 4πr².
Inverse (radius from surface area): r(SA) = √(SA/(4π))."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Rational){note:"Find the inverse of f(x) = (2x+1)/(x-3), x ≠ 3

Step 1: y = (2x+1)/(x-3)
Step 2: Swap: x = (2y+1)/(y-3)
Step 3: Solve for y:
    x(y-3) = 2y+1
    xy - 3x = 2y + 1
    xy - 2y = 3x + 1
    y(x-2) = 3x + 1
    y = (3x+1)/(x-2)
Step 4: f⁻¹(x) = (3x+1)/(x-2), x ≠ 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Resistance and Conductance){note:"Practical Application: Inverting a rational function to find the inverse relationship.

Variables:
- x = the resistance (input)
- f⁻¹(x) = the conductance (output)

Scenario: Resistance: R(I) = V/I.
Inverse (conductance): G(R) = V/R.

Ohm's law: I(V) = V/R.
Inverse (voltage from current): V(I) = IR.

Power: P(V) = V²/R.
Inverse (voltage from power): V(P) = √(PR)."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: Finding original quantity from price.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Science Applications){note:"Practical Application: Inverting scientific formulas.

Variables:
- x = the pressure (input)
- f⁻¹(x) = the volume (output)

Scenario: Gas law: P(V) = nRT/V.
Inverse (volume from pressure): V(P) = nRT/P.

Boyle's law: P(V) = k/V.
Inverse: V(P) = k/P.

Gravitational force: F(r) = GMm/r².
Inverse (distance from force): r(F) = √(GMm/F)."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Square Root){note:"Find the inverse of f(x) = √(x-2)

Step 1: y = √(x-2), x ≥ 2, y ≥ 0
Step 2: Swap: x = √(y-2), y ≥ 2, x ≥ 0
Step 3: Solve for y:
    x² = y - 2
    y = x² + 2
Step 4: f⁻¹(x) = x² + 2, x ≥ 0"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Distance and Time){note:"Practical Application: Finding time from distance.

Variables:
- x = the distance (input)
- f⁻¹(x) = the time (output)

Scenario: Distance: d(t) = √(t-2).
Inverse (time from distance): t(d) = d² + 2.

Height: h(t) = √(t-5).
Inverse: t(h) = h² + 5.

Population: P(t) = √(t-3).
Inverse: t(P) = P² + 3."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: Finding energy from velocity.

Variables:
- x = the velocity (input)
- f⁻¹(x) = the energy (output)

Scenario: Velocity from energy: v(E) = √(2E/m).
Inverse: E(v) = 0.5mv².

Frequency from wavelength: f(λ) = c/λ.
Inverse: λ(f) = c/f.

Period from frequency: T(f) = 1/f.
Inverse: f(T) = 1/T."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: Finding quantity from profit.

Variables:
- x = the profit (input)
- f⁻¹(x) = the quantity (output)

Scenario: Profit: P(x) = √(x-2) - 1.
Inverse: x(P) = (P+1)² + 2.

Revenue: R(x) = √(x-3) + 2.
Inverse: x(R) = (R-2)² + 3.

Cost: C(x) = √(x-1) + 4.
Inverse: x(C) = (C-4)² + 1."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]Example 5 (Verification){note:"Verify that f(x) = 2x - 5 and g(x) = (x+5)/2 are inverses.

Check: (f ∘ g)(x) = f((x+5)/2) = 2((x+5)/2) - 5 = x + 5 - 5 = x
Check: (g ∘ f)(x) = g(2x-5) = ((2x-5)+5)/2 = 2x/2 = x

Yes, they are inverses."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Verification in Business){note:"Practical Application: A function and its inverse are used in cryptographic applications.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- f(x) and g(x) = inverse functions

Scenario: f(x) = 2x - 5, g(x) = (x+5)/2 are inverses.
Used in encryption/decryption.

f(x) = 3x + 2, g(x) = (x-2)/3 are inverses.
f(x) = 1.5x - 3, g(x) = (x+3)/1.5 are inverses."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[6]lvl5[1]lvl6[5]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Scientific Applications){note:"Practical Application: Functions and their inverses in science.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]One-to-One Functions{note:"A one-to-one function (injective) maps distinct inputs to distinct outputs. Each output has exactly one input.

Key tests:
- Horizontal line test: any horizontal line intersects the graph at most once
- Algebraically: if f(a) = f(b), then a = b
- Only one-to-one functions have inverses that are functions"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of one-to-one functions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (One-to-One){note:"f(x) = 2x + 3

Horizontal line test: passes (any horizontal line intersects once).
Algebraic: If f(a) = f(b), then 2a+3 = 2b+3 → 2a=2b → a=b.
This is one-to-one."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Unique IDs){note:"Practical Application: A function where each input gives a unique output, like assigning unique IDs.

Variables:
- x = the employee number (input)
- f(x) = the unique ID (output)

Scenario: An ID assignment function: ID(employee) = 2×employee_number + 3.
Each employee gets a unique ID.

A serial number function: SN(x) = 3x + 2.
Each input gives a unique serial number.

A hash function: H(x) = 2x - 5.
Each input gives a unique hash value."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Data Mapping){note:"Practical Application: A function that maps data to unique outputs.

Variables:
- x = the data value (input)
- f(x) = the mapped value (output)

Scenario: A mapping function: M(x) = 4x + 1.
Each data point maps to a unique value.

A transformation: T(x) = 5x - 2.
Each input transforms to a unique output.

A conversion: C(x) = 2.54x.
Each measurement converts to a unique value."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: A function that maps physical quantities uniquely.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Not One-to-One){note:"f(x) = x²

Horizontal line test: y=4 intersects at x=2 and x=-2 (two points).
Algebraic: f(2) = 4 and f(-2) = 4, but 2 ≠ -2.
This is not one-to-one."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Multiple Inputs Same Output){note:"Practical Application: A function where multiple inputs give the same output.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- f(x) = the output (output)

Scenario: A square function: f(x) = x².
Both x=2 and x=-2 give f(x)=4.

An absolute value function: f(x) = |x|.
Both x=3 and x=-3 give f(x)=3.

A quadratic: f(x) = (x-1)².
Both x=0 and x=2 give f(x)=1."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Non-Unique Mapping){note:"Practical Application: A function that maps multiple inputs to the same output.

- x = the input (input)
- f(x) = the output (output)

Scenario: A temperature function: T(t) = (t-32)².
Multiple times give same temperature.

A distance function: d(t) = (t-5)².
Multiple times give same distance.

A profit function: P(x) = (x-3)².
Multiple quantities give same profit."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[7]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: A physical quantity where multiple inputs give the same output.

- x = the input (input)

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[8]Onto Functions{note:"An onto function (surjective) maps the domain onto the entire codomain. Every element in the codomain has at least one preimage in the domain.

Key points:
- Range = Codomain
- For every y in codomain, there exists x in domain with f(x) = y
- Often determined by the context and given codomain"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[8]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of onto functions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[8]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Onto){note:"f(x) = 2x + 3 from R to R

For any y in R, solve 2x+3 = y → x = (y-3)/2, which is real.
So f is onto."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[8]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Pricing Function){note:"Practical Application: A function that covers all possible outputs, like a pricing function that can generate any price.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- f(x) = the price (output)

Scenario: Price function: P(x) = 2x + 3. For any price y, x = (y-3)/2.
This means any price is achievable.

Cost function: C(x) = 5x - 2. For any cost y, x = (y+2)/5.
Any cost is achievable.

Revenue function: R(x) = 10x. For any revenue y, x = y/10.
Any revenue is achievable."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[8]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Physical Applications){note:"Practical Application: A function that maps to all possible physical values.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- f(x) = the physical value (output)

Scenario: Distance function: d(t) = 10t. For any distance d, t = d/10.
Any distance is achievable.

Velocity function: v(t) = 5t. For any velocity v, t = v/5.
Any velocity is achievable.

Pressure function: P(h) = ρgh. For any pressure P, h = P/(ρg).
Any pressure is achievable."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[8]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Not Onto){note:"f(x) = x² from R to R

For y = -1, no real x satisfies x² = -1.
So f is not onto (range is [0, ∞), not all R)."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[8]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Defined Output Range){note:"Practical Application: A function where the output range is explicitly defined.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[9]Piecewise Functions{note:"Piecewise functions use different formulas for different parts of the domain. They are defined by multiple sub-functions, each with its own domain interval.

Key notation:
f(x) = { expression1 if condition1, expression2 if condition2, ... }

Common uses:
- Absolute value functions
- Step functions
- Functions with different behavior in different intervals
- Real-world situations with changing conditions"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[9]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of piecewise functions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[9]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Basic Piecewise){note:"f(x) = { x+2 if x < 1, 3x-1 if x ≥ 1 }

Find:
a) f(0) = 0+2 = 2 (since 0 < 1)
b) f(1) = 3(1)-1 = 2 (since 1 ≥ 1)
c) f(2) = 3(2)-1 = 5 (since 2 ≥ 1)
d) f(-1) = -1+2 = 1 (since -1 < 1)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[9]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Tax Brackets){note:"Practical Application: Tax brackets where different rates apply to different income levels.

Find W(50)=10, W(150)=15, W(250)=20.

Electricity bill: E(x) = {0.10x if x ≤ 100, 0.15x - 5 if x > 100}.
Find E(50)=5, E(150)=17.50, E(200)=25."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[9]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]Example 2 (Absolute Value as Piecewise){note:"f(x) = |x| = { -x if x < 0, x if x ≥ 0 }

Find:

b) |0| = 0
c) |5| = 5

This shows absolute value is always non-negative."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[9]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Distance Measurement){note:"Practical Application: Distance functions where distance is always non-negative.

W(50)=10, W(150)=15, W(250)=20.

Electricity: E(x) = {0.10x if x ≤ 100, 0.15x - 5 if x > 100}.
E(50)=5, E(150)=17.50, E(200)=25."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[9]lvl5[1]lvl6[4]Example 4 (Graphing Piecewise){note:"f(x) = { x² if x < 0, 2x+1 if x ≥ 0 }

Graph:
For x < 0: parabola (x²)
For x ≥ 0: line (2x+1)

At x = 0: f(0) = 2(0)+1 = 1
From the left: f(0) would be 0² = 0 (but not included since x < 0)
So there is a jump at x = 0."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[10]Absolute Value Functions{note:"Absolute value functions have the form f(x) = |x|. They are piecewise functions that give the distance from zero.

Key properties:
- f(x) = |x| = { -x if x < 0, x if x ≥ 0 }
- Domain: all real numbers
- Range: [0, ∞)
- V-shaped graph with vertex at (0,0)
- Symmetric about the y-axis

Transformations:
- f(x) = |x - h| + k: vertex at (h, k)
- Stretches and compressions: a|x|"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[10]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Detailed examples of absolute value functions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[10]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Graphing){note:"Graph f(x) = |x|

Vertex: (0,0)
For x ≥ 0: y = x
For x < 0: y = -x
V-shape opening upward."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[4]lvl4[10]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Distance from Zero){note:"Practical Application: Distance from zero on a number line.

This is always non-negative.

Always non-negative deviation.

Always non-negative error."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]Linear Functions{note:"Linear functions have the form f(x) = mx + b, where m is the slope and b is the y-intercept. They graph as straight lines.

Key components:
- Slope: rate of change (rise/run)
- y-intercept: where the line crosses the y-axis
- x-intercept: where the line crosses the x-axis

Forms:
- Slope-intercept: y = mx + b
- Point-slope: y - y₁ = m(x - x₁)
- Standard form: Ax + By = C"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]Slope{note:"Slope measures the steepness and direction of a line. It is the ratio of vertical change to horizontal change.

Formula:
- m = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁)
- m = rise/run

Types of slope:
- Positive: line rises from left to right
- Negative: line falls from left to right
- Zero: horizontal line
- Undefined: vertical line

Parallel lines: same slope
Perpendicular lines: slopes are negative reciprocals (m₁ × m₂ = -1)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Positive Slope{note:"A positive slope means the line rises as x increases.

Key properties:
- m > 0
- Line goes up from left to right
- Example: y = 2x + 1 (slope = 2)

Example: Points (1,3) and (3,7)
m = (7-3)/(3-1) = 4/2 = 2

The line rises 2 units for every 1 unit of horizontal movement."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]P.E. 1 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: A cost function where cost increases with quantity. The slope represents the cost per unit.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (input)
- C(x) = the cost (output)

Scenario: Cost function: C(x) = 2x + 1. Slope 2 means cost increases by $2 per unit.

Revenue: R(x) = 5x. Slope 5 means revenue increases by $5 per unit.

Profit: P(x) = 3x - 4. Slope 3 means profit increases by $3 per unit."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[2]P.E. 2 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: A velocity function with positive slope means acceleration.

Variables:
- t = the time (input)
- v(t) = the velocity (output)

Scenario: Velocity: v(t) = 2t + 1. Slope 2 means acceleration is 2 m/s².

Distance: d(t) = 3t. Slope 3 means speed is 3 m/s.

Temperature: T(t) = 0.5t + 10. Slope 0.5 means temperature increases by 0.5°C per minute."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]P.E. 3 (Engineering Applications){note:"Practical Application: A force function with positive slope.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- F(x) = the force (output)

Scenario: Force: F(x) = 4x + 2. Slope 4 means force increases by 4 N per unit.

Pressure: P(h) = ρgh. Slope ρg means pressure increases with depth.

Voltage: V(t) = 5t. Slope 5 means voltage increases by 5 V per second."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Negative Slope{note:"A negative slope means the line falls as x increases.

Key properties:
- m < 0
- Line goes down from left to right
- Example: y = -3x + 2 (slope = -3)

Example: Points (1,5) and (3,1)
m = (1-5)/(3-1) = -4/2 = -2

The line falls 2 units for every 1 unit of horizontal movement."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]lvl6[1]P.E. 1 (Depreciation){note:"Practical Application: A depreciation function where value decreases over time.

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Zero Slope{note:"A zero slope means the line is horizontal.

Key properties:
- m = 0
- The line does not rise or fall
- y is constant
- Example: y = 5 (slope = 0)

Example: Points (1,3) and (5,3)
m = (3-3)/(5-1) = 0/4 = 0

The line is horizontal."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Undefined Slope{note:"An undefined slope means the line is vertical.

Key properties:
- m is undefined
- The line has no slope (division by zero)
- x is constant
- Example: x = 4 (undefined slope)

Example: Points (2,1) and (2,5)
m = (5-1)/(2-2) = 4/0 = undefined

The line is vertical."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]lvl6[1]P.E. 1 (Vertical Lines){note:"Practical Application: A function that has multiple outputs for a single input (not a function).

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (could be any value)

Scenario: A vertical line x = 4. This is not a function because at x=4, y can be any value.

A vertical asymptote: x = 2 in a rational function.

A vertical wall: x = constant in physics problems."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]lvl6[2]P.E. 2 (Engineering Applications){note:"Practical Application: Vertical lines in engineering.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (could be any value)

Scenario: A vertical support: x = 5 in a structural analysis.

A vertical line in a graph: x = -3 representing a boundary.

A vertical asymptote in a control system: x = 0 representing instability."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]lvl6[3]P.E. 3 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: Vertical lines in physics.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (could be any value)

Scenario: A vertical wall: x = constant in a projectile problem.

A vertical asymptote: x = 0 in a gravitational field.

A vertical line of charge: x = constant in electrostatics."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Parallel Lines{note:"Parallel lines have the same slope and never intersect.

Key property:
- m₁ = m₂
- Different y-intercepts

Example: y = 2x + 3 and y = 2x - 5
Both have slope 2, so they are parallel."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]lvl6[1]P.E. 1 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: Two businesses with the same variable cost but different fixed costs.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (input)
- y = the cost (output)

Scenario: Cost A: y = 2x + 3, Cost B: y = 2x - 5.
Same slope (2), different intercepts (3 vs -5).

Revenue A: y = 5x + 10, Revenue B: y = 5x + 20.
Same slope, different intercepts.

Profit A: y = 3x + 2, Profit B: y = 3x - 1.
Same slope, different intercepts."}

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (output)

Scenario: Two parallel lines: y = 2x + 1 and y = 2x - 3.
Same slope, different intercepts.

Constant velocity lines: v = 10 and v = 20 (parallel horizontal lines).

Constant force lines: F = 5 and F = 8 (parallel horizontal lines)."}

Parallel beams in a structure.

Parallel lines in a circuit diagram."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[6]Perpendicular Lines{note:"Perpendicular lines intersect at a right angle (90°).

Key property:
- m₁ × m₂ = -1
- Slopes are negative reciprocals

Example: If m₁ = 2, then m₂ = -1/2
y = 2x + 3 and y = -0.5x + 1 are perpendicular.

Check: 2 × (-0.5) = -1 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[6]lvl6[1]P.E. 1 (Geometry){note:"Practical Application: Finding lines that are perpendicular to a given line in geometry.

Variables:
- m = the slope of the original line (input)
- -1/m = the slope of the perpendicular line (output)

Scenario: A line y = 2x + 3. Perpendicular line has slope -1/2.

A line y = 3x - 1. Perpendicular line has slope -1/3.

A line y = -4x + 2. Perpendicular line has slope 1/4."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]Slope-Intercept Form (y = mx + b){note:"The slope-intercept form is y = mx + b, where m is the slope and b is the y-intercept.

Key features:
- m: slope (rise/run)
- b: y-intercept (point where x=0)
- Easy to graph from
- Directly gives rate of change

Example: y = 2x + 3
- Slope: 2
- y-intercept: 3 (point (0,3))
- The line rises 2 units for every 1 unit right"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]Examples{note:"Examples of slope-intercept form."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]Example 1 (Graphing){note:"Graph: y = 2x - 1

Step 1: Identify m = 2, b = -1
Step 2: Plot y-intercept (0, -1)
Step 3: Use slope to find another point: rise 2, run 1 → (1, 1)
Step 4: Draw line through points"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[1]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: Graphing a cost function where cost increases by $2 per unit with a fixed cost of $1.

Variables:
- x = the quantity (input)
- C(x) = the cost (output)

Scenario: Cost: C(x) = 2x - 1. Fixed cost is -$1? Actually b = -1 means at x=0, cost is -1 (not realistic). But for C(x) = 2x + 1, fixed cost is $1.

Revenue: R(x) = 5x. y-intercept 0, slope 5.

Profit: P(x) = 3x - 4. y-intercept -4, slope 3."}

- t = the time (input)
- v(t) = the velocity (output)

Scenario: Velocity: v(t) = 2t - 1. y-intercept -1 m/s, slope 2 m/s².

Distance: d(t) = 3t + 2. y-intercept 2 m, slope 3 m/s.

Temperature: T(t) = 0.5t + 10. y-intercept 10°C, slope 0.5°C/min."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]Example 3 (Convert from Standard){note:"Convert 2x + 3y = 6 to slope-intercept form

Step 1: 2x + 3y = 6
Step 2: 3y = -2x + 6
Step 3: y = (-2/3)x + 2
Answer: y = (-2/3)x + 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[1]P.E. 1 (Business Applications){note:"Practical Application: Converting between different forms of a linear equation for easier graphing.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (output)

Scenario: Cost: 2x + 3y = 6 → y = (-2/3)x + 2.
Slope -2/3, y-intercept 2.

Revenue: 4x + 5y = 20 → y = (-4/5)x + 4.

Profit: 3x - 4y = 12 → y = (3/4)x - 3."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[2]P.E. 2 (Physics Applications){note:"Practical Application: Converting physics equations to slope-intercept form.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (output)

Scenario: Force: 3x + 2y = 6 → y = (-2/3)x + 2.

Velocity: 4t - 2v = 10 → v = 2t - 5.

Energy: 5E - 3P = 15 → P = (5/3)E - 5."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[2]lvl5[1]lvl6[3]lvl7[3]P.E. 3 (Engineering Applications){note:"Practical Application: Converting engineering equations to slope-intercept form.

Variables:
- x = the input (input)
- y = the output (output)

Scenario: Voltage: 2V - 3I = 6 → V = 1.5I + 3.

Pressure: 4P - 2V = 8 → P = 0.5V + 2.

Stress: 3σ - 4ε = 12 → σ = (4/3)ε + 4."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[1]lvl3[5]lvl4[3]Point-Slope Form{note:"The point-slope form is y - y₁ = m(x - x₁), where (x₁, y₁) is a point on the line and m is the slope.

Key uses:
- When you know a point and slope
- When you know two points (find slope first)
- Useful for writing equations quickly

Example: Line through (2, 5) with slope 3
y - 5 = 3(x - 2)"}
